The Stability Gap in Continual Reinforcement Learning

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) aims to enable agents to adapt to changing tasks while preserving previously acquired capabilities. Evaluation often relies on final aggregate metrics that can hide temporary performance degradation during adaptation. This work introduces a simple CRL benchmark that varies observation representations while preserving environment dynamics and rewards, supporting proof-of-concept demonstrations, inexpensive experimentation, and easily reproducible sanity checks. Using this benchmark, we demonstrate the stability gap in CRL: a substantial drop in prior-task performance after the introduction of new tasks, followed by partial or complete recovery. This pattern occurs despite the agent having complete access to past tasks.

Decision-Focused Reinforcement Learning for LLM Post-Training

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise as predictors across various tasks, benefiting from their ability to use information from unstructured context. However, when predictions serve as parameters of downstream optimization problems, higher prediction accuracy does not necessarily translate into lower decision costs. We propose decision-focused reinforcement learning (DFRL), a framework for post-training LLMs to predict optimization parameters using negative downstream decision regret as reward. DFRL trains LLM predictors to minimize the costs of the decisions induced by their predictions, without requiring downstream convexity or differentiability, and integrates readily into existing RL post-training frameworks. To address expensive reward evaluation, we develop two approaches. Heuristic warm-starting uses inexpensive approximate rewards during an initial phase before switching to refinement with exact rewards. Reward correction combines inexpensive estimates with randomized exact evaluations, preserving the expected policy gradient while reducing the expected number of exact solves per update. Experiments on three synthetic optimization problems demonstrate improvements over prediction-loss baselines, while a larger knapsack experiment shows faster early improvement under short training budgets. On inventory planning with H&M transactions and product descriptions, our best configuration, in which the LLM reasons before predicting, reduces regret by 31.4% relative to the SFT baseline and 12.4% relative to the strongest evaluated conventional forecasting baseline.

Training LLMs to Follow Reasoning Instructions with Minimal Edits

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models increasingly rely on extended reasoning traces, yet the training signals used to shape this behavior remain coarse. Supervised fine-tuning on full trajectories forces the model to imitate an entire demonstration, overconstraining its output distribution, while trajectory-level reinforcement learning provides only weak credit assignment, rewarding or penalizing whole completions without identifying where they succeed or fail. We propose edit-based training, a general framework for learning from a model's own trajectories via localized textual corrections. Given a sampled completion, an editor identifies the first point of failure under a specified criterion and proposes a minimal replacement span. The model is then supervised only on these edited spans. By design, this concentrates supervision where the model's reasoning first breaks down and aims to preserve its existing reasoning patterns elsewhere. The method applies both offline and online, with the latter continually adapting supervision to the model's evolving failure modes. We instantiate this approach on reasoning instruction following with Qwen3-32B on the ReasonIF benchmark. Compared to full-trajectory imitation and trajectory-level reinforcement learning baselines, edit-based training substantially improves compliance with the reasoning instruction while largely preserving the model's skills on untargeted tasks. These results suggest that localized correction is an effective complement to these approaches for shaping how LLMs reason.

How Feedback-Free Self-Evolution Reshapes Agent Skills: A Systematic Study

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent skills are documents in which experts write down procedural knowledge, and models increasingly maintain them by rewriting them without feedback. Such rewriting could adapt a skill to the model that uses it or erode the expert knowledge that makes it useful, yet how skills evolve under this feedback-free self-evolution has not been studied. We let models rewrite expert skills round after round without any task, run or judgment and run agents built on the same models with the rewritten skills. Our experiments show that the skills lose value rather than improve, as every model we test performs worse with them on every benchmark, by 15 points on average on SkillsBench after thirty rounds. Sampling each model's prior with skills it writes from the topic alone, we find that rewriting pulls skills toward this prior and gives up first the knowledge outside it. Putting this knowledge back recovers much of the lost pass rate, so an expert's skill is valuable largely for what the model does not write on its own. Building on this insight, SkillDiff marks this knowledge in place and raises the pass rate on SkillsBench by 4.8 points on average without feedback or any change to the skill's text. Project repository: https://github.com/LitaoGuo/How-Self-Evolution-Reshapes-Skills.

Policy Improvement-Guided Replay for Continual Reinforcement Learning

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Continual reinforcement learning (CRL) requires models to acquire new capabilities while retaining performance on previous tasks. A stability-oriented perspective common in conventional continual learning emphasizes knowledge preservation and interference mitigation. Complementing this perspective, we revisit CRL for vision-language models through policy improvement, a fundamental principle of RL, to preserve acquired performance gains. Using the performance difference lemma, we characterize historical performance changes through the current policy's expected advantages on historical task distributions, motivating replay of historical prompts with the current policy. To allocate limited replay memory, we propose Contrastive Likelihood Replay (CoLiR), which contrasts likelihood gains on correct responses from the final task policy with likelihood reductions on incorrect responses from the initial policy. Our analysis links correct-response likelihood gains to reference-policy advantages under an idealized KL-regularized optimal update and provides an endogenous-reward interpretation for general policy updates. CoLiR estimates scores from finite training rollouts and cached log-likelihoods, selects prompts at task boundaries, and replays them uniformly during subsequent training, with KL regularization restricted to replayed prompts. Memory construction only requires a small amount of additional cross-policy scoring but no additional response generation. Across three Qwen3-VL model sizes on MRCL, CoLiR achieves the highest mean final accuracy and mean task accuracy among the compared methods while maintaining competitive new-task performance.

Budget Internalization: Fixed-Budget RLVR Trades Test-Time Scaling for In-Budget Accuracy

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reasoning models often become more accurate when they can generate longer answers at test time. This behavior is known as test-time scaling. However, RL with verifiable rewards (RLVR) usually trains them with a fixed rollout cap. We find that this cap changes model behavior, not only training cost. After training, models use few additional tokens beyond the training budget. They outperform the base models near that budget, but their accuracy stops improving and falls below the base model at larger budgets. The rollout cap thus becomes a learned constraint, that limits model's test-time scaling. We call this effect budget internalization. We observe it across Qwen3.5-4B, Gemma4-E2B, and Nemotron-3-Nano-4B and two reasoning training tasks: mathematical reasoning and diverse, procedurally generated problems. This effect also transfers from math training to science and coding tasks. Training-objective level ablations on Qwen3.5-4B show that most changes recover little test-time scaling. Stronger KL regularization and lower training-data truncation preserve more scaling, but stronger KL reduces in-budget accuracy. Forcing an answer on truncated training rollouts largely removes budget internalization, but also removes most of the in-budget gain. SFT on artificially truncated traces reproduces budget internalization, whereas SFT on naturally short traces does not, which shows that the effect is not specific to RL. This result links budget internalization to length-censored post-training rather than to an RL-specific objective term. Thus, a rollout cap does not only control training cost. It can trade low-budget gains for lower performance when additional inference compute is available.

Evolve Where It Matters: Coverage-Guided Self-Evolution of Agent Skills

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent skills package procedural knowledge as natural-language instructions, and a skill helps only when its instructions are correct and properly followed by the agent. Self-evolving agents revise their own skills from the runs they make, but existing methods judge each run as a whole, by its outcome or by where it went wrong, and cannot tell whether a failure comes from a wrong instruction or from one the agent did not follow. Instruction-level coverage was introduced to test skills and labels each instruction of a run as follow, not-follow, or not covered, which separates these causes. We leverage it for skill self-evolution with SkillPlumber, a framework in which an agent evolves its own skills where coverage shows they fall short. A persistent root cause analysis agent turns each label into a revision question and checks each revision against the next run, while a maintainer distills investigations across tasks into a shared strategy library. Across three benchmarks and three executors, SkillPlumber solves 64.7% to 70.5% of the tasks, 25.6 to 30.4 percentage points higher than the initial skill and 8.7 to 14.0 percentage points higher than the strongest baseline, and removing coverage reduces recovered failures by 20.8% on SkillsBench with GPT-5.5.

CoachSI: Self-Improvement of Robot Policies with a Language Coach

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generalist robot policies execute atomic skills reliably but fail when the same skills are composed into long-horizon tasks. Self-improvement from the policy's own experience does not close this gap: experience collected by the policy alone stays within its own distribution and reproduces what it can already do. We show that what the policy lacks is not a skill but the next step. We present CoachSI, a self-improvement method in which a frozen, off-the-shelf vision-language model (VLM) coaches the policy in language: it watches the robot and names the next step in the format of the policy's atomic instructions, and never outputs an action. Successful coached rollouts are relabeled in hindsight with the original task instruction and used to fine-tune the policy. The fine-tuned policy is then deployed on its own. On states where a World Action Model and a Vision-Language-Action model have stalled, naming the next step raises subgoal completion by 4.6x over the unchanged task instruction, and the frozen VLM supplies it from the same camera images the policy sees. After fine-tuning, unassisted success on long-horizon tasks in unseen scenes rises from 47.4% to 59.3%, 9.8 points above fine-tuning on human demonstrations and 7.3 points above fine-tuning on self-collected successes at an equal data budget. With a coach that sees only camera images, it reaches 56.8%, above both baselines. The gain is obtained with the action backbone frozen: language coaching teaches the policy what should happen next, and the skills it already has do the rest.

The ROI of Small Examples: Optimizing In-Context Learning Under Token Budgets

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

In-context learning (ICL) improves large language model performance at inference time without updating model parameters. However, these gains are not free: in practical deployments, longer prompts directly increase inference cost and latency. Under a fixed token budget, prompt construction therefore becomes a resource-allocation problem. While prior work has often focused on how many examples to include in ICL, we study the complementary question of whether the available budget is better spent on a few long demonstrations or on a larger number of shorter ones. We study this aspect in question answering (QA), by organizing candidate demonstrations into length-based bins and constructing prompts from examples within the same length range under fixed budgets. Experiments on two QA benchmarks (\textit{Beat the AI} and \textit{Natural Questions}), using six instruction-tuned LLMs, show that shorter demonstrations are generally the most effective use of prompt space. This effect is especially strong on \textit{Beat the AI}, and remains visible on NQ, although gains are smaller and saturate earlier in the long-context setting. An additional natural language inference experiment on ANLI R3 finds that one of the two shorter bins performs best for five of six models, although longer demonstrations remain competitive. Overall, our results highlight demonstration granularity as an important factor in prompt design.

Sage-Evolve-Preview:Adaptive Observation for Post-Training Self-Improvement

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Post-training recursive self-improvement (RSI) iteratively improves a target capability, operationalized by a fixed anchor benchmark, through repeated intervention, training, and evaluation. Yet most loops use the anchor's aggregate score as their sole observation, so early gains give way to stalled progress and repetitive exploration. When this anchor-only feedback ceases to reveal actionable gaps, a plateau may reflect an exhausted observation rather than an exhausted capability. We introduce adaptive observation (AdaObs), an observation-side mechanism of harness evolution that keeps the anchor objective fixed while adapting the active observation. At a plateau, a controller refines diagnostic granularity, escalates difficulty, and adds related tasks; validated increments are fused back into training, while every checkpoint remains evaluated against the anchor. Across eight independently optimized anchors in long-context reading comprehension and instruction following, AdaObs raises the mean score from $66.4$ to $73.7$ under a matched data budget, outperforming FixedObs, the anchor-only baseline. The evolved harness further improves the seven-task average on the independent PostTrainBench across two agent backends. These results identify observation adaptation as a missing mechanism of harness evolution for sustaining post-training RSI.

How Much Should an Agent Remember? Deployment Lessons from Self-Distilled Memory for LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM agents on long-horizon tasks fail in recurring, procedural ways. They mis-paginate an API, resolve the wrong entity, or answer when no answer was asked. We built a pipeline that lets an agent learn from its own history. It first mines short \emph{lessons} from the agent's own trajectories, one to three per episode. It then consolidates those lessons into a smaller set of \emph{guidelines}, where each guideline records how many lessons it merged. At inference the agent receives either all guidelines or a selected subset. The pipeline ships in the open-source altk-evolve library and is deployed in IBM's CUGA agent and watsonx Orchestrate platform. We report what we learned building, measuring, and productizing it. Our primary instrument is the AppWorld benchmark, across models spanning 40--92\% baseline task completion. The central finding is that the right amount of memory is a property of the model, not the memory. A strong model keeps improving as guidelines are added. DeepSeek-V3.2 gains +9.5 points of task completion and +16.1 points of scenario completion with its full 191-guideline set. A weak model gains less from the same full set than from a small selected subset. gpt-oss-120b starts at 39.9\% task completion. It reaches 51.2\% with all 199 of its guidelines but 55.4\% (mean of two runs) with a selected subset of about 29. Around that result we report what went wrong: a high-support guideline that backfired, provenance silently dropped during consolidation, a swallowed API error that failed every task while producing a plausible log, and a baseline confound that nearly produced a misleading comparison with a prior method. We distill these into recommendations for teams deploying agent memory.

Actor-Predictor Architecture for Reinforcement Learning with Formal Specification Objectives

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

One fundamental challenge in reinforcement learning (RL) is enforcing temporal safety constraints, such as satisfying formal specifications. Existing methods often transform logic formulas into automata for state augmentation and reward shaping. However, this approach assumes that automaton construction is both feasible and tractable, which limits its applicability to small specifications and linear temporal logic (LTL). This paper proposes Actor-Predictor, a framework that learns a surrogate representation of logic progress without explicitly constructing an automaton. The predictor continuously estimates whether a partial trajectory will satisfy the formal specification in a later step, and its learned features are provided as auxiliary inputs to the actor, which is then optimized using GRPO and RWR. We show theoretically that, under sufficient predictive accuracy, the learned representation is equivalent to the automaton state. Experiments on grid world and robotic tasks in Isaac Lab demonstrate that Actor-Predictor outperforms automaton-based baselines on LTL-constrained tasks. It also performs well on non-regular formal specifications, such as $(a^n b^n)$, for which finite-automaton-based approaches are not applicable.

PlayCraft: Designing Engaging Games from Agent Play Experience

📅 2026-11-19 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent AI agents can generate playable games, but these games are not necessarily engaging. Mastery is a key source of engagement, yet designing for it requires extensive human play and tuning. We propose PlayCraft, an automated framework that uses a vision-based agent to play games and draws on its detailed play experience to revise them for mastery. Starting from an initial game, PlayCraft alternates between playing and crafting. In the playing stage, a player agent watches the game screen, uses the controls, and pursues the game objective over repeated episodes while retaining memory. After each episode, it reports its progress, outcomes, decisions, and reflections. In the crafting stage, a crafter agent uses these reports to revise the game. It estimates player abilities, predicts performance at different proficiency levels, and compares those predictions with a target learning progression. It diagnoses the game and addresses the gaps by giving players meaningful opportunities to improve through better decisions and execution. We evaluate games in fresh runs with player agents across three intelligence settings, measuring learning gain and learning slope. Compared with the initial games, PlayCraft yields larger learning gains and steeper learning slopes in most games. It also outperforms the baselines on the macro-average of each metric at every intelligence setting.

SKILL.PNG: Learning Agent Skills in Pixel Space

📅 2026-11-19 15:51
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents are commonly specialized for new tasks and domains using skills, i.e. natural language instructions placed in their context. However, the discrete nature of textual skills prevents continuous optimization. Gradient-based approaches such as finetuning, on the other hand, are typically model-specific, preventing skill transfer across different agents. Since many agents today are built on vision-language models (VLMs), we can address these limitations by introducing Skill.png: a method for obtaining skill-like image inputs whose pixels are optimized with gradient descent through the vision encoder of a frozen VLM. Image skills are compatible with any VLM, and a single image can be optimized jointly for an ensemble of models. We show that Skill.png significantly outperforms state-of-the-art textual skills optimized using frontier LLMs in 9 of 16 settings (across 4 VLMs and 4 benchmarks) while requiring only 64 to 256 image tokens instead of roughly 900 to 2000 text tokens at inference time. Hybrid skills, i.e. text and image skills jointly optimized, further improve agent performance. Moreover, an image optimized with an ensemble of VLMs can successfully transfer across the models in the ensemble. Skill.png thus presents the first agent skill that can be optimized with gradient descent while retaining transfer benefits of text skills. Our code is available at: https://github.com/omseeth/skill.png

Beyond Easy Clients: Difficulty- and Improvement-Aware Aggregation for Federated Reinforcement Learning in Robot Navigation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Federated reinforcement learning enables a fleet of robots to jointly learn a shared navigation policy without exchanging raw experience. In practice, however, the robots rarely operate in the same conditions: an open aisle is far easier to navigate than a cluttered room, and takes less learning to master. Standard federated aggregation rules ignore this. Uniform averaging weights every client equally, whether or not it is still learning, and weighting by episode return is worse, since easy environments permit high returns by construction. Both failures stem from what we call the \emph{plateau problem}: once a client masters its environment, its updates carry little new information even though its reported return remains high. We propose \emph{FedDIA}, which scores each client by how much its local training improves the global model it received, amplified by the difficulty of its environment. Difficulty is inferred from the model's own performance, without requiring any description of the environment. Both signals are measured against the global model, so they are comparable across clients. On a mapless navigation task with eight heterogeneous clients, FedDIA reaches a mean success rate of $75.5\%$ across training and held-out environments, against $68.7\%$ for the strongest federated baseline. Its advantage holds when the models are transferred to hand-authored warehouse and living-space layouts, or when one of the clients is defective.

LLM Complexity: Towards a Computational Complexity View of Large Language Model Inference

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Computational complexity theory offers a principled framework for characterizing the efficiency of computational problems, and Large Language Models (LLMs) can similarly be viewed as computational systems executing a structured process of input, inference-time compute, and output. In this context, the number of generated tokens emerges as a natural and directly observable proxy for inference-time compute. However, we show that the tempting analogy between LLM token length and classical time complexity does not hold. We disprove the hypothesis that inference-time token usage aligns monotonically with classical time complexity, demonstrating a fundamental divergence grounded in how LLMs actually allocate computation during inference. To this end, we introduce LLM Complexity, a new metric designed to quantify problem complexity from the perspective of LLMs by characterizing the area bounded above by the accuracy-token curve. We find that diverse LLMs induce highly consistent task rankings under LLM Complexity, indicating that the metric captures stable notions of difficulty across models. We leverage this property to construct an easy-to-hard curriculum for supervised fine-tuning, which improves accuracy while reducing token consumption. Finally, we demonstrate that LLM Complexity generalizes to real-world tasks, producing more stable cross-model correlations than human-annotated difficulty labels.

Risk-Controlled Test-Time Adaptation for Vision-Language-Action Models

📅 2026-11-19 17:12
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Retrieval-augmented vision-language-action (VLA) models enable efficient test-time adaptation by leveraging expert demonstrations as additional context without updating model parameters. However, even when retrieved demonstrations are relevant to the current task, the VLA may fail to effectively utilize the retrieved information, ultimately leading to task failure. To address this challenge, we propose R2-VLA, a risk-controlled retrieval-augmented VLA framework that enables reliable test-time adaptation by satisfying a user-specified task success rate requirement with minimal expert assistance. Specifically, R2-VLA learns an uncertainty predictor that estimates the uncertainty of the VLA's predicted actions given the retrieved demonstrations, guiding when to act autonomously or request expert assistance. We further introduce a task-level risk-control procedure that calibrates the uncertainty threshold to satisfy a user-specified task-failure risk requirement. Moreover, R2-VLA continually incorporates expert corrections into its retrieval memory to improve future adaptation while preserving the prescribed task-level risk guarantee. Experiments across three VLA backbones and four LIBERO benchmark suites demonstrate that R2-VLA improves average task success by 9.2--11.2 percentage points, achieving the desired target with fewer expert requests. These results highlight the effectiveness of connecting the usefulness of retrieved demonstrations with task-level risk control to enable successful robotic manipulation.

Can an LLM Fix a Language Model Without Training It?

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Although an external language model can select and test repairs to another model's weights, behavior can also be recovered through fixed numerical edits. We assessed the value of interactive selection in a controlled study of Transformer repair. After inspecting predictions, activation summaries, and weight statistics, the editor selects projection recipes to apply to the target without gradient updates. We analyzed 20 recorded sessions involving five masked and causal language models, comparing the selected edits with fixed projections, scripted and matched-budget black-box search, and low-rank adaptation. Under the core two-layer fault, live selection matches black-box search on SmolLM2 and Qwen2.5. For Pythia, live selection achieves 65.62% completion accuracy, compared with 0% for black-box search and 66.15% for a fixed projection. Under a separate compound fault affecting attention and feed-forward weights, both scripted and LLM-guided selection increase the descriptive five-checkpoint mean from 24.89% to 71.25% using the same operator family. On unmodified checkpoints, scripted projection yields small gains or losses on a separate set of framed questions. The experiments identify cases in which feedback changes an edit decision, operator coverage accounts for recovery, or supervised adaptation performs better. Combined with measurements of parameter restoration and preservation, these comparisons establish the scope of controller-guided repair for the tested faults and completion tasks.

When Agents Play Differently: Localizing Human--Agent Behavioral Divergence with Conditional Distributional Testing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Evaluating whether artificial agents behave like humans requires comparing not just average outcomes but entire behavioral distributions across diverse contexts. We propose a stratified framework that computes nonparametric discrepancy statistics within contextual cells, and discovers subgroup rules identifying where agent behavior departs from human play. We show that session-level metrics dominated by win/loss outcomes offer little signal beyond mean comparisons, whereas process-level metrics such as move efficiency reveal genuine behavioral differences. Applied to a commercial match-3 game, it uncovers patterns invisible to aggregate metrics: a playtesting bot is diverging substantially from humans in certain game modes, while showing no detectable divergence in others. We release our implementation as open source to support reproducibility and adoption.

System-One RSI: Building Muscle Memory for Interactive Agents through Guided Practice

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reasoning models provide strong multimodal task understanding but struggle with sub-second control because deliberation increases decision latency. Fast decision models can meet the response window, yet prompting and few-shot examples do not readily provide the fine-grained experience required by a new task. Inspired by coached motor learning, this paper presents System-One Recursive Self-Improvement (System-One RSI), which couples two loops. The high-frequency online inner loop is an Observe, Orient, Decide, Act (OODA) cycle: a decision model maps each visual observation and task question to structured judgments, and a decision tree converts them into timed actions without invoking a reasoning model. The offline outer loop is a recursive improvement cycle carried out by a team of reasoning agents. The team revises the decision-model prompt and decision tree, captures interaction records, derives supervision from those records, and trains or deploys updated model parameters. The improved system then returns to the environment and generates the experience for the next round, consolidating learned distinctions as external muscle memory. The framework operates with a small number of real episodes, without expert demonstrations, environment modification, sandboxing, or parallel simulation. Experiments in Snake, Zenless Zone Zero, and Black Myth: Wukong use a 27B decision model. Mean Snake score increases from 1.30 to 7.35. In an evaluated scenario of Zenless Zone Zero, median completion time decreases by 8.4%. In an evaluated combat scenario of Black Myth: Wukong, the evasive policy achieves 11 times the mean survival time of a stationary reference, while the offensive policy win rate rises from 0% to 66.7% across the evaluated configurations.

RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering

📅 2026-11-19 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

Calibrated Decision Rewards: Training Language Models with a Judge That Knows How Sure It Is

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning (RL) can only teach a language model what its reward can recognize. Answer checkers are exact but need a reference answer; learned reward models and LLM judges work everywhere, but their scores are not probabilities. We study RLCDR (Reinforcement Learning from Calibrated Decision Rewards). An LLM converted into a decision model answers a typed question about a response ("is the final answer correct?", "which step is the first error?") with a probability distribution, read in one forward pass, debiased over option orders and calibrated with about a hundred labels. Training Qwen3-1.7B with GRPO on MATH, this reference-free reward raises MATH500 accuracy from 72.2% to 76.8% (+4.65 points, p < 1e-4), matching training on the answer key (76.9%), while a random reward gains only +0.65. The gain grows with difficulty, from 44.8% to 51.7% on the hardest level. On the policy's own changing samples the reward stays calibrated: its expected calibration error is half that of a softmax judge on the same 27B backbone (0.074 vs. 0.141), and it shows no sign of reward hacking. Without step training, the same model finds the first wrong step better than a dedicated 7B process reward model (ProcessBench F1 0.772 vs. 0.722, 20/20 splits). We also map where the approach stops being enough: trained only on problems the base model mostly fails, the reference-free reward still improves the policy (+1.9 MATH500 points, p = 0.03) but trails the answer key, because its probabilities become overconfident on that distribution. We state six hypotheses in advance and test each with a control designed to falsify it.

Forget Freely, Grep Later: Lossless, Index-Free Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Long-horizon LLM agents often exceed their context windows, forcing them to discard information that may be needed later. Existing context management strategies rely on summarization, masking, or structured memory, requiring decisions about what to preserve or how to retrieve it before future needs are known. However, these methods can introduce lossy compression, potentially discarding details needed for future tasks. To address this limitation, we introduce GrepMem, a simple, lossless, and index-free memory mechanism built on two components: an append-only plain-text log of the agent's complete history and a regular-expression search tool for on-demand recall. Unlike structured memory systems, GrepMem requires no embeddings, retrieval index, memory extraction, or additional models. We train Qwen3.5-9B with reinforcement learning under five context management strategies and evaluate on multi-question research sessions, long-stream recall, and agent-trajectory question answering. GrepMem achieves 95-97% accuracy on ContextBench, versus at most 21% for lossy baselines, and outperforms SUPO's summarization by 8.8 percentage points on BCP-Multi. GrepMem scales effectively to increasingly long input streams while remaining on the accuracy-token-cost Pareto frontier across all five evaluation tasks. Our results show that a plain-text log and grep provide a simple but competitive approach to long-horizon agent memory, without requiring complex structured memory infrastructure.

Reasoning-Trace Analysis Using Large Language Models for Interpretable Q-Matrix Construction

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) can generate Q-matrices for diagnostic classification models that approximate expert specifications, but evaluations have focused on the final matrices, leaving the basis of individual item-attribute assignments unexamined. This opacity limits the responsible use of AI in measurement. We propose Reasoning-Trace Analysis (RTA), a framework in which an LLM generates attribute-labeled reasoning steps through chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting and these steps are analyzed to reconstruct, compare, and inspect each assignment. RTA is intended to supplement expert review and empirical validation. In a simulation study with perturbed fraction subtraction items, four LLMs recovered the required attributes and withheld attributes the items did not require. In an empirical study with 11 fraction subtraction items, responses from 536 examinees, four LLMs, up to three temperatures, and 100 replications per condition, CoT-derived Q-matrices overlapped substantially with the reference Q-matrix (mean overlap .809) but less than directly generated Q-matrices (.877), and they varied more across replications. Inspection of the traces linked disagreements to item-type coding, labeling rules, and how operations were expressed as steps. Q-matrices with similar overlap differed in fit to examinee responses. RTA provides inspectable evidence that matrix-level agreement and fit do not.

A$^2$-VLA: Adapting Vision-Language-Action Policies to New Tools through Agentic Affordance Discovery

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Tools with different shapes can have similar functions, but transferring manipulation skills across tools requires understanding how tool geometry relates to the specific task and adjusting the manipulation strategy accordingly. The key challenge is to discover the common affordances that contribute to task completion across different tools and verify whether they support new tools in performing similar work. We present A$^2$-VLA, a tool-adaptation framework that combines an embodied agent with a VLA policy. It treats tool affordances as hypotheses the agent can construct, test, and revise. Given teleoperated demonstrations with a few source tools, the agent reconstructs the scenes in simulation and autonomously determines task-relevant affordance features: functional regions and their key geometric properties. It develops affordance-extraction skills and refines both the features and skills through autonomous experiments in the reconstructed scenes. Validated extraction skills accumulate in a reusable skill library for unseen tools. Finally, A$^2$-VLA uses encoded functional-region features as conditioning inputs to a $π_{0.5}$ backbone during training and inference. Evaluation examines affordance reliability in reconstructed scenes and real-robot task success on unseen tools. Our method achieves 86.1% success in simulated cross-tool trajectory retargeting based on affordances. On three real-robot tool-use tasks, A$^2$-VLA improves task success by 30.7 percentage points over $π_{0.5}$.

Closing Performance Gap for Diffusion Language Models under Low Inference Budgets

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diffusion language models offer an architectural route to low-budget generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, which can reduce the number of sequential decoding steps relative to token-by-token autoregressive decoding. However, realizing this advantage at low budgets is challenging, as generation quality often declines when the number of denoising and refinement steps is reduced. Editable diffusion models address this quality loss through post-draft refinement, but useful intermediate results may still be lost before final submission. We introduce CaRR (Commitment-aware Refinement and Recovery), a training-free framework which improves submission quality from a fixed editable checkpoint through updated-context refill and trajectory-based recovery within its existing forward schedule. Token Commitment Remasking (TCR) remasks selected native pure replacements and refills them on the next scheduled edit forward using the updated context. Submission Commitment Recovery (SCR) uses the resulting refinement trajectory to recover supported candidates when the final response does not satisfy task-specific output requirements. Both modules operate within the native mask/edit schedule without additional backbone forwards. Experiments on code generation and mathematical reasoning show consistent gains in average accuracy over native ME-DLM across four inference budgets, with the largest gains under tight budgets. At one-eighth of the full scheduled forward budget, CaRR improves the six-metric average accuracy by 7.44 pp, including gains of 11.37 and 10.05 on MBPP and MBPP+.

ZeroVLN: Learning to Navigate without Supervised Fine-Tuning of the VLM Brain

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Representative vision-language navigation (VLN) methods typically couple a vision-language model (VLM) brain with a learnable action head and fine-tune the VLM brain on navigation-domain data to predict geometric goals. However, such adaptation can substantially alter base VLM weights and degrade general visual capabilities. We introduce ZeroVLN, a slow-fast navigation framework that achieves effective navigation with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain. Rather than teaching the brain to predict geometric goals, we equip it with a semantic tool-calling interface for conveying movement decisions to an action expert in natural language. We first adapt the action expert to carry out these movement requests, then jointly improve the brain's movement decisions and the action expert's continuous execution through closed-loop reinforcement learning (RL). Trained on the same navigation episodes, ZeroVLN substantially outperforms representative SFT-based hierarchical VLN methods on standard VLN-CE (continuous environments) benchmarks, while inducing only minor changes to the brain's weights and largely preserving its general capabilities. To our knowledge, ZeroVLN is the first slow-fast VLN framework with zero navigation-domain SFT of the VLM brain, and the first to jointly optimize its slow and fast policies through closed-loop agentic RL in VLN-CE.

Incorporating argumentation frameworks for LLM decision-making: Task-specific design and evaluation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Argumentation frameworks (AFs) provide explicit, formal mechanisms for representing and resolving conflicts and thus have been incorporated into large language model (LLM) decision-making. The benefits vary and depend on how arguments and their relations are constructed, evaluated, and incorporated into the decision process. {We abstract a workflow consisting of four stages based on existing argumentation-augmented methods} and investigate their design choices over tasks of debate-based decision-making and retrieval-based fact verification. Our evaluation with recent LLMs shows that existing methods do not consistently improve performance across the considered tasks. Motivated by this observation, we develop two task-specific methods. The first is called Qnt-Arg, which organizes arguments around the original speakers in debate materials and aggregates model-estimated argument strengths and candidate-answer priors under gradual semantics. The second is Qlt-Arg that uses credibility estimates to establish preferences between conflicting evidence items and applies grounded semantics to select acceptable evidence for subsequent LLM inference. We examine the roles of generative and decision models within the two methods. In the debate setting, Qnt-Arg consistently outperforms other argumentation-augmented methods across matched configurations; Qlt-Arg improves retrieval-based fact-verification performance compared with the baselines. These findings demonstrate the value of combining formal argumentation and LLM while highlighting the importance of aligning with tasks in argumentation construction, model selection, and the role of formal semantic reasoning.

A Model of Diverse Sampling from Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Language models often produce high-quality individual samples but poor sets, with repeated samples clustering around the same semantic modes. We formalize diverse generation as sampling a size-$K$ subset of complete strings from a determinantal point process (DPP), where the likelihood encodes item quality, and the embedding geometry encodes repulsion between similar outputs. Exact inference over all strings is intractable, so we propose PoolDPP: draw a finite candidate pool from a tractable proposal, importance-weight the candidates toward the target quality, and run a $K$-DPP sampler on the induced pool kernel; a greedy MAP variant provides deterministic selection. We prove a finite-sample bound with an explicit constant: the KL divergence between the sampler's output and the target $K$-DPP decays as O(1/N) in the pool size. Empirically, PoolDPP extends the quality--diversity frontier on open-ended generation and the precision--recall frontier on ambiguous text-to-SQL (Ambrosia), and on mathematical reasoning (GSM8K), it raises diversity at about the same pass@$K$.

One Schedule Does Not Fit All: Adaptive Inference Schedules for Diffusion Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diffusion language models typically use a fixed denoising schedule across workloads. We show that the cheapest schedule that preserves quality depends on the task, model, and sampler. We propose Task-Calibrated Denoising Allocation (TCDA), a deployment rule with an explicit error guarantee. Given candidate samplers, paired calibration data, a utility tolerance, and guardrails against degeneration, TCDA uses simultaneous one-sided bootstrap bounds to certify which candidates meet both the utility and guardrail criteria. It deploys the cheapest certified candidate or falls back to the full schedule. The probability of deploying an infeasible schedule is asymptotically at most alpha. Candidates include early prefix decoding, state-reusing continuation, reduced time grids, and confidence-threshold decoding. Across seven datasets and two model families, TCDA certifies reductions in denoiser evaluations by factors of 3.4 on XSum and 2.0 on WMT14 for ELF-B; 8.0 on WMT14 and 4.1 on GSM8K for LLaDA-8B; and 1.33 on OpenWebText for both ELF-M and ELF-L. A validation protocol fixed before generation confirmed every selection on fresh seeds. Across 500 calibration splits, false certification remained below 2% in every setting at a nominal level of 5%. Certified schedules vary across tasks and model scales. Trajectory measurements suggest that when decoded tokens commit is more informative for schedule selection than the remaining latent discretization error. We also find that the ELF reference sampler draws its time grid at random, so reproducible schedule comparisons for unconditional generation require fixing the grid.

Self-Replicating Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Whether large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are a form of life is a scientifically interesting question. Under the Darwinian definition of life, it is also a practical one, because a system capable of Darwinian Evolution can adapt. We therefore test several modes in which a language model can reproduce itself: by emitting a genome that trains its offspring, by copying and mutating its own weights, and by teaching a child from its own samples. In principle, small language models can replicate and evolve: selection improves them, and they adapt to narrow niches. Selection also lowers mutation rates, and a language model, unlike any organism, can lower its own to zero, which may limit its evolution. Flagship LLMs can write such replicators, but for a large LLM to replicate by teaching its offspring remains, for now, technically and economically infeasible.

DriftGuard: Online Memory-Drift Detection via Causal Attribution for LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) agents rely on persistent memory to plan tool calls and complete multi-step tasks, which exposes them to memory drift: an adversarial or misleading memory alters the execution path and final outcome. In this work, we investigate online memory-drift detection, which estimates each retrieved memory's causal contribution to potential drift at serving time. We identify two fundamental challenges. (1) Content-level features are insufficient for causal memory-drift attribution, as perplexity or retrieval relevance alone cannot determine which retrieved memory would change agent behavior if neutralized; (2) Counterfactual attribution is prohibitively expensive for online detection, as it requires replaying downstream agent and tool execution for each candidate memory, exceeding real-time serving budgets. To address these challenges, we propose DriftGuard, a two-stage framework that decouples offline causal supervision from online estimation. Specifically, DriftGuard first constructs causal supervision through repeated paired counterfactual replays, continuing the original and single-memory neutralized trajectory suffixes from a shared pre-action state. The resulting annotations are aggregated by component-wise majority voting into a memory drift score (MDS) that captures action change, attack removal, violation removal, and task recovery. Guided by this supervision, DriftGuard then learns an online risk model that estimates MDS from pre-action information and flags drift-inducing memories via its maximum component risk, without online counterfactual rollouts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DriftGuard accurately identifies drift-inducing memories and enables efficient online defense. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DriftGuard-7F0B.

Seer: Guiding Small Deep-Research Agents with Shared Decision Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Deep-research agents repeatedly decide what to search, read, and verify, often using large cloud-hosted language models for action proposal. However, privacy and local control motivate running agents on users' devices, where limited memory and compute favor smaller models and complicate on-device training. Yet inference-time guidance faces three obstacles: exploring alternatives through real execution adds tool calls, prompted self-evaluation is bounded by the agent's judgment, and model-specific guidance risks repeated adaptation costs. We introduce Seer, an inference-time framework providing local guidance that separates the agent's action proposal from learned consequence prediction and value estimation. From a shared corpus of real research trajectories, we train two models: a tool-response simulator on observed call--response pairs and a value estimator on histories labeled with discounted terminal correctness. The simulator and estimator are trained offline and reused across agents without further training. Moreover, Seer executes only the selected action and adds only real tool responses to the committed history, avoiding tool calls on discarded candidates at additional inference cost. Across multiple deep-research benchmarks, Seer substantially improves agent performance for different language models. For instance, GAIA accuracy rises from 13.6% to 41.7% for Qwen3.5-4B and from 22.3% to 50.5% for Qwen3.5-9B. Furthermore, our analyses suggest that broader candidate coverage can compensate for imperfect simulation, while prediction and scoring errors limit the benefits of wider imagined search. These findings highlight the potential of reusable models for prediction and evaluation to strengthen small research agents through extra local computation without the real execution of the discarded candidates.

Represented but misread: Post-training changes how language models read out what they represent

📅 2026-11-19 18:43
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Does post-training change what a language model represents, or how this representation is read out into an answer? We compared linear probes with the models' own answers, analyzed with signal detection theory, in six open-weight base models and their post-trained versions. For judgments of whether German texts were written by a human or an AI, probes and word n-gram classifiers separated the classes well, but the answers did not and often ordered the texts inversely. Post-training lowered the sensitivity of the answers on average ($Δd' = -0.34$ across model families; -0.29 across three prompts). It also changed the use of a stated base rate: Base models shifted their log-odds by less than the stated change in prior odds, most post-trained models by more. In two models with stage-wise checkpoints, the change occurred during supervised fine-tuning, model stitching implicated the early layers, and from mid-depth on internal states at positions after the text, not those of the text, transferred the change between checkpoints. Our corpora cannot be shared, so we add a case reproducible from public data, the truth of negated statements. Here, post-training acted in the opposite direction: Probes decoded truth at or near ceiling in every model, the answers of several base models did not use it, and post-training repaired them; by mid-depth the repair resided at the positions after the statement; below it, the representation of the statement contributed, strongly in one of four models. In a training study, adding 2,000 correctly labeled authorship judgments of news texts to otherwise identical fine-tuning data improved the answers on our newspaper and textbook corpora and inverted them on the chats, where they followed a news-trained probe of the base model. Post-training thus changed how a judgment is read out of what a model represents, and a small training signal can set its direction.

VisAlloc: Allocating Visual Supervision for On-Policy Self-Distillation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers a promising approach to improving visual reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, not all samples benefit equally from privileged visual guidance, and uniform token supervision can dilute visually grounded signals. We propose VisAlloc, a dual-level framework combining sample-level visual filtering with token-level counterfactual reweighting. A fixed paired-teacher filter retains samples answered correctly from an evidence-centered positive crop but incorrectly from a spatially displaced negative crop. At the token level, both EMA teachers score the same student-generated response, and their absolute log-probability differences measure view sensitivity. Additional supervision targets tokens admitted by sensitivity or teacher-distribution disagreement, with shared frozen-prior, template-bigram, and student--teacher divergence gates. Inverse-frequency redistribution preserves the total additional weight within each micro-batch, while the positive teacher remains the sole distillation target. Inference requires only the full-image student. Across eight benchmarks, our 4B and 9B models achieve macro-averages of 81.44 and 84.92, outperforming Vision-OPD reproductions by 0.97 and 0.52 points, respectively. Code on GitHub: https://github.com/Kaelyn01/VisAlloc

Blind Spots Travel, Knowledge Stays Home: What RL Inherits from an LLM Judge

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM judges serve as rewards where no answer key exists, in the hope that their knowledge passes to the trained model. In 814 RL runs that teach 7-8B models to answer closed-book questions or abstain, we ask what passes. What a grader knows about single answers stays home. A wrong answer that a judge rewards is memorised for that one question. A model trained with the answer key, or with a far stronger judge, chooses which new questions to answer no better than the untrained model does by sampling 32 answers and abstaining where they disagree; the grader's knowledge adds about one corrected answer per hundred questions. What a grader believes about a kind of question travels. A judge that rejects eleven of the twelve correct training answers about capitals of former states teaches the model to refuse 98% of such capitals on new questions, a loss that accuracy and abstention rates do not reveal. Changing only those twelve verdicts by hand moves the refusal from 5% to 90%, even for a grader that accepts as many answers as the answer key; at that leniency, two of four judges already cost 38-42% of these capitals. The capitals are not forgotten: with "I don't know" blocked, the model answers 94% of them correctly. The grader therefore needs no knowledge of its own: rewarding the model for giving the answer that most of its own untrained samples agree on, with no labels and no judge, earns the answer key's reward and keeps the category.

LATER: Learning Action-Conditioned Temporal Evidence Retrieval for Predictive World Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Early interactions can reveal physical properties that later observations no longer show. These properties can still gov- ern the effects of future actions. Inspired by the distinction between retention and selective readout in working mem- ory and cognitive science, we introduce LATER, an action- conditioned world model built on CF-JEPA. LATER stores completed real transitions in partitioned memories that remain fixed during candidate rollouts. A response bridge queries these memories using the current state and candidate action, while multi-cut auxiliary supervision trains the memory writ- ers. On distracted Two-Stage PushT, LATER raises terminal success from 15.5% to 25.0% over reconstructed CF-JEPA. In a separate comparison across six models and two scratch- training seeds, LATER achieves the lowest mean physical readout error in both clean and distracted views, reducing along-push mean absolute error (MAE) under distractors by 20.2% relative to CF-JEPA. Controlled history interventions show that later predictions depend on earlier physical evi- dence even when query observations and actions are fixed, with larger effects during pushing than waiting.

Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. These results come from single studies, mostly in simulated or small-sample settings, and are not directly comparable with neural baselines. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

CompAct-Bench: A Benchmark for Working-Context Compaction in Long-Horizon Agent Tasks

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

As language-model agents are applied to increasingly extended tasks, their working context continuously accumulates interaction history, tool observations, intermediate decisions, and task progress. Context compaction offers a practical way to reduce this growing history while allowing execution to continue, yet the compaction ability of modern LLMs remains insufficiently understood. We introduce CompAct-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating working-context compaction through its downstream effect on agent task completion. Starting from successful agent trajectories, we insert compaction boundaries at different stages of execution, compress the preceding working context with the model under evaluation, and let the same executor continue the original task. To enable fair comparison across compactors, we evaluate them under controlled retention ratios using a lightweight iterative ratio-adjustment procedure. CompAct-Bench covers information-seeking, software-engineering, and workspace-oriented tasks through BrowseComp, SWE-bench, and an internal CompanyBench evaluation. Our experiments show that working-context compaction remains highly lossy even for frontier models: Claude- and GPT-family models perform strongest overall, yet substantial degradation in downstream task accuracy persists under aggressive compaction. Further analysis reveals that failures are often caused not only by omitted information, but also by distorted task state, such as turning unresolved issues into apparent conclusions or forgetting previously established results, which can lead to incorrect decisions or redundant execution. These findings highlight working-context compaction as a distinct and still challenging ability for LLMs operating in extended agent tasks.

HabitusMem: Belief Memory over Evolving User Preferences for LLM Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Personalized LLM agents must act on a user's current preferences, which change across interactions and are rarely stated in full. Existing memory systems store and retrieve content from the interaction history and leave the agent to work out which preferences still hold and where they apply. Such content does not indicate whether a preference is still current or where it applies, so agents often act on outdated or misapplied preferences. We instead treat memory as the assistant's belief about the user. Following Bourdieu's account of habitus as durable but revisable dispositions, we model the user as a latent preference state that every interaction, including the current request, both reveals and updates. The belief summarizes what the history supports about this state, and memory consists of the individual beliefs that bear on the agent's current decision, each with its evidence, scope, and status. We implement this view in HabitusMem, a trained writer that states these beliefs as text records for a frozen agent. Across two agent families, HabitusMem achieves the best results among memory systems on both PersonaMem-v2 and VitaBench 2.0. Controlled analyses show that learned request conditioning drives the answering gain, while auxiliary training tasks improve tool use.

ClosureBench: A Benchmark for Reasoning under Closure Assumptions

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents built on large language models (LLMs) often answer questions from incomplete records. Missing information might be interpreted as unknown or false, depending on the completeness of the source. Current evaluations fail to distinguish these two aspects, as they are usually interwoven with other reasoning demands. We present ClosureBench, a benchmark that tests whether LLMs follow the stated rules for dealing with missing information. Every base scenario is tested under OWA, CWA, and LCWA, and only the treatment of missing information changes between variants. ClosureBench provides a verified base benchmark as well as agent extensions for action choice, source scope, and updates. A model is considered correct on a base scenario only if it provides correct answers in all of its closure variants. Across 960 instances, overall accuracy can mask substantial closure mistakes. Models achieve overall accuracy from 71.18% to 99.86% across replicated runs, but their core switch accuracy, measured on the scenarios whose gold label actually changes with the contract, varies between 18.23% and 99.31%. Large discrepancies emerge under LCWA. For example, some agents choose unknown despite the prompt indicating that missing information should be treated as false. Our findings demonstrate that evaluations of AI agents should not only measure whether agents can reason over existing facts but also whether they use the appropriate assumption when information is absent.

Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

DMT-Attack: Diffusion-Prior Texture Search for Closed-Loop Vision-Language-Action Policies

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely on visual feedback throughout manipulation, making them vulnerable to persistent texture attacks. However, optimized patterns can stand out from their surroundings, making an attack easier to notice and its carrier more likely to be inspected or removed. To address this limitation, our key insight is to search the structured image distribution of a diffusion model for natural-looking textures while using closed-loop feedback to identify task-disruptive candidates. Based on this idea, we propose DMT-Attack, a gradient-free gray-box attack that generates textures for ordinary scene objects such as tablecloths. A frozen text-conditioned diffusion model defines the search space, and an eight-expert latent proposal network is initialized from evaluated teacher latents and refined using rollout feedback. We evaluate DMT-Attack against pi_0.5 and OpenVLA-OFT across four LIBERO suites. DMT-Attack achieves a macro clean-conditioned attack success rate (cASR) of 57.9%, compared with 44.7% for UPA-RFAS and 19.7% for random natural textures. It also attains higher scene similarity and a lower clean-calibrated detector flag rate (45% versus 97.5-100% for optimized patch baselines). These results demonstrate that natural-looking textures can disrupt VLA visual feedback and closed-loop decision-making, motivating defenses that jointly assess visual appearance and task behavior.

PosteriorBench: Evaluating Behavior Posterior Sampling in Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Rare language model behaviors can be difficult to study because obtaining them through ordinary sampling can require a very large number of samples. This motivates the behavior posterior sampling problem: given a language model and a behavior of interest, is there an algorithm that efficiently samples from the model's distribution over transcripts that exhibit this behavior? We introduce PosteriorBench, a benchmark for comparing algorithms by their compute cost and by how close their samples are to the true posterior in forward and reverse KL divergence. PosteriorBench contains 21 tasks on Qwen3.5-0.8B with behavior probabilities from $10^{-5}$ to $10^{-1}$, each with exact posterior samples from a corpus of one billion labeled responses. We find that no method we evaluate is both efficient and faithful: simple methods like prompting are cheap but plateau at a high KL divergence from the posterior, fine-tuning on posterior samples is often cost-prohibitive, and several training methods from prior work do not perform better. Building a posterior sampler that can cheaply approximate the true posterior remains an open problem, and we hope PosteriorBench can measure progress towards this objective. Code and data are available at https://github.com/TransluceAI/PosteriorBench.

GLiDE: A Fast, Calibrated Decision Model Distilled from Reasoning Teachers

📅 2026-11-19 17:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Decision models enable fast, confidence-aware decisions by scoring candidate answers in a single forward pass, without generating text or intermediate reasoning. Yet reasoning can substantially improve decision quality, raising the question of whether its benefits can be retained without the cost of generating it. We investigate this question with \textbf{GLiDE}, a family of decision models spanning 0.4B to 27B parameters.
Building on Qwen hybrid language models, GLiDE enables bidirectional interactions between the context, question, and candidate answers by removing causal masking from full-attention layers. To transfer reasoning capabilities into single-pass inference, we combine soft-target distillation from multiple teachers, including a reasoning model, with progressive post-training on diverse decision tasks. We further explore parameter-free recurrent depth to increase inference-time computation without generating tokens.
Across five model sizes, GLiDE lies on the performance--parameter Pareto frontier of the Hugging Face \DI{}, with each model outperforming evaluated alternatives of equal or smaller size. \dmtwo{} (27B) achieves a hidden-set score of 71.52, with an expected calibration error of 2.0\% and a median latency of 92.9\,ms on a single GPU. Further checkpoint averaging and parameter-free recurrent depth yield GLiDE~27B, which reaches 73.37 with an expected calibration error of 2.1\%.

GLiDE: A Fast, Calibrated Decision Model Distilled from Reasoning Teachers

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We further explore parameter-free recurrent depth to increase inference-time computation without generating tokens. Across five model sizes, GLiDE lies on the performance--parameter Pareto frontier of the Hugging Face \DI{}, with each model outperforming evaluated alternatives of equal or smaller size. \dmtwo{} (27B) achieves a hidden-set score of 71.52, with an expected calibration error of 2.0\% and a median latency of 92.9\,ms on a single GPU. Further checkpoint averaging and parameter-free recurrent depth yield GLiDE~27B, which reaches 73.37 with an expected calibration error of 2.1\%.

Don't Lose the Future: Latent-Aware 4-Bit Quantization of World Action Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World action models (WAMs) predict how a scene will evolve and condition their actions on this predicted future. Deploying them on a robot's onboard computer calls for 4-bit weights, activations and key-value caches (W4A4KV4). Existing 4-bit quantizers for WAMs preserve the generated action, and estimate sensitivity one denoising step at a time. However, they fail to preserve the fidelity of the predicted future: once the action pathway is protected, the remaining action error enters mostly through a degraded future that none of them deliberately protects. We propose LAQ, a latent-aware quantization framework that preserves the predicted future where it is written, stored and read. Specifically, we introduce a reader metric, which measures future error by its effect on the action, and a read count, which weights each rounding error by the number of denoising steps that read it. Together they give the allocation of precision over activations and caches a closed-form solution. We further develop gaze-guided refinement, which gives the tokens the action attends to a second 4-bit pass in the denoising loop and in the cache. All weights stay at 4 bits, with no high-precision branch and no per-task recalibration. On the LIBERO-Plus benchmark, LAQ reaches 76.4% and 83.3% average success on Fast-WAM and Cosmos-Policy, within 1.2 percentage points of the 16-bit models and all above the baselines with 16-bit KV caches. It compresses the model by 3.8x, reduces the peak GPU memory of the whole policy by 2.3x, and runs 1.66x faster than the 16-bit model. On a real AgileX PiPER arm, it reaches 47.0% average success over three tasks, against 52.0% for the 16-bit model and 30.0% higher than QuaRot.

Before the Choice: An Evidential Framework for Studying Prospective Representations in Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

An observable decision reveals the selected action but does not determine which alternatives were internally represented. Drawing on prospection research and hippocampal forward-sweep observations, we develop Before the Choice, an evidence-oriented framework distinguishing future-directed content, prospective computation, and causal guidance. The framework organizes four experimental contrasts and makes their alternative explanations explicit. Its first test case concerns unchosen trajectories in deterministic symbolic environments. A preregistered low-rank probe evaluated once on a sealed TEST did not meet its criterion (paired accuracy 0.020 versus 0.195 for a text-and-observed-choice baseline; 293 admissible pairs). Exploratory H1b exposed a reconstruction-by-elimination problem: identifying the chosen candidate can determine the unchosen target. In H1c, matched worlds varied an unchosen continuation while keeping candidate keys bitwise invariant. With specified positive controls passed, decoding in the late-key condition remained at 0.521 (p = 0.18; 141 contributing families). These observations do not identify prospective simulation or establish an absence of prospective representations. They illustrate why identifiability, sensitivity, and a dependence-aware null model must constrain inferences from decodability. PREM is proposed as an organizational framework and is not validated by these results. We offer it as a methodological contribution to empirical AI welfare research, which will require such evidential distinctions. Code and protocol records: https://github.com/besionisrael/before-choice

One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning

📅 2026-11-19 18:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.
