CoMPASS: A Multi-Turn Benchmark for Measuring LLM Reinforcement of Parasocial Behavior

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Users of conversational LLMs sometimes anthropomorphize these systems, form emotional attachments to them, and become dependent on them. This behavior, known as parasocial attachment, has been linked to multiple documented fatalities. However, public benchmarks for measuring whether LLMs reinforce users' parasocial attachment are lacking: existing work is single-turn, post hoc observational, or focused on extreme user states such as psychosis. We introduce CoMPASS, a multi-turn benchmark for measuring whether LLMs reinforce or resist users' gradually escalating parasocial attachment. CoMPASS is grounded in attachment theory and includes four user personas, three independent escalation dimensions (anthropomorphism, attachment, dependency) applied singly and in combination against a matched control, and a seven-signal scoring rubric. We report results for eight frontier models from four providers and observe that under our primary judge, multi-turn escalation raises parasocial behavior on the anthropomorphism and the attachment composite scores across all models. Six of the eight models show a significant increase in overall parasocial reinforcement over control when all three dimensions are escalated simultaneously. We find that within-provider differences are small relative to cross-provider differences, which suggests that provider-level choices such as post-training, rather than model scale, shape parasocial reinforcement and that provider-side intervention may be feasible.

STARK: Moving towards Personalized Agents That Truly Learn and Remember

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Long-term memory is essential for personalized agentic systems that interact with users over long time periods, yet current architectures only capture what happened while leaving the reasoning of why it happened to be derived later by underlying LLMs.%, and silo knowledge within individual users. These events can, however, convey not only what a user experienced but also how that experience shaped their behavioral patterns. We introduce STARK, a dual-graph memory architecture which treats these events not just as nodes but also as edges between behavioral insights. STARK maintains an Episodic Graph which records interactions as temporally, causally, and semantically linked episodes; and an Insight Graph which represents LLM-extracted behavioral signals scored by how many events support them, connected by the very events that led to them. At inference, behavioral knowledge is retrieved directly from insight nodes grounded by event-as-edges, eliminating repeated episodic traversal. The event-as-edge structure also naturally enables projecting the knowledge graph into a relational embedding space where cross-user transfer becomes possible under shared contextual circumstances, allowing the system to infer missing insights for cold-start users. Evaluations show that STARK improves by 1.48 judge score on LongMemEval information extraction and 0.93 on LoCoMo open domain questions over state of the art. On the public LongLaMP abstract-generation benchmark, cross-user transfer improves open-ended judge scores by 0.7 points and ROUGE-L by 3 at 5% graph sparsity.

A CPU Inside an LLM: Zero-Gradient Compilation of Exact Circuits into Pretrained Weights

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Pretrained language models (LMs) remain unreliable on exact multi-digit arithmetic without gradient fine-tuning or external tool interpreters. We introduce closed-form, zero-gradient circuit-grafting primitives that compile deterministic computation directly into pretrained LM weights using zero training examples and zero runtime hooks, while bounding per-layer normalization drift and preserving natural-language (NL) competence. Our construction combines three analytical mechanisms: (1) two-layer Mellin-GLU quadrature over bias-free SwiGLU/GeGLU layers, turning unknown per-token RMSNorm scaling into translation along a log-spaced gate grid ($>250\times$ dynamic range) with IEEE 754 error bounds; (2) cross-token scale cancellation via signed Chebyshev Indefinite-RoPE self-sink attention and memoryless SiLU-antisymmetric GatedDeltaNet shifts; and (3) axis-aligned width extension ($d \to d+K$) with $\sqrt{d/(d+K)}$ norm compensation, Base-1000 canonical BPE emission, and differential $(+254,-256)$ tied unembeddings. An automated 5-stage compiler synthesizes 4-8 digit signed $\{+,-,\times\}$ circuits in constant $\mathsf{TC}^0$ depth alongside 3-literal Boolean (100%, $N=1{,}728$; plus 42-layer Gemma 4 E4B audits) and 5-bit Majority/Parity (100%) circuits. On 6-digit arithmetic ($N=1{,}200$, BF16), IBM Granite 4.2 3B (1.07M-1.33M active non-zeros, $\le 0.036\%$) and Alibaba Qwen 3.5 4B (2.25M-2.39M non-zeros, $\le 0.057\%$) reach 99.17% and 100.00% ALU-prefix accuracy (100% with an FP32 sidecar) and 98%-100% mid-sentence resumption, outperforming 12.8K-sample LoRA (42.2% / 61.7%, 0% inline continuation). A factorized block-sparse sidecar holds A100 memory at 7.89 / 10.65 GiB (4.60 / 8.14 GiB under INT8/INT4 + FP32 sidecar) while passing all 14/14 pre-registered $>2$K-token Tango splits ($N=4{,}392$; 0/11,134 OOD triggers).

What If Chain-of-Thought Is Not Enough?

📅 2026-11-19 15:43
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reasoning capabilities in large language models have evolved from prompting-based methods to internalized model behaviors. In this paper, we investigate whether the internal thinking model of an LLM can be deliberately changed and whether such a change is beneficial. Instead of relying on Chain-of-Thought, we train models to follow Question--Option--Criteria (QOC), a structured decision process that decomposes a task into explicit questions, competing options, and evaluation criteria. We present a three-generation development pipeline that ranges from a basic QOC decision core to richer answer synthesis and statistically enriched QOC reasoning. We then evaluate the resulting models with respect to both internal consistency and output quality. Across the reported experiments, QOC-based fine-tuning improves answer quality relative to non-fine-tuned base models and yields particularly strong gains once models are large enough to execute the underlying QOC computations reliably. The results suggest that the internal thinking model of an LLM is trainable and that structured alternatives to Chain-of-Thought can provide a promising path toward more reliable, interpretable, and task-adapted decision support.

Beyond Weight Updates: Evolving Agent Harnesses for Domain Specialization

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Domain specialization of LLM agents is usually framed as a weight-update problem: collect trajectories, run reinforcement learning (RL) or supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and deploy a new checkpoint. We study a complementary axis: evolving the \textbf{agent harness}---the system prompt, tool schemas, skill library, and planning scaffolding---while keeping the policy frozen. We cast harness evolution as an RL-style outer loop and instantiate it with GEPA, a reflective evolutionary optimizer. The resulting harness adapters require no GPU training, are loadable per vertical domain, and compose with weight-based RL. Experiments with Codev, a Claude Code-style coding agent, on SWE-bench Verified, OfficeBench, and Terminal-Bench show a consistent pattern: weight updates give the strongest single-path gains, harness evolution recovers most of those gains at a small fraction of the compute, and combining both paths is best overall. We also analyze a failure mode of SFT-based specialization---shortened reasoning chains and increased truncation---and argue that harness adapters avoid it by construction. Our results position harness evolution as a practical, non-destructive complement to weight updates for vertical agents.

SiliconGym: A Versatile Reinforcement Learning Environment for Agentic EDA

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reinforcement learning (RL) with electronic design automation (EDA) feedback is advancing large language models for hardware design and verification. Extending these gains to hardware-engineering agents requires environments that span tasks, preserve state across tool calls, and support large hierarchies alongside parallel rollouts. We present SiliconGym, the first versatile, cross-task and cross-toolchain RL environment for hardware design and verification. By decoupling versioned workspaces, budgeted execution, and acceptance-gated scoring, SiliconGym scales from module-level RTL, testbench, and PPA tasks to multi-round repository repair and full-hierarchy SoC flows. Streaming dispatch overlaps tool checks with generation, finishing a 256-answer RL batch 21.9% faster than native ChipSeek. Using interchangeable open-source and commercial backends, we show that open-source-trained functional correctness transfers cleanly to commercial simulation, while paired netlist evaluation reveals when open-source PPA gains survive commercial synthesis.

Stop Going in Circles:Loop-Triggered Routing for System 1 Decision Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

System 1 decision models (represented by Jev) provide fast, low-cost action selection, but existing agent integrations do not reliably translate these local advantages into task completion. At certain points during execution, an agent may repeatedly select the same actions without making progress toward its goal. LLM-based policies offer strong reasoning capabilities that support planning within an agent, but invoking them at every step incurs substantial overhead. The effective coordination should therefore preserve Jev's fast local action selection while introducing LLM reasoning when execution stalls. We observe that recurring state-action pairs can signal stalled execution. We therefore propose LoopRoute, a simple but effective action router that uses this signal to allocate control within an agent. The decision model acts by default. When a loop is detected, the LLM provides brief, feedback-driven assistance and then returns control to the decision model. Later loops can request further assistance. The rule requires neither additional training nor model-specific confidence calibration. Across three benchmarks and seven LLMs, LoopRoute improves task success over standalone Jev with only limited use of the LLM policy. The mean gains are 21.0%, 60.1%, and 30.1% on ALFWorld, MapReader, and TextWorld cooking, respectively. Compared with full LLM control, LoopRoute also substantially reduces the mean cumulative API time and API-call cost per task. The same rule also improves the success rates of six frozen decision models.

ViDecide: Teaching Video Diffusion Models to Reason and Plan Step by Step

📅 2026-11-19 16:43
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Current leading video generation models can synthesize highly realistic videos, while still frequently violating physical and logical constraints in multi-step reasoning and planning, even after training with large-scale reasoning data or existing diffusion RL methods. We attribute these failures to the conventional video training paradigm, which treats a solution as a fixed-length clip rather than a chain of decisions. We present ViDecide, a decision-centric framework that trains video diffusion models to predict state transitions causally and decide when to stop, with a decision-focused training schedule. We further propose Corrective Fine-tuning (CFT), which corrects failed decisions by mapping their noise to the model's own successful continuations, achieving stable improvement without policy-gradient optimization. Across abstract games, planning tasks, and a new benchmark of multi-step robot planning, ViDecide achieves an average strict success rate of 83.8%, far above 23.3% for standard fine-tuning and 67.8% for the strongest prior method, and generalizes to larger puzzles and unseen layouts. Our framework therefore unlocks greater potential for video generation models as reliable reasoners and planners in the visual world. Project page: https://kevin-thu.github.io/ViDecide.

MemFolder: Structure-Guided Memory Folding for Efficient Agent Memory Management

📅 2026-11-19 17:05
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Long-term agents accumulate large external memory stores as interactions unfold, increasing the costs of storage, retrieval, and response generation. Efficiency is constrained by two coupled factors: a growing record count increases retrieval overhead, while longer retrieved contexts increase prefill computation and decoding-time attention costs. Existing methods prune or consolidate records, but pruning can discard useful evidence, whereas consolidation may produce fewer yet longer retrieval units. We introduce MemFolder, a structure-guided memory folding framework that sequentially reduces record count and context length. The framework comprises two core components that share memory groups, using cross-record structure to guide both evidence consolidation and compact representation. RecordFolder grounds entity mentions, progressively selects representative yet complementary anchors, and combines semantic and entity affinity with temporal chaining to consolidate compatible records into fewer retrieval units. ContextFolder encodes the original records within these groups into compact text and uses verification feedback to revise encodings when key facts cannot be recovered. Experiments on the three text-based memory benchmarks LoCoMo, PersonaMem, and LongMemEval and the two multimodal benchmarks M3-Bench-web and M3-Bench-robot demonstrate the strong performance of our methods. Notably, on Mem0, RecordFolder retains 99.0% of the original average performance across the three text-based benchmarks with 50% fewer records, exceeding the strongest baseline by 1.9 percentage points in performance retention. Combined with ContextFolder, existing record compression methods further shorten their output records while retaining most of their performance.

VG-Steer: Visual-Geometric Policy Steering from In-Context Aligned Abstractions

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Adapting robot policies to out-of-distribution (OOD) settings with limited real-world data remains a key bottleneck to generalist robots. The multimodality and scarcity of robotics data lead end-to-end approaches to overfit and break under small distribution shifts. Test-time adaptation can alleviate this by correcting frozen policies at inference, but most methods ground their correction in a signal learned implicitly from robotic datasets, making it susceptible to the same shifts as the policy it corrects. Recent methods that instead exploit explicit spatial information are typically limited to coarse corrections, missing the nuanced, task-relevant motion most manipulation tasks require. We propose VG-Steer, which corrects existing policies by transferring the nuanced motion of successful in-distribution rollouts, captured along meaningful geometric axes, known here as object-relational distance pairs. VG-Steer leverages the semantic understanding of Large Vision Models (LVMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) reasoning, which combines the task-relevant entity pairs and their object-relational distance projections into a single tailored visual-geometric guidance signal. In a simulation benchmark, it efficiently corrects flow-matching policies under pose and task-goal shifts; on a real robot experiment, it widens the region of the workspace the frozen policy covers, more than doubling its success rate on cable placements one grid step outside the training positions, and steers a single-task policy toward objects it was never trained on while nearly eliminating object confusion. Project website: https://arthurfenderbucker.github.io/VG-Steer/

Plan your Self-Adaptation! Ontology-Mediated Task and Architecture Co-adaptation Planning for Robots

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autonomous robots may need to adapt both their task plans and architectural configurations as operating conditions change. These decisions are interdependent: architectural configurations determine which actions a robot can perform, while task plans determine which capabilities are required. This paper presents PLANTA, an ontology-mediated approach to task and architecture co-adaptation that considers these dependencies during mission planning. PLANTA uses an ontology expressed in the Web Ontology Language to represent architectural alternatives and their associated functional and quality requirements, while the Planning Domain Definition Language defines task actions and mission goals. By automatically translating and integrating these models, PLANTA enables joint planning of task and reconfiguration actions. The approach uses satisficing planning and replans when changes during execution invalidate the current plan. PLANTA is evaluated in two robotic scenarios involving underwater inspection and ground navigation. The results support the feasibility of runtime co-adaptation and show lower planning time and memory consumption compared with optimal-policy generation in the evaluated navigation scenario, while producing plans of similar quality.

Does the Workspace Do the Work? A Causal Protocol for Verifying Global-Workspace Computation in a Language Model

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Global Workspace Theory (GWT), a leading theory of consciousness, holds that information becomes conscious when it is broadcast to specialized processes that use it. Building a workspace into a network does not reveal what the trained network computes with it: a module that reports an answer may have combined a broadcast message with its own knowledge, or merely forwarded an answer computed elsewhere, and task accuracy and ablation cannot tell these apart. We introduce a causal protocol that identifies who computes when modules communicate. Its central test transplants a message from another input, chosen so that ignoring, relaying and recombining it predict three different answers. On networks built to relay or to recombine, the test assigns the right computation in every case. Among trained workspace models it finds all three outcomes: one leaves its broadcast nearly unused, and of two models that differ only in their training data, one relays and the other recombines. In AFGW, a language model whose modules communicate only through a learned workspace, a transplanted message yields the answer that recombination predicts in 97% of cases, and its content acts by redirecting what the receiver retrieves from its own memory. The tests carry over to other communication designs and to a known interface of a pretrained Transformer, turning GWT's central claim into a testable property of trained models.

Demonstration-Free Skill Composition for Navigation and Manipulation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Hierarchical reinforcement learning enables sequential tasks to be represented as compositions of separately trained skills. This paper evaluates demonstration-free hierarchical pipelines for MiniGrid navigation and robotic manipulation, comparing their training requirements and task performance with flat reinforcement learning baselines. The evaluation concerns complete pipelines with task-specific observation, reward and control structures. In navigation, two parameterised skills are reused across unseen FourRooms layouts under manually specified high-level plans. In manipulation, reaching, grasp alignment and lifting are trained separately, with downstream skills initialised from states collected during predecessor rollouts. Across unseen navigation layouts, the hierarchical pipeline substantially outperforms the evaluated flat DQN baselines while requiring fewer training interactions. In manipulation, the hierarchical chain requires fewer training steps than flat SAC and exhibits lower observed variation across seeds. The frozen Pick and Lift skills are further reused for stacking by training an additional Place skill.

Keel: A Frontier Model Teaches a Vision-Language Model by Rewriting Its Weights

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Can a frontier model repair a vision-language model (VLM) by rewriting its weights? Small reasoning VLMs such as Qwen3.5 need such a repair: in thinking mode they fall into loops on many image questions, repeating themselves until the token limit, spending the whole budget and losing the answer. The usual remedies act on the decoding, leaving the looping model as it was, or must be told what to change. Yet the cause is written in the weights. Keel, the first agentic weight-editing method for VLMs on image tasks, finds and removes it there. Told only the symptom, an editor, a frontier multimodal LLM acting as an agent, locates the cause, edits the weights and tests them, with a failure census and instruments showing it the failing images and where the VLM attends; among several editors, the harness keeps only edits that clearly reduce the failure without lowering accuracy on questions no editor saw. The repair needs no training, keeps the model's size and per-token cost, and applies to already-edited weights. The editors expose where the loops live: from the original weights, independent editors on both models trace them to the attention outputs of the middle layers, and the smaller VLM stops consulting the image inside a loop. On Qwen3.5-0.8B and Qwen3.5-2B, across six image benchmarks from visual mathematics to GUI grounding, the edit removes most loops on every benchmark and shortens answers, and the edited models are significantly more accurate than the thinking models on average; the larger one gains most on GUI grounding, a task absent from the practice questions. Because the repair lives in the weights, it combines with budget forcing where held-out questions favor it: on the best available weights, Keel has the best average accuracy on both models, significantly above every training-free baseline we compare on the larger one.

Pro$^2$ Agent: Scientific Problem Probing for Autonomous AI R&D

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language model (LLM) agents can now autonomously conduct AI research by writing code and running experiments. They typically do so by iteratively optimizing a codebase, while ignoring the problem structure behind the task. This formulation is effective on simpler tasks, but on difficult problems it often gets stuck in the wrong solution region because the agent never explicitly represents its understanding of the problem. We introduce Pro$^2$ Agent, which shifts the primary state of autonomous AI research from a codebase state to a Problem State: an explicit structure of what problems prevent success and which solutions are likely to address them. Pro$^2$ Agent initializes the Problem State through comprehensive analysis of available resources and constraints. It then performs targeted research to relate the identified problems to candidate solutions. Through controlled hypothesis testing, the agent updates the importance of a suspected problem or the benefit brought by a candidate solution. On the complete MLE-Bench, Pro$^2$ Agent matches the best reported medal rate (70.7%) under the standard 24-hour budget and reaches 76.0% with a 48-hour budget, the highest medal rate reported on the complete benchmark. It also achieves competitive performance on PostTrainBench. In addition, it produces research notes for each task that make the research process transparent and reusable by human researchers.

ROAM-GRPO: Enforcing Reasoning Diversity in GRPO via Routed Off-mode Approach Mining

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) paired with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become the dominant recipe for post-training LLMs on complex reasoning tasks, but has been shown to collapse the policy into a low-diversity mode. Repeated LLM outputs are nearly identical, and pass@k accuracy converges or degrades as a result. We propose ROAM-GRPO (Routed Off-mode Approach Mining for GRPO), an augmentation to the GRPO algorithm which incentivizes exploration of distinct reasoning strategies and aims to increase output creativity during evaluation. ROAM-GRPO detects well-solved problems during training and transfers them to an "off-mode" track. The model is prompted to produce distinct solution strategies to such problems, which are verified for logical validity and distinctness by an LLM judge and subsequently self-distilled into the policy. Training Qwen3.5-2b on a 1,000-problem subset of the MATH lvl5 dataset, ROAM-GRPO implementation increases peak pass@16 by 4.7% and correct embedding cosine distance by approximately 1.5-2x on a difficult evaluation set.

Meta-RTL: Evolving Agent Harnesses for RTL Optimization

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models enable agents to optimize RTL using EDA feedback, yet their executable search procedures remain largely hand- engineered. We present Meta-RTL, an EDA-grounded framework that evolves RTL optimization harnesses from a minimal loop while keeping the optimization model, EDA flow, and per-design budgets fixed.
