Efficient Latent Reasoning for Vision-Language Model via Dynamics-Aware Quantization and Distillation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Efficient chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs) requires reducing both intermediate reasoning steps and their execution cost. Latent CoT methods compress textual rationales into continuous thoughts, yet their full-precision (FP) models retain substantial storage and computation costs. Lowering numerical precision could reduce these remaining costs, yet direct quantization severely degrades reasoning accuracy, particularly with 4-bit weights and activations. Operator and perturbation analyses reveal key sensitivity and direction-dependent predictive effects of continuous-thought errors, motivating preservation of information retrieval and subsequent reasoning beyond reconstruction accuracy. We therefore introduce QL-VLM, a dynamics-aware Quantization framework for efficient Latent reasoning in Vision-Language Models (VLMs). First, Dynamics-Guided Quantization (DGQ) uses nonuniform codebooks that preserve rotary-key structure, mitigating quantization-induced distortion in vision-language information retrieval. Second, Dynamics-Preserving Distillation (DPD) aligns continuous thoughts, latent-query readouts, and predictions with a full-precision teacher, maintaining predictive consistency through subsequent low-precision reasoning. On ScienceQA-IMG with Qwen2-VL-2B-Instruct, 4-bit QL-VLM achieves 90.43% accuracy and improves baseline by 40.33%, with 16.64x fewer bit-operations and 6.40x lower measured latency than explicit CoT.

Some Memories Must Fade: Persistence and Decay of User Memories in Model Parameters

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Parametric memory allows Large Language Models to internalize knowledge directly into their weights, offering a potentially powerful alternative to retrieval-based memory (RAG). However, existing approaches to parametric memory are difficult to deploy as they largely mirror the Continual Learning (CL) objective of retaining information indefinitely. This is neither sustainable nor sufficient for a memory system, which must govern knowledge across its lifecycle by retaining stable facts, reinforcing recurring observations, updating changed beliefs, and allowing transient information to fade. We propose a system for parametric memory management consisting of a memory substrate (handling information storage and forgetting) and a learned policy (what to remember, how deeply, and for how long). Our substrate is a fixed-capacity LoRA adapter that consists of k-hot rank combinations which store facts without catastrophic interference and maintain an independently controlled temporal decay, enabling outdated information to fade while long-term facts persist. On this substrate, we train a lightweight controller that governs each incoming user event to ensure the memory system reflects the user's true, updated belief state. To evaluate how memory systems manage user memory, we introduce a controlled dataset of long-horizon user trajectories with varying persistence of facts based on changing belief states and user-behavioral shifts, and probe queries that test recall while information is valid and abstention after it expires. Our policy learns to effectively manage user memory over its entire lifecycle, balancing recall and abstention to achieve 86% of oracle performance, unlike CL and RAG baselines that primarily default to one of the two behaviors. By providing the first system to manage memory directly in model weights, this work establishes a foundation for long-horizon parametric memory.

Falsifiable Compositional Reasoning in Time Series Forecasting

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Time series foundation models as well as the language-guided analytical agents achieve strong accuracy on standard chronological benchmarks. However, models that rank highest on historical validation splits often degrade across non-stationary regime transitions. We hypothesise that this gap arises from using historical backtesting as an open-ended search and model-selection objective instead of using it to test pre-specified compositional assumptions. Real-world time series are generated by recurring latent primitives that co-move within finite historical windows. Under strong historical co-movement, chronological holdout error cannot separate predictors that learn invariant couplings among primitives from predictors that fit transient historical correlations. Searching over large pools of architectures, hyperparameters, or chain-of-thought rules further biases selection toward historical shortcuts. Through theoretical analysis and diagnostic experiments on a controlled multi-primitive benchmark and German day-ahead electricity prices, we show that expanding validation search fails to improve out-of-regime accuracy, and that decomposing primitives without an ex-ante falsification test still breaks down when residual correlations shift. Hence, we propose formulating time series forecasting and reasoning around falsifiable compositional hypotheses that combine neural primitive encoders, explicit coupling constraints, and training-calibrated falsification tests.

FlashX: Scaling Robotic Experience Reuse for Embodied Agents with CBA-Based NAND Flash

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Embodied agents are emerging as a promising paradigm for complex robotic tasks, leveraging Vision-Language-Action (VLA) and World Action Models (WAMs) to generate actions through Diffusion Transformer (DiT)-based iterative denoising. However, existing models often struggle to generalize to out-of-distribution (OOD) environments. We observe that retrieving successful experiences and using them to warm-start denoising enables agents to better adapt to unfamiliar situations while reducing computational overhead. This creates a new opportunity to improve task accuracy and inference efficiency simultaneously, in contrast to conventional acceleration techniques that trade accuracy for computation. However, realizing this opportunity requires scaling experience banks, introducing substantial storage and search overhead that challenges the capacity, bandwidth, and power constraints of edge systems. We propose FlashX, a CMOS Directly Bonded to Array (CBA)-based NAND flash architecture for scalable experience reuse in embodied agents. FlashX exploits CBA's high internal bandwidth for parallel pSLC key search, employing interleaved Compute-in-Flash (CIF) engines to sustain sensing throughput while retaining dense TLC latent storage. Proprioception-aware clustering further reduces search overhead and enables incremental experience acquisition without global reclustering. Evaluations across VLA and WAM workloads demonstrate up to 12.24\%p and 7.43\%p improvements in task success under dense and compute-efficient configurations, respectively. FlashX achieves 2.57$\times$ higher search throughput than the strongest prior NAND search architecture under pSLC sensing, with a further 9.7$\times$ search reduction through clustering. This delivers 1.29--3.95$\times$ end-to-end speedup and 1.12--2.34$\times$ energy efficiency improvement over SSD-based retrieval.

Global Importance Estimation for KV Cache Eviction

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Memory consumption of the key-value (KV) cache remains a central bottleneck for long-context LLM inference. We introduce PRKV, a training-free KV eviction method that estimates multi-hop global token importance by applying Personalized PageRank over attention-induced transition graphs. This enables importance propagation beyond recency or instantaneous attention, without modifying the model. We benchmark PRKV on long-context understanding, sparse retrieval, and chain-of-thought reasoning tasks using multiple LLM families. Under moderate to aggressive compression ratios, including up to 90%, PRKV achieves competitive task performance against established eviction baselines while reducing peak memory usage with low end-to-end overhead. These results demonstrate that multi-hop global influence estimation yields a favorable accuracy--memory trade-off for scalable long-context inference.

AppSci-Bench: Can LLMs Build AI Agents and Make Them Better?

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used not only to power AI agents but also to build them. While many benchmarks assess the quality of LLM- powered agents, few ascertain how good LLMs are at building them. To this end, we introduce AppSci-Bench, where we construct a setting akin to how human Applied Scientists build agents for businesses - through eliciting task requirements from stakeholders, exploring the business's infrastructure, and scientifically improve the target agent by designing experiments and internal benchmarks. AppSci-Bench features seven realistic domains - five new ones (Slides Generation, Order-to-Cash, Compliance Review, Customer Call Insights, and CRM Insights) and two domains recast from tau3-bench (Yao et al., 2024; Shi et al., 2026b)(Airline and Banking) - each with a held-out evaluation suite customized for that task. We evaluate seven frontier models and find AppSci-Bench challenging: the best model, Claude Opus 5.0, only reaches a mean-minimum score of 59.1% across 3 build attempts; other models do significantly worse. Models struggle with eliciting task requirements (only 65% for Opus 5.0), build representative internal evaluations (models saturate their own evaluation 65% of the time but fail to solve the held-out task), presenting challenges for future research. The benchmark code is available here: https://github.com/sanjari-orb/appsci-bench.

AutoMaster: Autonomous Post-Training by Making the Same Model a Better Teacher

📅 2026-11-19 17:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Task success alone does not specify which behaviors post-training should reinforce. Even successful teacher trajectories can contain unsupported assumptions, unproductive repetition, or superficial verification. We propose Skill-FT (Skill-Guided Fine-Tuning), which uses natural-language skills to suppress undesirable habits and elicit underrepresented effective behaviors during teacher rollouts, then fine-tunes a target model on the resulting trajectories. We introduce AutoMaster, an autonomous post-training system in which an LLM agent develops these interventions through trajectory analysis, skill design, data selection, and feedback from rollouts and target-model evaluation. Across Terminal, Search, and software engineering tasks with 4B and 9B target models, AutoMaster improves mean performance over Vanilla SFT on successful unguided teacher trajectories by 1.42-8.99 percentage points, with the teacher, task pool, training set size, and SFT recipe held fixed. Together with the ablations and trajectory analyses, these results suggest that behavioral shaping can make a fixed model a more effective teacher. We further find that retaining skill instructions during SFT yields higher mean performance than removing them across all six settings, despite their absence at evaluation and identical supervised outputs. These findings suggest that preserving skill context as part of a training demonstration can support better behavioral internalization.

BuggyMLE: Scaling Machine Learning Engineering Difficulty with Bug Injection

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Enabling AI systems to autonomously improve AI models requires agents capable of performing machine learning engineering (MLE). In practice, designing, training, and evaluating complex models demands modifying multi-file repositories to reproduce results, debug training runs, integrate datasets, and port frameworks. However, existing MLE benchmarks limit agents to single-file edits without full repository or shell access, and common repository-level software engineering benchmarks have rapidly saturated. To address this, we introduce the Code Complexity Index (CCI), a difficulty metric based on ten structural features spanning repository scale, dependency depth, and patch size. CCI strongly predicts average solve rates across eight public coding benchmarks ($R^2 = 0.951$), identifying patch size and complexity as top predictors. Guided by CCI, we develop BuggyMLE, a scalable framework where agents inject controlled mutations into working ML repositories, synthesizing over 6,600 bug-injected MLE repository variants and nearly 260K lines of injected code based on 222 source repositories. Using BuggyMLE, we make two contributions. First, we construct BuggyMLE-Bench-Lite, the first difficulty-graded, repository-level variant of MLE-Bench Lite; across 14 evaluated models, the top solve rate drops from 95% to 9% as difficulty is scaled from the easiest to the hardest tier. Second, we synthesize a training curriculum across three difficulty levels over 200 additional ML repositories, fine-tuning a Qwen3.5-9B model to rival frontier models on BuggyMLE-Bench-Lite. Our trained model transfers beyond ML, raising its SWE-bench Lite resolve rate from 24% to 33% and its LiveCodeBench v6 pass@1 from 69% to 71%.

MemMend: A Selective Revision Framework for Memory Question Answering

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Revising every answer from a long-term memory can consume substantial inference budget and damage facts that were already correct. We introduce MemMend, a modular selective revision framework that routes potentially incomplete answers to an editor, represents corrections as bounded exact-text replacements, and accepts only edits with resolvable source coordinates and valid edit operations. The editor preserves the current answer when no legal repair is proposed; accepted edits require no additional semantic-verification call. On 1,986 LoCoMo development questions, MemMend achieves 67.17 conversation-macro F1 and 88.35 mean Judge score, improving over a verified revision chain by 0.82 and 1.24 points while using 62.37% fewer revision calls. Routing uses 54.15% fewer revision calls than ungated local review, with similar F1 but a 0.67-point lower Judge score. Applied directly to native PropMem, Mem0, and SimpleMem answers, the editing interface raises F1 by 3.79, 6.44, and 0.42 points. It outperforms same-gate rewriting and saves 49.85-83.72% of editing calls relative to ungated editing, which retains higher F1 in this adaptation. A source-by-editing factorial supports local editing for F1 but leaves an independent quality benefit from source obligations unresolved. Transfer to 451 LongMemEval-V2 Small questions also favors revision efficiency, while an external PerLTQA dialogue subset reveals a completeness gap. These results support selective editing as a reusable quality-cost operating point.

PGX: Physically Grounded Execution for Long-Horizon Humanoid Loco-Manipulation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Robotic vision-language-action (VLA) models can execute diverse individual manipulation skills, yet they still struggle with long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks, where temporal structure, skills, and precision are tightly coupled within a monolithic model. We propose Physically Grounded Execution (PGX), a framework that offloads temporal and precision control to two classical training-free components, allowing the VLA to focus on stage-specific skill dynamics. PGX decomposes long-horizon loco-manipulation into three layers: (i) a predicate-based state machine governs task progression, using physical predicates to gate phase transitions, trigger recovery, and coordinate navigation and waiting. Shared predicate semantics connect offline demonstration segmentation, online execution, and stage-wise evaluation; (ii) VLA skills operate within active manipulation phases, with flat or stage-trained models concentrating on task-relevant actions; and (iii) world-coordinate locking (WCL) maintains spatial consistency through base station keeping, object-relative positioning, and world-anchored contact targets. We demonstrate the effectiveness of PGX with extensive experiments conducted on three simulated tasks and one real-world task using a Unitree G1 humanoid robot for long-horizon loco-manipulation, showing improvements in stage progression and task success rates. Project page is available at: https://kl-research.github.io/pgx

AgentRoboCritic: Understanding the Capability of Frontier Agents on Direct Robot Control

📅 2026-11-19 18:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Frontier multimodal language agents such as GPT-6 Astra have demonstrated strong capabilities in controlling robots directly via low-level numerical actions without high-level primitives. However, existing benchmarks report only episode-level success rates, which show neither how these agents succeed nor where they fail. In this paper, we present a systematic action-level analysis of frontier agents on direct robot control across 12 manipulation benchmarks. We introduce AgentRoboCritic, an intent-grounded critique framework in which the policy agent states a local intent alongside each action, and a critic evaluates the action's outcome and failure mode against this intent. Our action-level analysis demonstrates the promising capabilities of frontier agents on direct robot control, including closed-loop recovery from local execution failures, reliable semantic grounding across diverse scenes, and strong zero-shot manipulation without task-specific adaptation. It also identifies their current limitations, including task-relevant physical execution bottlenecks such as grasping and alignment, and uneven recovery across failure modes. Finally, we validate our critic on a 400-action human-annotated benchmark and release a dataset of 12,844 annotated actions alongside a fine-tuned open-source critic, Muse-Glimmer-30B, whose failure-mode accuracy rises from 32.58% to 74.24%, enabling low-cost action-level analysis.

When Safety Rationales Hallucinate: Diagnosing and Grounding Multimodal Safety Guards

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal safety guards increasingly generate rationales alongside safety predictions, yet whether these rationales are reliable remains largely unexplored. We systematically study rationale hallucination from two perspectives: visual rationale hallucination, where rationales contain unsupported visual facts, and safety attribution hallucination, where grounded content is assigned an incorrect safety interpretation. Across rationale-generation methods, multiple vision-language backbones, and safety datasets, we find that rationale hallucination is widespread and does not consistently diminish with higher classification accuracy or increased model scale. Further analysis of the Think-with-Image paradigm shows that hallucination is concentrated in fine-grained attributes and relations, while visual tools are often invoked infrequently or without sufficient spatial focus. Based on these findings, we propose Tool-Contrastive Grounding Reinforcement Learning (TCG-RL), which rewards tool use only when it improves rationale grounding over a tool-free reference. TCG-RL substantially reduces rationale hallucination while maintaining competitive safety prediction performance. By revealing the gap between prediction correctness and rationale reliability, our study motivates a shift in multimodal safety guarding from outcome-only evaluation toward safety decisions that are both accurate and verifiably grounded.

Bridging vision-language reasoning and learned control for legged navigation in the wild

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autonomous navigation in diverse real-world environments remains one of the greatest challenges in robotics. It demands both robust low-level motion control to handle diverse terrain and high-level reasoning to interpret environmental cues. We present ROAM (Robust Open-world Autonomous Mobility), a system that enables legged robots to perform kilometer-scale navigation in complex alpine and urban settings. ROAM combines the world knowledge of a Vision-Language Model (VLM), pre-trained on internet-scale data, with robust locomotion and local planning policies learned in simulation via reinforcement learning (RL). The VLM provides common-sense reasoning and semantic interpretation of the scene, assessing risks, interpreting signs, and producing semantic labels with associated navigation costs. An open-vocabulary segmentation module converts these labels into a dense, real-time risk map, which conditions two pre-trained RL policies responsible for agile locomotion and local navigation, effectively coupling high-level VLM reasoning with low-level physical control. Real-world experiments showed the system's ability to autonomously navigate a wide range of complex real-world environments. The robot completes kilometer-long hikes with minimal intervention, interprets signs to avoid danger, and evaluates its environment to identify safe areas on which to step. This work represents a step toward the reliable deployment of systems in unstructured environments. However, it also underscores the challenges that remain in VLM reliability and spatial reasoning, indicating that foundational models are a crucial component but not yet a complete solution for navigation in the wild.

SoK: Assessing the Evolution of LLM Capability in Software Vulnerability Research

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM-based agents are becoming increasingly capable at discovering, validating and patching vulnerabilities in real software, and vendors and researchers report that this is already changing how vulnerabilities are found and fixed. These claims, however, are scattered across papers, vendor reports and disclosures and are rarely comparable. We introduce a framework that rates these capabilities on an autonomy scale modeled on the levels of driving automation, and qualifies each claim by its correctness, its availability and the strength of its evidence. We use this framework to systematize the published evidence on LLM capability in vulnerability research. We find that agents discovered, validated, and patched vulnerabilities in real-world software without human intervention before the restricted frontier models of 2026, and that open-weight models subsequently attained comparable autonomy for discovery and validation. We then measure the consequences of evolving LLM security capabilities in a decade of public vulnerability records, advisories and exploitation data. In 2026 the number of disclosed vulnerabilities more than doubled, with an increasing share rated critical or high, and severe bugs that had survived a decade or more in widely used code were reported several times more often than before. Yet the time between the stages of a vulnerability's life, from report to fix and from disclosure to attack, has not shifted noticeably so far. We grade the mitigations proposed in the literature with an explicit rubric and draw guidance for each group of stakeholders from the best-supported ones.

Decoupled Knowledge Retrieval and Compact Memory Enable Reliable Lifelong Model Editing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Lifelong model editing requires continuously incorporating new facts into deployed large language models (LLMs) without expensive retraining or compromising existing capabilities. Memory-based editors offer an appealing solution by keeping the base parameters untouched, yet their practical use is hindered by linearly growing storage requirements and deteriorating retrieval performance as edits accumulate. To tackle these problems, we introduce BiSpace, a lifelong model editing framework that decouples memory retrieval from knowledge injection across two distinct representation spaces. BiSpace routes queries and identifies relevant edits in an external semantic space, while injecting the corresponding knowledge into the internal representation space of the LLM through sparse and storage-efficient updates. Extensive experiments show that BiSpace shrinks each entry's storage to only about 10.2% of the original and shows strong resilience to long edit sequences of up to 16K edits. On a more challenging benchmark, BiSpace further improves Overall Performance by more than 23% over existing methods. These results advance memory-based editing toward reliable long-term model maintenance at scale.

Physics of Language Models: Part 4.2, Canon Layers at Scale where Synthetic Pretraining Resonates in Reality

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Do standard benchmarks see the architectural differences that matter? We study it with 64 from-scratch pretraining runs of Transformers and three linear-model families at 1.3-8B parameters on 1-2T Nemotron-CC tokens, at a cost of roughly 100k USD per 8B run. In academic-scale pretraining (1.3B parameters, 100B tokens), architectural differences can all but disappear when the models are carefully tuned. Instead, at 1-2T tokens they become measurable: Canon layers, which add local token mixing, consistently improve Transformers, saving about 30% of pretraining tokens for the same MMLU accuracy at 8B. We also introduce GLA5 and GDN2, which improve gated linear attention and Gated DeltaNet with low-rank output gates. Surprisingly, with Canon, the simplest linear model, gated linear attention, matches Gated DeltaNet and outperforms Mamba2 even at 8B/1T. Therefore, the extra machinery of Gated DeltaNet and Mamba2 may do little more than mix neighboring tokens. Even at 8B/1T, however, high scores on standard benchmarks can hide weaknesses in basic in-context skills. In particular, linear models that match or beat Transformers on these benchmarks cannot, in fact, reliably retrieve information from a short 500-token context. Moreover, no model, Transformer or linear, reliably beats random guessing on arguably the simplest two-hop question, stated in a few short sentences, even at this scale. These findings $\textbf{challenge traditional architecture research}$ based primarily on standard benchmark scores. In contrast, small synthetic playgrounds, which predicted the architectural differences above at <100M parameters, can reveal what these benchmarks miss. For practitioners, one lesson is to include in-context reasoning examples in pretraining data from an early stage, rather than reserve them for later mid-training, let alone post-training.

Many Solutions, One Answer: Search Order Shapes the Default Solutions of Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When a constraint puzzle has several correct answers, language models tend to give the same one.We study four open-weight models from three families on symbolic puzzles and multi-solution ZebraLogic variants with exactly known solution sets.Repeated samples concentrate on one default solution far beyond uniform sampling, and different models often share it.Yet models usually produce even rarely sampled solutions when added clues uniquely identify them.On held-out symbolic puzzles, we compare visiting entities in name order with visiting those with the fewest initial candidates first. Both searches try values in the stated order. Where their pre-specified predictions differ, the latter matches a larger share of responses in all four models, by 26 to 40 percentage points.Reversing the stated value order or specifying a search order shifts responses towards the solution that the new order favours, on both testbeds. Randomising the presentation or supplying a random search order raises breadth (the effective share of solutions in use) by 26 to 42 percentage points on the symbolic task and 11 to 26 on ZebraLogic. The largest observed validity losses are 3 and 4 percentage points, respectively. Randomised presentation broadens answers more than a random-choice instruction in each of the three main models.

What the model knows but does not say: monitoring medical LLMs through verbalized thoughts

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Efforts to keep clinical language models safe have concentrated on what enters and leaves the model, but a manipulated model can return an answer that looks entirely ordinary. Here we show that the model's own internal state, rendered in plain language, exposes manipulations the output conceals. Using a natural-language autoencoder, we translate the activations of a multimodal LLM (Gemma-3-27B) into text and let an input-blind judge read this verbalized internal state (the thoughts channel) and the model's answer separately. Across two clinical attack objectives (omitting a pathology, covertly recommending a drug), two input modalities, and two delivery mechanisms, the thoughts channel caught the injection significantly more often than the answer in all eight injected conditions (p<0.05), while both channels held near-zero false-positive rates on clean inputs; the omission result carried over to Llama-3.3-70B. The thoughts channel also registered unsafe clinical requests as harmful while the model was still reading them, before it decided whether to refuse. None of this requires retraining: an off-the-shelf verbalizer and a general-purpose judge are enough.

A Verifier from the Past: Test-Time Compute for Time Series Foundation Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Test-time compute (TTC) improves language models by spending additional inference compute to generate, verify, and select among candidate outputs, but this recipe has not transferred directly to time series forecasting. A language model can verify a proof or vote across samples, but a forecaster must be causal; it cannot use future information to score a candidate. We propose Backtest-Gated Augmentation Averaging (BGAA), a TTC algorithm for time series forecasting that builds a causal verifier from the series's own history, using rolling-origin backtests to score structured views of the context, discard those that underperform the untransformed context, and average the survivors' predictive distributions. BGAA improves every tested TSFM-benchmark combination, reducing MASE by 0.5-1.0% on GIFT-Eval, 1.3-5.0% on fev-bench, and 1.7-5.9% on BOOM, where a 313M model with TTC outperforms the 1B and 2.5B models in its family. We further show that useful views have structured, model-dependent effects consistent with a prior-transport interpretation. On the 53 cross-fittable GIFT-Eval configs, the deployed policy gains only 0.4-1.3% CRPS, versus 1.4-4.1% when choosing the best view for each dataset configuration and 17-19% when choosing the best view separately for each test window. This gap suggests that a major remaining challenge is window-level view selection.

TipDex-VTLA: Learning When to Refine Actions with Touch for Dexterous Manipulation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Changes in illumination can induce action drift in dexterous manipulation even when the physical state remains unchanged. Although existing vision--tactile--language--action (VTLA) policies incorporate tactile feedback, when and how touch refines actions under changing visual conditions remains challenging. We introduce \textbf{TipDex-VTLA}, a VTLA policy that combines estimated visual action reliability and tactile forecast verification to regulate execution-time refinement. Specifically, Visual Action Sensitivity Estimation (VASE) learns visual reliability from a frozen reference policy's action sensitivity to illumination perturbations. Tactile Anticipation and Correction during Execution (TACE) compares predicted and measured touch at matched times and combines the resulting discrepancy with visual reliability to gate bounded feature corrections for unexecuted actions. This effectively helps to refine actions under challenging unseen visual conditions. We evaluate TipDex-VTLA on four real-world dexterous manipulation tasks and the UniVTAC simulation benchmark. TipDex-VTLA trained on data collected under normal visual conditions, achieves average full-task success rates of 87.5% (resp. 73.8%) under normal (resp. challenging unseen) lighting in real-world tasks, significantly outperforming some state-of-the-art methods. We will release the collected data and source code.

Validation-driven Deployment of LLM Inference Platforms for Agentic Workloads

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Enterprises are moving agentic workloads from closed APIs to their own open-weight model deployments. For this reason, lifecycle-wide validation, from small-scale testing through sizing and pre-production to production, becomes all the more important. Typically, rollouts are evaluated with ad hoc tools whose accuracy is rarely checked. We present an end-to-end evaluation pipeline for agentic LLM serving and empirically ground the design choices that connect its stages. Our experiments show that replaying captured traces reproduces the per-session load of the original agent sessions: at the 95th percentile replay stays within 1.18x of the recorded input tokens while live re-runs reach 4.98x, so a small set of recorded sessions yields a repeatable per-session token load. A code-coverage analysis of vLLM further shows that the evaluated live, replayed, and synthetic workloads are complementary in this configuration: each exercises Python serving paths not reached in the measured runs of the others. In a case study rolling out four large mixture-of-experts (MoE) models on llm-d, experiments show that the target models reached about half the optimal concurrency, and correspondingly about 60% of the throughput, on captured workloads compared with public traces. We open-source the relevant tools and release a trace dataset.

Sirius-Tiamat: How Small Can a One-Pass Decision Encoder Get Before Its Decisions Change?

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A one-pass decision encoder reads a text, a question and several named options, and returns a probability for each option in a single forward pass. We ask how far Laya, a 421M-parameter encoder of this kind, can be compressed before its decisions change. We count decisions that differ from Laya's own full-precision (fp32) answer, and track calibration (how well confidence matches accuracy, measured by expected calibration error, ECE), on 1,221 synthetic items with the training schemas, 273 with an unseen schema, and 800 out-of-family questions from ARC, MMLU and HellaSwag. Layer pruning of a related model family is known; we add flips, calibration, held-out data and retraining. bf16 changes 0.2% of in-family decisions but 2.2% out of family; 6-bit grouped rounding 1.3% and 5.9%; deleting 6 layers 0.9% and 9.1%, while ECE rises from 5.9% to 39.2%. One fitted temperature, needing no labels, restores it to 3.6%. One scale per tensor fails below six bits, and CPU dynamic int8 changes 30% of decisions, nearly all through one layer. Total variation tracks flips for rounding but not for deletion, and an early exit saves layers over a static cut only if its confidence gate is first recalibrated. Distilling a 16-layer student cuts its in-family flips from 44% to 9% (45% out of family). At equal stored size, rounding flips fewer decisions than deletion. Data are synthetic and LLM-labelled, for one architecture.

Safe Subgame Resolving in Multiplayer Games

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Poker and other games with hidden information are too large to solve in one pass. Strong systems compute a complete blueprint strategy offline and refine it in real time inside the reached subgame. In 2p0s games, a safety theorem guarantees that subgame resolving never increases how much an adversary can gain by exploiting the resolved strategy relative to the blueprint strategy. With three or more players acting independently, no such guarantee exists yet, and heuristic resolving can increase the profile's best-response gap. We prove the first conditional safety theorem for uncorrelated multiplayer subgame resolving, a post-hoc certificate for any delivered update. The best-response gap of the resolved profile exceeds that of the blueprint by at most four terms. Two concern the resolver: the subgame value the blueprint promised without realizing ($A_i^{S}$) and the resolver's subgame solve error ($\varepsilon_i$). The other two are the opponents' best-response gains inside the subgame over their promised values ($\sum_q M_{q}$) and a structural tax ($Θ$), which measures how much the players' total utility can change. The tax is zero in constant-sum games, including every rake-free poker variant. Constructions attain each term with equality. On three-player Leduc poker and on Kuhn poker with up to six players, resolving without a certificate raises the gap beyond a small tolerance in 12% to 100% of the updates. The certificate flags every such update and accepts 41% (Kuhn) and 86% (Leduc) of the standard resolving updates. A resolver that keeps the blueprint whenever the certificate exceeds the tolerance delivers no harmful update. The certificate needs only one best-response pass per opponent inside the subgame, so real-time multiplayer solvers that refine a blueprint can attach it as a safety check. Code is available at https://github.com/lbn187/MultiPlayerSolver

Anchor and Check: Deterministic Cross-Prompt, Cross-Model Reconciliation for Auditable LLM Decisions

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Pipelines that let a large language model (LLM) make discrete decisions need to know which decisions they may act on unattended, and a record that explains each one. We study a small, fully specified reconciliation contract: commit the target model's greedy answer (the anchor), draw one or two greedy checks that reach the answer by a different route (an alternate prompt, or a model from another family), and let a pure function return one of five outcomes, of which only Agree is acted on. On 48,000 model-item evaluations over 6,000 questions, with every method given access to verbalized confidence as a tie-breaker, the main finding concerns where calls come from: pooling another family's samples raises semantic entropy's AUROC by 1.7 to 4.0 points at every budget, and a same-model temperature check is the weakest design. As complete pipelines, the point estimates of the three-call contract and pooled semantic entropy over 3 or 5 samples differ by less than one point, with intervals containing zero. As detectors of the anchor's errors, the contract is ahead of pooled entropy with 3 and 5 samples under both tie-breaker conventions; with 10 samples the point estimates differ by less than one point and the sign depends on the convention. Under the strict Agree gate it automates 70% of items and still acts on 16% of errors. What the contract adds is a fixed committed answer, an explicit outcome and a decision replayable from its record; generation itself was not reproducible on the hosted API.

TacInno: Teaching Vision-Language-Action Models the Contact Dynamics Beyond Vision and Action

📅 2026-11-19 16:53
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently been equipped with tactile sensing to perceive visually subtle contact events in contact-rich manipulation. However, adding tactile input yields inconsistent gains, and some visuo-tactile VLA models rely little on tactile input, because much of the tactile change, such as the gel deformation during gripper closure, is predictable from visual observations and executed actions. To address this problem, we introduce TacInno, which isolates the contact dynamics that vision and executed actions cannot explain, termed tactile innovation, and uses tactile innovation only as a training target. Specifically, the TacInno tokenizer, conditioned on visual observations and executed actions, compresses the future tactile change into a discrete code, and the policy, TacInno-VLA, learns to predict the code, adding no module for the code at deployment. TacInno-VLA achieves average success rates of 87.5% across six simulation tasks and 70.5% across four real-world tasks, outperforming the strongest visuo-tactile baseline by 10.7 and 9.0 percentage points, respectively. Ablation studies validate the contributions of visual and action conditioning, multi-frame encoding, and auxiliary code prediction. Together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness of tactile innovation as a supervision signal for contact-rich manipulation.

AutoRAGBench: A Benchmark for LLM Agents Automating RAG System Development

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a well-established paradigm for augmenting large language models (LLMs) with information retrieved from external corpora. However, developing effective RAG systems requires substantial engineering effort and iterative experimentation, as effective design choices vary across tasks and domains. With rapid advances in LLM agent capabilities, we ask how well these agents can autonomously build RAG systems and how effectively they can improve them through iteration. In this paper, we propose AutoRAGBench, the first benchmark to evaluate how well LLM agents develop RAG systems from scratch in an open code space. It comprises nine RAG system development tasks. For each task, agents build RAG systems from scratch without starter code or prescribed strategies, under a six-hour budget on a single H800 GPU. They analyze task data, implement RAG pipelines, and iteratively refine their designs based on experimental feedback. We evaluate nine agent configurations and analyze both the effectiveness of the resulting RAG systems and their development trajectories. GPT-6.1 Sol with Codex CLI achieves the highest average score of 56.1. The composite Human-SOTA baseline averages 44.4. These results show that LLM agents can automate effective RAG system development. When starting from a human-designed system on each of seven tasks, GPT-6.1 Sol with Codex CLI improves the score by 16.6 points on average.

Dune: A Zeroth-Order Optimizer with Time-Conditioned Historical Error Regression for VLA Test-Time Adaptation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) enables Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models to handle distribution shifts during deployment without source-domain retraining. However, backpropagation-based adaptation incurs heavy memory overhead on resource-constrained robots, whereas zeroth-order (ZO) methods suffer from high estimation variance under limited perturbation budgets. We observe that while ZO estimation enables low-memory adaptation with rapid initial gains, it often stalls or degrades over long-horizon updates due to noisy gradient estimates. To address these limitations, we propose Dune, a zeroth-order optimizer that stabilizes long-horizon adaptation by coupling online historical error prediction with current perturbation observations. Specifically, Dune learns a lightweight, time-conditioned error predictor from past optimization dynamics and fuses its predictions with instantaneous ZO estimates, achieving effective variance reduction without backpropagation or extra forward passes. Additionally, we theoretically characterize how estimation variance restricts convergence in high-dimensional, small-population regimes, motivating the use of historical statistics. Extensive experiments on LIBERO and real-world robots demonstrate that Dune achieves superior success rates under a low memory footprint, with ablations confirming improved closed-loop stability and post-disturbance recovery.

Generalist Meets Specialists: An Evolving Multimodal Agent with Persistent Tool Orchestration for Traffic Scenario Understanding

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Traffic scenario understanding requires combining fine-grained visual evidence with spatial and temporal reasoning. Tool-augmented vision-language models (VLMs) can use specialized perception tools, but effective collaboration faces two challenges: terminal rewards provide limited guidance for intermediate decisions and error recovery, while repeated planning adds unnecessary cost across similar tasks. Echoing the fast-slow dichotomy of human cognition, we propose StarTV-Agent, which combines policy learning with experience evolution to support adaptive reasoning when needed and efficient reuse of prior experience when possible. During training, Role-Sensitive Process Optimization (RSPO) combines process advantages with terminal advantages, while Weakness-Driven Demonstration Learning (WDL) provides validated teacher demonstrations targeting the student's weak questions. During inference, Lightweight Adaptive Workflow Reuse (LAWR) enables a fast path by selecting suitable workflows and binding task-specific arguments and resource references, while a slow ReAct-based path handles tasks without suitable workflows. We further design label-free feedback based on execution completion, workflow adoption, and cost savings to guide workflow reuse and memory updates. Across three challenging benchmarks, StarTV-Agent achieves up to 8.0% relative performance improvement, while reducing token consumption by up to 52.1%.

From Geographic Memory to Spatial Generalization in Large Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) can associate places with geographic descriptions, but recovering an observed association does not establish an ability to generalize across geographic space. We distinguish geospatial memory, the recovery of location--description associations observed during training, from spatial generalization, the prediction of descriptions for locations in unobserved regions. We investigate whether continuous location representations improve this generalization and encourage geographic knowledge to become spatially organized within an LLM. We frame this organization as a latent spatial field: a learned mapping from locations to descriptions connected through spatial dependencies. Building on a continuous spatial encoder, we project location representations into the model's embedding space and supply them as spatial tokens. We evaluate the resulting models on two newly curated geo-text benchmarks for Los Angeles and London using two complementary assessments. Geospatial captioning tests both memory and spatial generalization by generating descriptions at observed locations and at locations beyond the spatial coverage of the training observations. A separate geospatial grounding model tests whether generated location--description pairs provide useful additional supervision for mapping text to geographic space. Across multiple LLM families, continuous location representations improve captioning at unobserved locations relative to textual coordinates, while their generated descriptions yield modest but consistent improvements in downstream grounding. Layer-wise probing and representation clustering provide additional evidence consistent with spatially organized geographic representations. Together, these findings show the value of evaluating spatial generalization separately from memory and of incorporating continuous spatial representations into language models.

Trace2ST: Recovering PLC Structured Text from I/O Traces with LLM Generation and Verification

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Programmable logic controllers (PLCs), the industrial computers that operate power and process plants, often run programs whose source code is no longer available; however, every input and output of the controller is logged continuously. This paper therefore asks whether a large language model (LLM) can recover an executable program in Structured Text (ST), the standard PLC language of IEC 61131-3, from such input/output records (traces) alone and, equally important, how the recovered program can be verified. To answer this question, we build a generate-replay-feedback pipeline. First, the LLM writes a candidate program from a compressed summary of the trace, without ever seeing the original program. The candidate is then executed one control cycle (scan) at a time and compared with the recording, and whenever a mismatch occurs the corresponding part of the trace is returned to the LLM, for at most three revisions. Because every result depends on the software that executes the candidates, we first validate it against two independent open-source PLC runtimes and find no unexplained difference. On this basis, we evaluate 47 third-party programs from two public benchmarks. The recovered programs reproduce the recorded trace exactly in 43 cases and agree with the original on 94% of scans for unseen inputs. The remaining errors, however, lie mostly in program branches that the trace never exercised; once a trace that targets these branches is added, agreement on unseen adversarial inputs rises from 0.80 to 0.98. By contrast, a classical automata-learning baseline without an LLM fits the recording but falls to 0.40 on unseen inputs, which indicates that recovering program structure is the hard part. Finally, on real data from a public water-treatment testbed, the recovered control laws match unseen measurements closely ($R^2 > 0.99$) and expose the only actuator attack in the dataset.

FinVeriQA: Financial Research Agents for Question Answering: Routing, Retrieval, MCP Tool Use, and Provenance-Aware Evidence Verification

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) write fluent financial analyses but miscompute ratios, recall stale prices, and route questions to the wrong tool with high confidence. We ask which component should do each job in a financial question-answering assistant, and study it with FinVeriQA, an open-source multi-agent research prototype over SEC filings and live market data: a compact typed-decision model (Laya) routes each question and screens out advice requests, Python computes every number, retrieval and tools fetch evidence, Mistral-7B writes a cited answer, a code validator checks that cited IDs exist and that numbers match computed notes, and a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposes the pipeline. In live experiments, Laya routed better than Mistral-7B (0.85 vs. 0.52; overall 0.86 vs. 0.68, McNemar p = 0.0003); a 12B Mistral decided no better, and DeepSeek-R1-8B decided better (0.79 vs. 0.68, p = 0.0192) but about 9.9 times more slowly. Mistral-7B computed 29% of 100 financial calculations correctly against 100% for Python, a live quote cut price error from 210% to 0.00%, and hybrid retrieval reached 76% Hit@5 on 50 filing questions. Yet in a live case study, both agent answers passed validation while describing AMD sample data under NVIDIA's and Alphabet's names. A controlled upper-bound study on the FinRank benchmark explains why: when one figure in the shared evidence is corrupted, self-consistency stays confident (agreement 0.55 to 0.59 while accuracy falls from 0.50 to 0.00), and a shared-evidence check (the kind our validator performs) catches 35% of the errors, whereas a check against the original passage catches 77% ($p = 5.96 \times 10^{-8}$); for DeepSeek-R1-8B, 68% vs. 90%. Verification must be independent of the evidence the writer saw.

Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs.

MixReact: Physics-Based Reactive Humanoid Control from Multimodal Cues via a Discrete-Continuous Mixture of Transformers

📅 2026-11-19 14:57
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Humanoid agents in everyday environments are expected to react to diverse cues as humans do, with semantically appropriate and physically executable behaviors. Existing cue-driven reaction generation methods support only a narrow range of cue modalities and are limited to purely kinematic generation, leaving the resulting reactions susceptible to physical artifacts. To this end, we present MixReact, the first unified framework for physics-based reactive humanoid control from text, audio, and video cues, integrating a Discrete--Continuous Mixture of Transformers (DisCo-MoT) for state prediction with a History-aware Action Transformer (HAT) for physical execution. DisCo-MoT couples a discrete masked-modeling branch with a continuous autoregressive diffusion branch via one-way mixture attention within a MoT architecture. Guidance from discrete context mitigates collapse into averaged behaviors in one-to-many reaction generation, while direct prediction in continuous state space bypasses the decoding bottleneck of discrete representations. HAT then maps the predicted next state and observed execution history to executable actions, enabling robust closed-loop control in physics-based simulation. Experiments across all three modalities demonstrate consistent improvements over the baselines in fidelity, diversity, and physical consistency, while ablations validate the complementary contributions of the components. Our code will be released to facilitate future research. Project page: https://binlee26.github.io/MixReact_Page/

Realized versus Population Reliability: Anytime-Valid Self-Consistency Certification under Correlated Reasoning

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Self-consistency certification treats repeated sampling from a language model as sequential evidence accumulation and stops when an anytime-valid e-process crosses a threshold, guaranteeing finite-sample Type I error control by Ville's inequality. Prior constructions assume sampled rollouts are conditionally independent. We show this assumption fails in practice: reasoning streams are positively correlated through shared reasoning prefixes or across-prompt heterogeneity, changing not merely certificate efficiency but the hypothesis certified. Modeling dependence via a per-unit latent state (the random-effects / intraclass-correlation structure) induces exchangeable, equi-correlated increments with correlation $ρ$. Correlation splits the single null into a realized null about the drawn belief and a population null about average model behavior, which coincide only when $ρ=0$. We show: (i) the standard betting e-process remains anytime-valid for the realized null, not the population null; (ii) read as a population-null test, a suitable mixture has asymptotic crossing probability at least $π=P(μ(Z)>0)$, potentially exceeding nominal $ε$; (iii) the within-unit mean's effective sample size saturates at $1/ρ$, so extra within-unit rollouts cannot restore population validity; and (iv) a prompt-level e-process restores population validity, with optimal allocation at one rollout per prompt under a pure rollout budget. Simulations and Claude Haiku 4.5 rollouts confirm this regime dependence: on GSM8K ($\hatρ=0.435$, $\hatθ=+0.96$) the gap is dormant, whereas on AIME ($\hatρ=0.480$, $\hatθ=-0.15$) the single-unit rule crosses on 24% of prompts at nominal $ε=0.05$, while the prompt-level procedure declines (0.00). This correctness result identifies which reliability claim an anytime-valid certificate actually supports under dependence.

Rigel-Ladon: An Option-Count Law for a One-Pass Decision Encoder, Tested on Held-Out Option Counts and Held-Out Schemas

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Laya is a small neural network that reads a text, a question and K written options, and returns one probability per option in a single pass. We ask how its accuracy falls as K grows from 2 to 35 and whether that can be predicted. We score nested option sets (every K-option question contains the smaller ones) on four question pools, for the released model (Base) and our fine-tune (FT), and score every item against each single rival. FT trained on one 12-option topic question (in-family); a 12-option HR (department-routing) question is out-of-family. Logit accuracy (the log of correct over incorrect answers) is linear in log(K-1) with slope 0.68 to 0.88. Fitted on K up to 6, the law predicts K=7 to 12 within 0.6 points and K up to 24 within 1.2 (a log-linear rule: 1.6 and 6.7). Taking the slope from the topic question and measuring only two-option accuracy on HR predicts its other K within 0.3 points (FT) and 0.7 (Base). On a 35-label pool of five schemas the law fails (5 to 11 points). Independence of irrelevant alternatives is rejected: the gold-versus-rival log-odds shifts by +0.24 to +2.07 nats per doubling of K, yet pairwise answers reproduce K-way accuracy within 4.7 points. Order averaging (averaging over several orderings of the options), shortlists, a temperature law and calibrated prediction sets did not transfer (FT's 90% sets cover 84% on HR). 2 of 10 predictions made in advance held. Caveats: one model family and LLM-labelled synthetic text.

HiSA: Sparsifying the Hidden Dimension of RoPE Attention in LLMs

📅 2026-11-19 16:31
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Attention in large language models (LLMs) scores every query against every key with a full dot product. We investigate whether this dot product must be computed completely for attention to be effective. We propose Hidden Sparse Attention (HiSA), which keeps the query and key projections intact but only computes a partial dot product, following a static, parameter-free rule set by the token positions: attention becomes sparse in the hidden (feature) dimension, an axis much less explored than token sparsity, rather than over tokens as in almost all sparse attention methods. Based on the structure of rotary position embeddings (RoPE), we decompose keys and queries into parts which can be omitted when computing attention. By training LLMs from scratch with HiSA, we find that this partial dot product does not hurt but improves performance compared with the complete product while computing only about a third of the query-key products. During inference HiSA improves decoding speed and shrinks memory footprint. We also extend HiSA to FlashAttention-3 kernels for training and inference. Finally, we motivate our approach by showing that dense attention learns to suppress the attention score parts that HiSA drops. Code is available at https://github.com/ambroise-b/hisa.

SGLayout: Schema-Guided Closed-Loop Refinement for LLM-Driven Parameterized Layout Generation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong potential in parameterized layout generation via tool-augmented code synthesis. However, LLMs lack spatial awareness, making one-shot generation unreliable. While layout quality can be evaluated with established techniques, a critical gap remains: geometric-domain evaluation outcomes cannot be directly translated into actionable code-level fixes, trapping iterative refinement in blind refactoring. We propose SGLayout, a self-refining framework that closes this gap via a schema-guided mechanism. Upon code execution, a structured schema captures the correspondence between each code-level instance and its geometric attributes, establishing a bidirectional link between code space and geometry space. Emulating engineer review practices, SGLayout evaluates layouts along two complementary dimensions: intent compliance through quantitative structural checking and qualitative vision-language assessment, and rule compliance through DRC. All identified violations are then back-projected through the schema onto precise code targets, enabling surgical repair. This closed-loop process iterates until layout convergence. On a curated benchmark of 112 parameterized layout generation tasks using GDSFactory, SGLayout achieves a 76.79% pass rate, compared with 50.89% for the strongest baseline.

COVERAGE-TRACE: Source-Side Counterfactual Tracing for Detecting Omissions in LLM Summaries

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Omissions are difficult to verify because missing information leaves no output span to inspect: a summary may be factually supported while still excluding a decision, qualification, quantity, or condition needed to preserve the source's meaning. We introduce COVERAGE-TRACE, a source-side counterfactual framework that evaluates omission as dependence on a designated atomic source fact. It replaces only the target fact with type-matched alternatives, holds the candidate summary fixed, and measures the resulting change in its teacher-forced, length-normalized likelihood. A summary covering the target should respond more strongly than one omitting it, while aggregation across multiple counterfactuals reduces sensitivity to replacement choice. We evaluate COVERAGE-TRACE on 80 matched covered--omitted QMSum pairs spanning several fact types and four open-weight models. Multi-CF achieves 0.764--0.825 AUROC, compared with 0.640 for the strongest evaluated text-only baseline. On Falcon-7B, targeted interventions achieve 0.779 AUROC, whereas shuffling counterfactual assignments reduces performance to 0.533; target deletion is effective while unrelated deletion is not. However, adjustment for meaning-preserving paraphrases reduces AUROC to 0.591--0.624. COVERAGE-TRACE therefore provides a practical, complementary signal of target-specific source--summary dependence, but not a wording-invariant semantic coverage probability. These results establish source-side intervention as a promising approach to omission detection while identifying semantic invariance as the central remaining challenge.

Toward a Science of AI Agent Societies

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents are rapidly evolving from isolated personal assistants into networked actors that interact with one another at scale. We envision the emergence of AI agent societies, with their own social and economic dynamics, as a new research frontier. We argue that AI agent societies should be studied as a distinct object of inquiry: neither simply larger collections of individual agents nor merely simulations of human society. To formalize this perspective, we propose four core properties that a valid AI agent society should satisfy: individualized objectives, rules and governance, autonomy, and scale and complexity. Building on this framework, we identify four classes of societal behaviors worth studying in AI agent societies: economic behaviors, behaviors under conflict-of-interest, unsafe and unethical behaviors, and system-level behaviors. We then outline key technical challenges--including property parameterization, parameter balancing, and robust implementation--and argue that progress on these challenges could enable scientifically informative and practically useful models of AI agent societies. Finally, we revisit existing multi-agent systems through the lens of the proposed core properties, show that they instantiate only subsets of them, and discuss implications for platform design, evaluation, and governance.

DARSY: A Tool for Data-Driven Abstraction and Policy Synthesis for Stochastic Systems with Unknown Dynamics

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

This paper presents DARSY: a software tool for abstraction-based verification and policy synthesis in discrete-time stochastic control systems with unknown dynamics and noise distributions. DARSY requires only a simulator of the system and a Lipschitz bound on its dynamics, and needs no knowledge of dynamics or noise distributions. The tool is fully automated and constructs a finite interval Markov decision process (IMDP) abstraction from sampled transitions, whose probability intervals are obtained via statistical techniques and Lipschitz reasoning. Robust dynamic programming on this IMDP then yields a policy together with a lower bound on the probability that the unknown system satisfies a desired specification. This lower bound holds with a user-specified confidence; that is, it is probably approximately correct (PAC). The tool is implemented in Python and supports general Lipschitz-continuous system dynamics and specifications expressed in syntactically co-safe LTL (scLTL), which includes reach-avoid and finite-horizon safety specifications as special cases. Beyond standard IMDPs, DARSY implements a two-layer IMDP (2IMDP) abstraction that also bounds the probability of transitioning to aggregated state blocks, yielding smaller abstraction errors than IMDP abstractions and, as a result, tighter satisfaction guarantees. We demonstrate DARSY's broad applicability across a variety of benchmarks and specifications.

SpecWM: Turning Verification into Refinement for Streaming World Models

📅 2026-11-19 14:50
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recently, video world models have advanced interactive generation, with large models offering higher quality and smaller models enabling faster inference. Existing speculative methods combine their strengths through fast draft generation and selective target regeneration guided by rewards on decoded frames. However, verification covers only the external scorer's evaluation dimensions and adds decoding and scoring costs. Moreover, these scores provide no correction, requiring costly target regeneration for rejected drafts. To address these limitations, we propose Speculative World Model (SpecWM), a unified, training-free inference framework for streaming world models. It verifies draft chunks using the target's latent velocity field. Verification also yields a reusable correction; two thresholds select Accept, Refine, or Reject. Specifically, one-step refinement reuses it to improve perceptual quality, while three-step refinement strengthens camera following and trajectory consistency. These findings motivate transition-aware refinement. On WBench Navigation, SpecWM improves visual quality at 1.63$\times$ speedup over the target and raises NavScore from the draft's 75.99 to 85.63, achieving the best quality-speed-control trade-off. Additionally, our LongSpecBench shows sustained quality and control over long, multi-turn rollouts, while experiments on Hy-Nomas validate SpecWM across backbones and denoising budgets.

World Models Favor Learned Priors over Visual Evidence

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models are increasingly used to simulate physical interactions, but training a separate model for each environment limits scalability. Cross-environment prediction requires identifying the current physical rule, which appearance alone may not reveal. We therefore study visual calibration: using object motion and interactions in a short video as direct evidence to infer the current rule and predict motion under that rule from a different initial state. However, differences in data availability across physical conditions can yield a long-tailed training distribution, biasing models toward common rules even when evidence supports rare ones. To study this challenge, we introduce TailWorld, which evaluates the transfer of demonstrated rules across initial states while independently controlling training imbalance and calibration informativeness. The benchmark compares predictions with two reference futures from the same prediction initial state, one following the demonstrated rule and the other a common rule, to measure prediction bias toward common dynamics. We further propose Rule Inference for Transfer (RIFT), which learns how different rules affect object motion, infers the current rule from calibration interactions, and applies it to prediction from a new initial state. RIFT explicitly controls the influence of training frequency on rule inference, allowing sufficiently informative observational evidence to support rare rules. Experiments on TailWorld show that RIFT improves prediction accuracy and physical consistency across varying training imbalances and distribution shifts, with gains in predicting rare dynamics.

Training Vision Models for LLM-Driven Weight-Space Programming

📅 2026-11-19 16:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

It has been demonstrated that, in certain settings, large language models can perform weight-space programming to directly modify the weights of neural networks. However, it is unknown how dedicated training of these networks can contribute to the successes of weight-space programming. We present a benchmark of LLM-driven weight-space programming on vision models, in which five LLMs edit convolutional networks and vision transformers on relabeling and error-correction tasks on Waterbirds, CIFAR-100, PACS and Entity-30.
