SHUSH: Emission-Aware Reward Design for Proactive Video Assistants

📅 2026-11-19 17:38
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Proactive video assistants must decide not only what to say, but also when to speak. Existing reinforcement-learning objectives can reward timely, correct responses without accounting for response volume, thereby encouraging unnecessary or repeated emissions. We identify this failure mode in MMDuet2, whose proactive area-under-the-curve (PAUC) reward promotes timely responses but does not price the number of emissions. We show analytically that this makes additional responses weakly penalized or effectively free, systematically encouraging over-emission. We then propose an emission-aware F1 reward for interval-valued annotations, using exclusive span--emission matching to price false emissions and missed spans. Under MMDuet2's training recipe, our policy on the visual subset of OmniPro replies 2.5x less often than MMDuet2 while raising timing F1 from 0.259 to 0.396 and content-aware F1 from 0.103 to 0.154; a more conservative variant cuts replies by 82.5% and raises precision from 0.161 to 0.418. On ProactiveVideoQA, the same reward adds replies where the baseline is nearly silent, and PAUC ranks a heavily over-emitting policy second despite near-last F1, because it is insensitive to response count. Our results indicate that proactive assistants should be trained and evaluated with objectives that price emissions.

MKA: Evaluating Multimodal Knowledge Agents on end-to-end professional tasks

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We introduce the Multimodal Knowledge Agent (MKA) benchmark, which measures how well autonomous agents perform end-to-end professional knowledge work that depends on visual understanding. Each task requires an agent to extract and combine information from multiple visually dense files, such as CAD blueprints, electronics schematics, radiographs, complex charts and geospatial maps, and to produce a standalone deliverable in .xlsx, .pptx, .pdf, .docx, .html or .png format. Existing benchmarks leave this capability largely untested: removing image-viewing tools leaves Gemini 4 Carbon's score on GDPval unchanged, and single-format evaluations such as GDP.pdf and GDP.xlsx do not require reasoning across files or modalities. MKA contains 200 tasks across 10 economic sectors. Deliverables are graded against expert-weighted rubrics by a sandboxed agentic autorater that agrees with human domain experts on 96.2% of criterion judgments in our expert-graded study (Cohen's $κ= 0.91$), scored by weighted satisfaction rate (WSR) overall and on frontier criteria unsaturated by prior-generation models (Climb-WSR). Across the frontier models we evaluate (Gemini 4 Carbon, Claude Fable 5.2, Claude Opus 5.5, and GPT-6 Astra), Claude Fable 5.2 (56.6% Climb-WSR, 73.3% Overall WSR) and Gemini 4 Carbon (54.3% Climb-WSR, 71.9% Overall WSR) achieve the highest scores, while Carbon uses $5.7\times$ fewer tokens. Even for the strongest models, Climb-WSR remains below 57%, leaving substantial headroom for multimodal agents.

Fast-JEPA: Advancing the Success-Latency Frontier for Latent World Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Latent world models predict the outcomes of action sequences, but comparing candidate plans through recursive rollout remains expensive at test time. We introduce Fast-JEPA, a latent world model planning framework that advances the success-latency Pareto frontier of predictive search. Fast-JEPA reorganizes predictive computation to support effective action-sequence search at low planning cost. It constructs a goal-directed plan to guide search and directly predicts the outcomes of complete candidates for efficient comparison, avoiding recursive state rollout during refinement. Across PushT, Reacher, Cube, and TwoRoom, Fast-JEPA achieves 96.50% average success rate with 2.41x faster planning than INTACT. Search-budget sweeps show that its competitive success and low latency extend across candidate counts and refinement depths. We further introduce DynaGoal, a benchmark for evaluating world-model planning under dynamic out-of-distribution conditions, execution deadlines, and controlled response delays. Fast-JEPA achieves average success rates of 73.83% on DynaGoal-Shift and 87.67% on DynaGoal-Deadline, demonstrating the practical value of low-latency predictive planning under changing control conditions and execution-time constraints. Our code is available at https://github.com/Sofilyzia/Fast-JEPA.

Diagnosis is not a policy: a state-transition audit of caoability-conditioned test-time inference

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Test-time actions can satisfy retrieval, state-realization, program-consistency, or rendering objectives without correcting an answer. Test-time Error Response and Realizability Audit (TERRA) is an evaluation/auditing protocol, not a new inference algorithm. It uses a unified decomposition of intervention outcomes into applicability, target-state realization, endpoint translation, harm, and resource/inference cost. Paired comparisons fix the incoming state and evaluation conditions; cross-model analyses vary the receiving configuration. Executable financial reasoning experiments yield three findings: the same context expansion can harm one configuration and benefit another; sufficient evidence does not ensure correctness because configurations differ in evidence-to-answer translation; and diagnostic headroom---accuracy attainable with idealized diagnostic information over the measured baseline---plus local consistency does not ensure practical gains. Action value is $V(a\mid x,m)$, not diagnosis alone; diagnosis is not a deployable policy $π(a\mid x,m)$.

Towards Autoregressive Neural Network Parameter Generation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently established a new paradigm in machine learning, demonstrating exceptional zero-shot generalization and complex reasoning capabilities. Concurrently, neural network parameter generation has emerged as a promising avenue for rapid task adaptation and model synthesis. Existing weight generation techniques, however, struggle to jointly learn the distribution of diverse models, to encode heterogeneous architectures within a single generator, and to generalize out of distribution. We therefore ask whether pretrained parameters can be encoded into a discrete domain so that the zero-shot generalization LLMs exhibit in text generation can be transferred to weight generation. Following this view, we propose an LLM-inspired approach, Instruction-Guided Parameter Generation (IGPG), that formulates network parameter generation as an autoregressive modeling problem. We first quantize pretrained weights with a VQ-VAE, then train a prefix-conditioned autoregressive generator over the resulting tokens. At inference, the generated weight tokens are mapped back to parameter space by the VQ-VAE decoder. We evaluate the framework across in-distribution generation, cross-dataset transfer, and cross-architecture settings. The proposed method achieves competitive performance in in-distribution generation and provides useful parameter initializations for held-out datasets and architectural configurations, improving downstream performance over random initialization after adaptation. These results suggest that discrete autoregressive modeling provides a promising framework for learning and transferring structure across collections of pretrained neural network parameters.

dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement

📅 2026-11-19 15:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diffusion language models (DLMs) support parallel generation, yet they often require many denoising steps because only a fraction of token predictions are sufficiently reliable to be committed at each step. Existing acceleration methods primarily optimize confidence learning or commitment policies. However, these approaches operate on the outputs of a single denoising forward pass, in which information from the prompt, previously committed tokens, and uncommitted positions may not yet be fully integrated. Consequently, some predictions remain under-refined when the commitment decision is made, and modifying the commitment policy alone cannot improve their underlying representations. We introduce dLoop, which restructures a pretrained diffusion language model into a Prelude-Loop-Coda architecture and recurrently applies a parameter-shared intermediate block to the same partially masked state. Rather than advancing the denoising process, each additional loop provides further latent computation, allowing information distributed across uncommitted positions to continue interacting and become more fully integrated before commitment. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval show that dLoop achieves the highest or tied-highest accuracy on every benchmark. With tokens per forward pass (TPF) adjusted for loop computation, it outperforms Fast-dLLM in both accuracy and TPF on all four benchmarks and dParallel on three, and is more accurate than d3LLM on all four, while d3LLM attains higher TPF; no compared method surpasses dLoop in both. Mechanistic analyses show that extra loops mainly help on slow, unstable trajectories and that updates at uncommitted positions drive changes in token decisions and later generation. Pre-commitment latent computation thus makes predictions more reliable, offering a route to DLM acceleration distinct from forcing confidence through the training loss.

LLM GerryMandering: Separating Interactions from Additive Effects in LLM Audits

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Intersectional bias arises when a model treats people who hold several marginalised identities at once differently from what their separate identities would predict. Auditing such intersectional subgroups remains a challenge: their number grows quadratically, and there is no agreed baseline for what independent attributes would predict. Without such a baseline, many existing audits flag a subgroup when it exceeds each attribute alone, a condition that attributes acting independently already satisfy. We test this standard criterion on 150 synthetic datasets built with no interaction. With the shuffle null, it flags 66.1 false subgroups per dataset on average, and at least one in every dataset. When we plant interactions of known size into these datasets, a repaired null lets the criterion recover only 32% of them. We introduce ADDIS (Additive Intersectional Screen), which fits an additive model over all attributes, scores each subgroup by its Additive Residual Distance (ARD) from that model's prediction, and tests it against a Freedman-Lane null. On our synthetic benchmark, ADDIS flags 0.09 false subgroups per dataset and recovers 84% of the same planted interactions. We then build IndiPersona, 10,000 synthetic Indian personas with six identity attributes, and audit 13 open language models. The standard criterion flags 7.1% of the 738 tested subgroups on average; ADDIS flags 3.0%. Gender identity crossed with sexual orientation is the largest pairing in every model, and counterfactual personas confirm it and additionally reveal a caste x class interaction in all but one model that the observational test lacked the data to detect. Much of what audits report as intersectional bias can thus arise from independent single-attribute effects alone, yet a small core of interactions survives.

SkillBridge: Cost-Aware Diagnosis and Repair of Cross-OS Compatibility Defects in Agent Skills

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

LLM agents increasingly reuse executable capabilities through skills, but a skill that works on one OS may fail on another because OS assumptions are spread across guide text, scripts, metadata, install contracts, and local entrypoints. Diagnosing such failures by running every skill on every OS is expensive: many executions spend model tokens, runner time, and review effort before revealing whether the failure is truly OS-related. We present SkillBridge, a contract-based workflow with three stages: static analysis, dynamic validation, and repair. SkillBridge first screens 3,270 skill-target-OS cases, covering 1,090 skills across Linux, macOS, and Windows, to find OS-sensitive contract evidence. It then runs dynamic tasks and root-cause review on selected cases to confirm whether a failure is caused by an OS assumption, and repairs the contract field that caused the failure, such as guide text, package scripts, dependencies, or platform declarations. Repair raises the reviewer-judged target-task pass rate from 30.6% to 38.4%, a gain of 7.8 percentage points. A retrospective Linux/macOS budget analysis further shows the efficiency effect: SkillBridge reaches 38 confirmed OS issues with 185 dynamic validations, where uniform random selection would require about 865 validations in expectation.

Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation

📅 2026-11-19 17:15
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.

Collision-Free Is Not Enough: Passage-Conditioned Navigation for Wheeled-Legged Robots

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A collision-free detour can still bring a legged robot to the next stair or ledge a few centimetres or degrees off, and a fixed locomotion skill, factory or learned, can stall or fall. Existing methods prevent this with a robot model or extensive trial and error, but on a new site with unknown skills neither is available, so they cannot be used. We find that a single 27-minute teaching session already supplies what these methods have to derive or learn: for every stair and ledge, one crossing the skill has completed. Our method, passage-conditioned navigation, makes the route remember how each transition was entered, not only where the path went. It stores each taught crossing as an event and, without training or a robot model, enforces it after every local replan: before each ledge, an entry gate makes the robot turn or back up and retry if it is more than 10 cm or 10° off the taught crossing. Under localization noise, with path, gait schedule, and sensing unchanged, this raises the share of disturbed ledge crossings that stay within these limits from 24.1% to 88.6%. In contact simulation with our learned policies, a robot pushed off course before stairs never falls in 180 climbs, against 9 falls when the stored direction is ignored; pushed 1.2 m away, it rejoins the route, ending on average 0.14 m from it (1.10 m without rejoining), and finishes as often as an undisturbed robot. This suits any robot with fixed skills that repeats a route over terrain transitions, as in inspection and patrol, where one wrong entry can end the task. We deployed it in competition on a real wheeled-legged robot: over 271 m of multi-obstacle terrain spanning 7.0 m of elevation, it completed all six runs, won third place at the 2026 Global Open-source AI Challenge, which drew 2,999 submissions, and was the only finalist in its task to finish fully autonomously.

Awaken-HI: Scaling Humanoid Intelligence with Whole-Body Human Experience

📅 2026-11-19 17:03
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. Existing egocentric video datasets cover many of these activities but provide little synchronized whole-body motion data for learning loco-manipulation at scale. We build Awaken-Human, which pairs approximately 50,000 hours of egocentric stereo video with synchronized whole-body human motion and covers more than 100 skills. Building on this, we also introduce Awaken-HI (Humanoid Intelligence), a vision-language-action model for humanoid loco-manipulation that jointly predicts original human motion and retargeted robot actions through a shared action expert. We show that loco-manipulation skills transfer more effectively from people to humanoids when human trajectories directly supervise the shared action expert. Increasing human pretraining data reduces held-out robot action prediction error according to an empirical scaling law and raises the mean success rate on downstream real-robot tasks. After full-corpus pretraining and T800 adaptation, Awaken-HI achieves a mean success rate of 93.75% across eight individual tasks and outperforms GR00T N1.7 and $π_{0.5}$ in both individual and long-horizon evaluations. The same policy generalizes to objects unseen during robot post-training and to new pairings of familiar objects and destinations.

Closing the Analyze-to-Design Gap: An Authorization-Bounded Agent for Self-Driving Laboratories

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Artificial intelligence (AI) agents are increasingly integrated into self-driving laboratories (SDLs) to plan experiments and interpret results. However, analytical reasoning alone does not make a laboratory autonomous: autonomy in an SDL is achievable only when the agent can be trusted to act on physical hardware unsupervised and knows when to defer to a human. Here, we present an architecture that integrates an agent at the Analyze-Design interface of the Design-Make-Test-Analyze (DMTA) cycle, enabling agent-driven measurement selection and data interpretation while ensuring that state-changing operations are authorized, validated, and auditable. We demonstrate this architecture on a liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC-MS) instrument in an oxidant-screening campaign for synthesizing an organic dye. Within human-defined campaign limits, the agent submitted measurement jobs, interpreted mass spectrometry (MS) and diode-array detection (DAD) data, flagged failed experiments, and produced an oxidant ranking. This ranking matched that obtained from scripted peak integration, and organic chemists confirmed the agent's spectral interpretations and failure assignments. In a simulated environment that replays the archived LC-MS data, where the agent chose which wells to inject under varying injection limits, it measured the highest-yielding condition at about its seventh injection, against 14.5 on average for a random order, and identified it in every campaign with a limit of eight or more injections. An agent can therefore close the analysis-to-design gap while its actions stay bounded and verifiable, which is a step toward greater autonomy for SDLs.

Game-Theoretic Data Allocation for Expert Specialization and Routing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Can the allocation used to train experts also predict which expert will perform best on a prompt? Such a connection would make the intended division of labor inspectable before training and allow the allocation itself to guide inference. We introduce InflEns, a mixture of LoRA experts whose training weights and inference routes come from the same location game in a fixed prompt-trait space. Players choose positions to maximize their expected shares of the prompt distribution, without using expert losses. We utilize the game's kernel-general theoretical criterion for when co-located positions become locally unstable to separation. We evaluate InflEns on a corpus assembled from seven safety-related benchmarks, which demonstrates that the highest-share expert has the lowest held-out response negative log-likelihood (NLL) on approximately 90% of prompts across four kernels under a sharpened simplex representation, compared with approximately 15% after permuting allocations across prompts. We demonstrate that the method performs comparably to published learned gates without having to train an explicit gate on the experts. Because the router is fixed, we can trace harmful behavior to a single expert and retrain only that expert, which lowers HarmBench attack success from 0.51 to 0.02 while keeping instruction following. The game also gives us several controls over how the experts specialize, including the kernel choice and its parameters, the trait-space geometry, and the number of experts. Importantly, InflEns is a general framework that can be extended to any encoder and data distribution.

Client Exits - A Way to Increase LLM Transparency on the Client Side

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Textual recommendations generated by large language models (LLMs) can be difficult to inspect. This paper introduces \emph{Client Exits}, a lightweight mechanism for enriching LLM responses while separating semantic intent from client-specific presentation. The model inserts predefined, machine-interpretable markers into its output, and the receiving client decides whether and how to realize the corresponding enrichments. This allows supplementary information to be presented as graphics, text, speech, or other modalities without prescribing a concrete interface. We implement a prototype combining a finetuned Qwen model with a modified LibreChat client. Using the Question--Option--Criteria (QOC) approach including three XML-based client exits. We evaluate two QOC-oriented model variants trained on essentially the same examples, with client-exit annotations added for one variant. Across 100 test questions, the client-exit model includes all three markers in every response. An LLM judge, informed about the mechanism without being instructed to favor it, prefers this model's answers in 66\% of the comparisons. These results demonstrate successful marker generation on the evaluated test set and support the feasibility of client-controlled enrichment.

FastRoverVLA: Fast Vision-Language-Action Execution for Off-Road Autonomous Driving

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Off-road vision-language-action (VLA) models generate scene-conditioned plans, but frequent trajectory updates can repeatedly invoke expensive multimodal inference. Reusing a prediction reduces this demand while leaving its commands exposed to changes in vehicle motion. FastRoverVLA addresses this mismatch by decoupling visual plan generation from motion-conditioned execution. Its training-free interface maintains decoded plans between visual predictions. A speed-curvature action buffer is aligned with measured motion at each update, integrated into a local trajectory, and advanced by consuming the current action. The corrected remainder is written back to the buffer, preserving motion corrections across successive updates. A bounded refresh rule normalizes speed and curvature variations within a newly decoded plan and assigns a short or long reuse budget according to the resulting plan-roughness score. The interface requires neither backbone features nor an additional learned driving policy. Over a 3 s horizon, independent real-request evaluation achieves average displacement error (ADE) and final displacement error (FDE) of 0.328/0.596 m on ORAD-3D and 0.266/0.526 m on RELLIS-3D, reducing FDE by 18.0% and 36.7% at request rates of 6.0% and 4.8%, respectively. These runs retain lower errors across four backbones and both datasets and reduce Qwen3-VL request-processing cost by factors of 16.6 and 20.8 under serial replay, while mechanism and motion-only controls identify persistent state correction as the main source of the observed accuracy benefit. The source code and evaluation materials are publicly available at https://github.com/ADEPT-ORG/FastRoverVLA.

SailSearch: Agent-Assisted Discovery in Combinatorial Optimization

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present SailSearch, a human-guided workflow for algorithmic research with two language-model agents, and report results on Market Split and maximum independent set (MIS). For Market Split, our randomized algorithm searches across multiple lattice bases, dividing computation between enumeration and continued basis reduction. Across ten random seeds per instance, it found the first reported CPU-only solutions to 18 of 33 previously open QOBLIB instances, solving 130 to 133 of the 156 benchmark instances per seed with a 24-hour cutoff on two CPU cores. For MIS, our generator encodes planted XOR constraints as graphs with certified optima; on seven instances with 1,752 to 3,996 vertices, the best solutions returned by the tested solvers in eight-hour runs were 5 to 15 vertices below the optimum.

Ask in the Crowd: Differentially Private LLM Inference via Dummy-Augmented Shuffling

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models are increasingly deployed as LLM-as-a-Service (LLMaaS), where user queries are sent to remote LLMs for inference, raising severe privacy concerns about the exposure of sensitive user inputs. Differentially private inference mitigates these risks by injecting noise into input embeddings. However, existing methods struggle to balance privacy and utility, as stronger privacy protection often leads to substantial accuracy degradation in LLM inference. In this work, we propose DP-SPD, a differentially private LLM inference framework that protects user queries by hiding the real query among client-generated dummy queries. All queries are randomly shuffled before being sent to the server, preventing direct identification of the user's true intent. To improve inference efficiency, we exploit the shared-prefix structure among queries and reuse transformer key--value (KV) caches during decoding. We analyze the privacy guarantee of DP-SPD and evaluate its utility on classification and generation tasks, as well as its privacy protection under QIA, EIA, and GPT-based attacks. Experimental results show that our method achieves strong inference-time privacy protection and near-plaintext utility while improving inference efficiency through shared-prefix KV-cache reuse.

A new algorithmic approach for finding flat minima: critically damped Polyak stepsizes

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present a new adaptive stepsize rule that biases gradient descent toward flatter minima in both theory and practice, and establish the first uniform-in-time guarantees for both sharpness and loss reduction for a broad class of locally quadratic objectives. Our critically damped Polyak (CDP) stepsizes multiply the classical Polyak rule by $4$ and a mild damping factor. The factor $4$ is critical in that it produces equioscillations around the manifold of minimizers $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order. Damping provably drives convergence to $\mathcal{M}$. CDP thus operates at the edge of stability by adapting its stepsizes to local curvature. Once the loss is small, its projected iterates follow gradient descent on $\log{λ_1(\nabla^2 \mathcal{L})}$ along $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order, paralleling the flatness bias of normalized gradient descent (Arora et al., 2022). Unlike normalized gradient descent, CDP step lengths shrink with the distance to $\mathcal{M}$, enabling convergence to an interpolating solution that locally minimizes sharpness at finite learning rate. We establish our theory for a broad class of non-negative losses with locally quadratic Morse-Bott structure near $\mathcal{M}$, capturing regular interpolation regimes in smooth overparameterized neural networks. Empirically, CDP stepsizes produce a flattening effect similar to that of Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM, Foret et al., 2021), reducing sharpness even in settings beyond our theory, including nonlinear teacher-student networks and fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs.

WeightCode: Installing New Capabilities into Neural Networks via Weight-Space Programming

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Neural networks encode learned capabilities in their weights, yet extending those capabilities with explicit task knowledge remains a fundamental challenge. Here we show that Weight-Space Programming (WSP), in which executable programs read, construct or modify a network's weights directly, can install new capabilities within trained neural networks. WeightCode, a WSP framework, uses a frozen language model to write executable weight programs that install new computations into trained neural networks using task knowledge and their existing weights, without gradient-based training. Once installed, the new capabilities run independently of the language model. Given a grammar or eight demonstrations, weight programs enable previously unseen command compositions (0% to 99.8% exact match) and a new pickup action (0% to 98.4% success), while preserving the original command and navigation capabilities. On ImageNet, four weight programs achieve net gains of 8.84-17.62 points while preserving clean accuracy, outperforming the best of eight fine-tuning methods under matched examples, checks, and retention constraints. In a Vision Transformer, causal interventions show that installed noise-correction gates drive robustness gains, while their selective activation preserves clean accuracy. Rerun on five independently trained Vision Transformers without further language model calls, ten weight programs retain 83-110% of their source-network gains on average, whereas copying their fixed weight edits yields negligible gains. Six programs written for an already edited network add 5.23-14.50 points of contrast robustness while retaining the previously installed noise gain. Together, these results establish WSP as a new route to acquiring and accumulating capabilities in trained neural networks through reusable weight programs.

Cost-Aware Agentic Feature Optimization System for Fleet-Scale Ranking Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large Web platforms operate large fleets of ranking models, each selecting features from a shared pool of tens of thousands. We present an end-to-end agentic system that optimizes features across the fleet under resource constraints shared across models and those set per model, such as feature pipeline cost and serving throughput (QPS), respectively. In this system, LLM agents propose hypotheses, and deterministic tools execute experiments and verify them. For each model, an LLM generates and shortlists cross features from a combinatorial candidate space, and all features are then ranked by several importance methods. Feature blending mixes these rankings into a single selection, using Pareto fronts across tasks for multi-task models. An LLM Researcher agent then tunes the feature selection in 10--25 asynchronous trials under resource caps, and a trimming step keeps the pipeline cost within budget. Across production models, the system improves normalized entropy by 0.31% on average within each model's budget, and the trimming step cuts pipeline cost by 2.3% with no regression beyond measurement noise. The optimized models were launched, and in online A/B tests they improved a primary online metric by 0.20%.

Raptor: Rejection-aware Adaptive Tree based on Speculative Decoding

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Tree-structured speculative decoding accelerates vision-language models (VLMs) by verifying multiple candidate continuations in parallel. However, long visual contexts increase the cost of speculative computation, making candidate coverage and redundant expansion important considerations beyond tree topology adaptation. We propose Raptor, a training-free framework that uses rejection feedback to jointly regulate candidate coverage and drafting effort. Our key insight is that acceptance length alone cannot distinguish insufficient candidate coverage from excessive deep expansion, and these conditions require different allocation decisions. Raptor therefore extracts complementary rejection-aware signals to characterize speculative progress, frontier coverage, and redundant expansion. These signals jointly guide bounded, momentum-smoothed updates of tree depth, branching width, and drafting budget, together with execution control that limits unnecessary drafting and progressively restores expansion when appropriate. Extensive experiments with two VLMs across seven multimodal benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of Raptor, achieving up to 2.39x average speedup over autoregressive decoding.

Probing the "Conscious" Mind: Selective Access and Global Workspace in Vision-Language Models

📅 2026-11-19 18:46
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language models (VLMs) integrate visual and textual information, but their outputs do not reveal internal concept representations. Global workspace theory (GWT) proposes that information is selected from parallel brain processes, integrated in a shared workspace, and made available to specialised processes. We introduce the Multimodal Concept Lens (MC-Lens), a multimodal Jacobian lens, to study these functions in VLM concept processing. Its vision encoder and language decoder lenses map intermediate states to a shared semantic space defined by the decoder's output vocabulary to compare concept accessibility across modalities. MC-Lens supports internal concept interventions to examine the role of accessed concepts in downstream computation. (i) Accessibility and selective access. Multimodal concept information is readable in intermediate states and selectively accessible as tasks require. We extend "readout carriers" from one name token to a token set to better capture and express concept information and support counterfactual experiments. (ii) Concepts in downstream computation. Counterfactual experiments show that accessed concept information participates in downstream computation and reasoning. (iii) Development of cross-modal alignment and selective access. We develop two J-space metrics tracking the development of cross-modal concept alignment more clearly than hidden state cosine in controlled VLM training. The experiment also shows that reliable selective access develops later than concept accessibility. (iv) Shared functions across models. Across VLM families and sizes, we find shared patterns of concept accessibility, query-dependent selectivity and cross-task intervention effects despite differences in internal organisation. These findings offer an experimental basis to study GWT in multimodal artificial systems and how it can guide cross-modal alignment improvements.

Erasing the Wrong Thing: Conditional Attribute Leakage and Concept Erasure in Shared Foundation-Model Embeddings

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Medical-image embeddings are increasingly shared in place of raw images for authorized downstream tasks, yet they may reveal sensitive attributes. Standard concept-erasure methods target population-level guardedness. However, we prove that, when binary task and sensitive labels are associated, global and task-conditional linear guardedness cannot both hold while a nonzero linear task signal is preserved, so global erasure must either eliminate that signal or retain task-conditional linear leakage. We further show how global erasure affects linear readers' discrimination between positive and negative samples with different sensitive attributes, deriving an approximate closed-form prediction of its utility cost from pre-erasure statistics. Motivated by this conflict, we introduce Task-Conditional LEACE (TC-LEACE), a closed-form variant of LEACE that enforces linear guardedness within each task stratum of its fitting data. We evaluate four vision foundation models on 85 CelebA attribute pairs spanning a broad range of associations. After LEACE, conditional leakage persists across all four encoders, the discrepancy between marginal and conditional audits increases with association strength, and predicted utility costs track observed losses. We then examine hospital chest radiographs using three foundation models, including two biomedical models, in which chest-tube status is strongly associated with diagnosis. Although a marginal attack on LEACE-erased embeddings underperforms a diagnosis-only reference, chest-tube status remains detectable within diagnosis strata. TC-LEACE recovers most of LEACE's linear task-utility loss while keeping task-conditional leakage within a prespecified margin of LEACE's.

GRODE-RTL: Source-Mapped Dependency Views for LLM-Based RTL Repair

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large Language Models show great potential for automated Register-Transfer Level repair, yet they struggle with non-local faults where root causes and failing outputs reside far apart in source code. Treating RTL as a flat token sequence obscures hardware dataflows, whereas exposing complete synthesized graphs overwhelms the model with fine-grained metadata. To resolve this representation dilemma, we propose GRODE-RTL, an anchor-centric representation that extracts the single-clock-stage fan-in cone of a suspect register from the netlist, maps netlist nodes back to editable source lines, and organizes them into a compact hop-partitioned dependency view. By prioritizing direct drivers and folding distant hops, GRODE-RTL guides standard tool-using agents without exposing ground-truth hints or oracle feedback. We evaluate GRODE-RTL across 30 formally validated non-local faults using three LLMs (DeepSeek-V4-Flash, GLM-5.2, and MiniMax-M3) alongside a controlled six-configuration ablation study. GRODE-RTL improves pass@1 over source-level exploration by 16.7% on DeepSeek, by 26.7% on GLM-5.2, and by 20.0% on MiniMax-M3, with consistent gains on the hard-to-retrieve far-distance and indirect-anchor subsets of all three models. Our controls isolate structural relevance from generic context expansion, proving that selective dependency organization rather than full graph exposure is key to effective RTL repair.

SteerTrace: Tracing How Target Attacks Steer World Action Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World action models (WAMs) predict future videos and actions with one backbone, and their imagined future is often seen as a safety signal. We introduce SteerTrace, a targeted universal attack on WAMs that also traces its effect inside the model. One $\ell_\infty$-bounded (4/255) perturbation per model, shared across frames, tasks, and camera views, pushes the executed action toward an attacker-chosen target. On eight WAMs from four families (FastWAM, Faster-WAM, OpenWAM and LingBot-VA) on LIBERO and RoboTwin, it drives task success close to zero and moves the attacked action toward the target. Same-budget noise and BadWAM produce little action shift in the target direction. We use the signed targeted effect to measure how much of the effect each intervention removes or reproduces. Under the standard attack, restoring future tokens removes 61-94% of the targeted effect in the five models whose action experts attend to them. When the attack is constrained to keep the predicted future close to the clean prediction, the targeted effect can become more dependent on current-frame tokens. In three of these five models, restoring current-frame tokens removes 87-99% of the effect, compared with 9-15% for future tokens. In six models, restoring just 0.36% of the FFN neurons in layers 10-29 of the video expert removes 72-96% of the targeted effect. Residual directions 2-16 hold only 11-18% of the shift energy, yet removing them cancels most of the targeted effect.

Harnessing Medical Visual-Language Models with Agentic Evidence Management

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.

Twice-Told Critiques: Claim-Level Homogenization of Peer Review in the LLM Era

📅 2026-11-19 15:45
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Peer review relies on several readers finding different problems. We measure critique overlap in conference and journal reviews. At ICLR, measured overlap rose from 12.0% in 2026 to 21.3% in 2027, in reviews from the same stage of the review process, with year-on-year rises at NeurIPS, ICML and the F1000 and PLOS journals. A third ICLR review of a paper added 5.46 new problems to the other two in 2027, against 6.12 in 2026. We apply MELD, a detector of LLM-generated text, to all reviews. Within a paper, two reviews that MELD flags overlap more than two unflagged reviews: by 3.0 points at ICLR in 2024-2025, by 4.5 points in 2026-2027, and by 5.4 points across five journal sources. These results provide evidence that LLM-flagged reviews of one paper are less distinct from each other than unflagged reviews, and suggest that the diversity of critiques among reviews of a single paper is falling.

Measuring Hallucination in Large Language Models for Cyber Threat Intelligence: An Exploratory Study

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in cyber threat intelligence (CTI) tasks, such as summarization and question answering. However, they may produce hallucinations, false or misleading content not grounded in the source text, which can mislead analysts and corrupt downstream security decisions. This paper presents an exploratory measurement study of hallucinations in LLM-based CTI. We introduce HalluVision, a reproducible framework that generates LLM outputs, extracts domain-specific entities using a fine-tuned named entity recognition model, and evaluates factual consistency using multiple similarity metrics. Using 4,940 real-world cybersecurity articles, we measure hallucinations in outputs from two commercial LLMs and one open-weight LLM. Measurements are validated against 100 human-annotated outputs and two established faithfulness detectors. Results show that semantic similarity aligns with human judgments, whereas entity-based metrics exhibit limited agreement. Differences between models are concentrated in question answering, while entity extraction quality limits what entity-level metrics can reveal.

FLUID: Flexible, Evidence-Grounded Situated Task Guidance

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Real-time task guidance is inherently flexible: when an agent guides a user through a task, multiple responses and timings can be appropriate, and sometimes the best action is to remain silent. Existing benchmarks often miss this flexibility by treating synthesized dialogues as fixed targets for what to say and when to say it. We introduce FLUID (Flexible, Evidence-Grounded Situated Task Guidance), a unified framework for modeling, evaluating, and enabling flexible interaction in multimodal task guidance. Using naturally occurring human-human guidance from WTaG and HoloAssist, FLUID grounds interventions in their supporting evidence, necessity, and timing flexibility, and uses this representation both to evaluate flexible guidance and to build a real-time agent for task guidance. Our agent achieves the strongest overall balance of response correctness, intervention precision, timely guidance, and response latency among evaluated systems, while still leaving room for improvement in avoiding speech overlap. Together, FLUID moves situated task guidance beyond rigid reference matching toward more natural and adaptive human-AI interaction. Project page: https://fluid-guidance.github.io.

Coordinated Use of Large Language Models in Academic Research

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

This study proposes a methodological framework for the coordinated use of multiple large language models in academic research. Its central argument is that involving more models does not in itself guarantee more reliable results; what matters is how cognitive and verification operations are organised. The framework is based on seven principles (procedural independence, functional allocation, the diagnostic value of divergence, the diagnostic value of convergence, external verification, proportionality of checking, and human epistemic responsibility) and sets out a non-linear multi-LLM protocol in which independently obtained results are compared, critically examined and, where necessary, verified against external sources. The approach is illustrated through real-world cases drawn from research practice. The study concludes that moving from a single language model to a coordinated multi-LLM process represents a change in the organisation of research, in which the researcher makes the final decisions and bears scholarly responsibility.

Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

Intelligent Embedding Pruning for Large-Scale Recommendation Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Modern recommendation systems face substantial memory pressure, largely from the sparse embedding layer that maps high-cardinality categorical features into dense vectors. In-training embedding pruning (ITEP) removes less important embedding rows during training, yielding end-to-end memory savings but sometimes degrading accuracy because per-table pruning ratios are set by size-based heuristics. We use feature importance (FI) to guide these ratios (FI-ITEP), assigning higher pruning to tables tied to less important features. Across three large-scale recommendation models, FI-ITEP achieves 20--40% total memory reduction while matching or improving accuracy relative to percentile-based ITEP. Because the feature-importance signal requires minimal resources to compute, these memory savings translate into concrete systems benefits: the same models train on fewer GPU trainers (e.g., 112 to 80) with higher per-GPU throughput, saving 1,000+ GPU-hours per model launch. We reproduce these trends on the open-source DLRM (Criteo 1TB) and quantify the end-to-end training-time and GPU-cost tradeoff---including the burden of computing FI---offering practical guidance for embedding pruning in real-world systems.

Online Mistake Detection in Instruction-Conditioned Procedural Video

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

An assistant monitoring a person performing a procedure is most useful when it can detect mistakes while there is still time to recover. This requires judging actions against task instructions as the video unfolds, identifying the attempted step, locating it in time, and assessing its execution. Existing online detectors do not use the instruction text, while instruction-conditioned methods operate offline. In this paper, we study online, instruction-conditioned mistake detection in egocentric videos. Using only observed video, task instructions, and prior predictions, our model identifies and localizes completed action attempts and classifies them as correct, modified, slip, corrections, or additions; omitted steps are determined after the video ends. We compare a modular pipeline, with separate step identification, temporal localization, and execution verification, to a joint model that predicts all outputs in a single step. To support supervised learning from mistakes, we repartition EgoPER to include erroneous videos in all splits. The approaches achieve similar step-segmentation F1@0.5 (93.6% and 92.1%), while the joint model achieves higher binary mistake-detection F1@0.5 (84.1% vs. 80.7%), with its largest gain on slips (56.4% vs. 37.5%). Component-level analysis suggests that error propagation between stages contributes to the modular approach's lower detection score. On test videos containing mistakes, both designs outperform released AEM and ESTANet checkpoints under our evaluation protocol.

ForeSeek: Foresight-Driven Reasoning for Long-Form Video Understanding

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Long-form video understanding, as a fundamental yet crucial vision task, is being revolutionized by multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based video agents. However, the retrieval-driven nature inherent to these agents creates a significant bottleneck, forcing them to reactively fetch clips based on local saliency without a global temporal plan. To address this, we present ForeSeek, a foresight-driven video agent that transforms long-video reasoning from reactive retrieval into predictive exploration. Rather than blindly probing the timeline, ForeSeek forms visually grounded expectations over unseen temporal regions, verifies them through targeted observations, and continuously refines its search based on emerging evidence, where failed expectations serve as informative signals for principled backtracking. Furthermore, we develop a two-stage training framework that equips MLLMs with foresight-guided reasoning and efficient decision making. Extensive experiments on five long-video benchmarks demonstrate that ForeSeek consistently improves the efficiency--accuracy trade-off of long-video reasoning, achieving competitive or superior accuracy while observing $2.5$--$4.4\times$ fewer frames than the strongest search-based agent. Code is available at https://github.com/harry0021/ForeSeek

LINEAGE: A Benchmark for Empirical Lineage Retrieval in AI Scientific Literature

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Current research agents are moving beyond academic search toward literature synthesis and experiment planning. These workflows increasingly rely on literature retrieval systems, which serve as standalone systems or as components of research agents. However, existing scientific literature retrieval benchmarks mainly evaluate these methods using topical relevance or citation links as supervision, which cannot capture which prior methods are used for empirical comparison. To address this gap, we introduce Lineage, a benchmark for empirical lineage retrieval in AI scientific literature. An empirical lineage relation links a prior paper to a source paper that explicitly uses its method as an empirical comparison baseline. These relations form lineage graphs whose multi-hop neighborhoods provide retrieval ground truth for structural evaluation. To construct these graphs at scale, we develop Lineage-Constructor, which converts heterogeneous paper records and full-paper empirical baseline evidence into calibrated lineage graphs. Built from 121,048 papers across 17 top AI venues from 2018 to 2025, Lineage contains 3,950 graph components, 67,502 paper nodes, 164,948 lineage edges, and 80,000 multi-facet retrieval queries. Experiments with 19 representative retrieval systems show that empirical lineage retrieval remains challenging. Performance drops sharply with lineage distance, while structural metrics reveal that isolated retrieval hits often fail to recover closely connected empirical comparison neighborhoods.

Energy estimates of open-weight large language models across stated food weights: a dose-response evaluation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We tested whether energy estimates of open-weight large language models (LLMs) scale proportionally with an exactly stated food weight, and whether computing energy density times weight removes the error. Under a prespecified protocol, seven open-weight LLMs estimated the energy of 168 randomly drawn foods from the USDA Food and Nutrient Database for Dietary Studies at nine stated weights, from 0.25 to 4 times the default amount. The primary outcome was the elasticity of the estimate with respect to weight (1: proportional scaling). A two-step estimate multiplied each model's own stated energy density by the weight in code. All seven models compressed their estimates, with elasticities from 0.548 to 0.979 (every 95% CI below 1); four compressed severely (0.548 to 0.709). These four overestimated a quarter of the default amount by 47% to 130% and underestimated four times it by 20% to 34%. Supplying the density restored near-proportional scaling in three of the four, and the two-step estimate cut the typical error factor at these extremes from 1.47-2.43 to 1.20-1.38. Compression persisted at full precision and grew in five of seven models under a plain question. The models hold the energy densities needed for proportional scaling and answer near a typical serving, reproducing the flat-slope syndrome of dietary recall. Accuracy at usual portions does not reveal it. Food-logging tools should compute the energy of weighed entries as density times weight, and evaluations should test fixed foods across stated weights.

ADAPT: Adaptive Decision and Abstention with Pool-Size-Aware Thresholds for Service Routing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Retrieval-based classifiers efficiently route queries to specialised services, but they typically select a candidate even when no suitable service exists. We introduce Adaptive Decision and Abstention with Pool-Size-Aware Thresholds for Service Routing (ADAPT), a lightweight routing architecture with an explicit fallback option that enables queries to abstain from specialist selection. ADAPT uses the separation between the highest relevance score and the candidate score distribution as routing confidence. Experiments across retrieval models and increasing numbers of services reveal a consistent empirical behaviour: the confidence distribution changes systematically with candidate-pool size K, making fixed abstention thresholds increasingly miscalibrated. ADAPT addresses this effect with a K-aware threshold that adapts as services are added without classifier retraining. Evaluation across two specialist datasets shows that this simple mechanism provides an effective accuracy-cost trade-off compared with generative LLM routing, supporting lightweight and dynamically extensible service-routing architectures.

Learning to Think with Images Faithfully via Adaptive Data Selection

📅 2026-11-19 14:43
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Thinking with images (TwI) empowers vision-language models (VLMs) to overcome perceptual bottlenecks by actively cropping and zooming into fine-grained visual details, promising both higher accuracy and interpretable, region-grounded reasoning. However, our analysis shows that the improved accuracy of TwI models does not guarantee faithful tool use; they frequently invoke tools without actually relying on the zoomed region, undermining the reliability of tool-assisted reasoning. We trace this unfaithful behaviour to the training data: when questions are answerable without zooming, the model can answer correctly while ignoring the returned evidence, and this problem is exacerbated during training as the model becomes better at answering without tools. To keep zooming useful throughout training, we adapt the training data to the model's evolving capability and train on questions where correct zoomed evidence can still help the model answer correctly. This selection is supported by a data synthesis pipeline that localises small objects and constructs verifiable questions about their fine details, and complemented by an adaptiveness reward that favours tool-free answers when zooming is unnecessary. The experimental results show that our method substantially improves reasoning faithfulness and achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual search benchmarks (+11.6% on V*, +7.9% on HR-Bench 4K, and +9.4% on HR-Bench 8K). On multimodal hallucination benchmarks, our model also outperforms the compared TwI baselines. These results suggest that improving TwI faithfulness does not sacrifice accuracy and that both can be learnt from the right training data without directly supervising the reasoning process.

Posterior Matching Distillation

📅 2026-11-19 18:59
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Diffusion distillation accelerates image and video generation by compressing a pretrained teacher's iterative sampling process into a few network evaluations. Distribution-based methods achieve this by matching the student's output distribution to the teacher's. This matching is often driven by reverse-KL objectives, whose mode-seeking bias favors dominant teacher modes at the expense of diversity across samples. We introduce Posterior Matching Distillation (PMD), a new member of the distribution-based distillation family that combines mode-covering posterior sampling with any-step generation. At zero population loss, PMD recovers the teacher's full posterior of clean images at each trained noise level. Its stochastic student supports any-step sampling budgets within the trained timestep grid without further training. To learn these posteriors, PMD matches exponentially reweighted averages of student samples conditioned on a fixed noisy state to corresponding teacher posterior means. Under Gaussian corruption, exponentially tilted posterior mean is a single teacher evaluation at a shifted input. We prove that, matching tilted means over an open neighborhood uniquely identifies the full posterior. PMD thus supervises the entire conditional distribution through a family of mean constraints, without any auxiliary student-score network. Experiments on SDXL and FLUX.2 [klein] 4B demonstrate that PMD achieves the best FID among 4-step methods on both models, while maintaining competitive or leading performance across prompt-alignment and perceptual-quality metrics.

Learn, Freeze, and Transfer an Activation Function Across Architectures

📅 2026-11-19 14:44
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Transfer learning typically reuses knowledge acquired by a source network through its learned weights. We investigate whether a learned activation function can also be transferred and remain useful in networks trained from scratch. We study this complementary form of transfer through a learn-freeze-transfer protocol, in which the activation is optimized jointly with a source network in a single training run, and is then frozen and reused in target networks trained from scratch. A pointwise activation with globally shared parameters is independent of layer dimensions and can therefore be incorporated into networks of different architectures trained from scratch. We instantiate this protocol with FAct, a five-parameter activation represented by a two-harmonic truncated Fourier series, initialized from a Fourier approximation of GELU and learned in a Vision Transformer (ViT) on ImageNet-1K. When reused in a ViT of the same architecture trained from scratch on ImageNet-1K, the frozen activation (Frozen FAct) improves top-1 accuracy by 1.46 percentage points over GELU. Across four smaller target architectures (standard ViT, Swin Transformer, linear-attention ViT, and MLP-Mixer) and four image-classification datasets, it achieves the highest mean accuracy among eleven activation functions in 15 of 16 settings under a common training configuration, with improvements of up to 1.71 percentage points over the best conventional activation. When the learning rate and weight decay are tuned separately for each activation under the same search budget, Frozen FAct still ranks first in 11 of the 16 settings, second in four, and fourth in one. These results provide empirical evidence that a learned activation function can retain its usefulness when transferred across vision architectures, independently of the weights with which it was learned.

MAWAM: A Multi-Agent World-Action Model for Coordinated Manipulation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Every move in a robot team changes the world in which its teammates must act. Coordinated manipulation therefore calls for predicting the team's actions together with the physical future they jointly create. We introduce MAWAM, a multi-agent world-action model that jointly generates each robot's future views and executable action chunks in a single sample. Built from a pretrained single-robot world-action backbone, MAWAM jointly denoises all robots' action and future-view tokens through cross-agent attention, while preserving a separate control interface for each robot. MAWAM achieves the highest success on all three real-robot tasks (laundry-bag collection, bath-towel folding, and umbrella handover), with 67.8% mean success, 18.9 percentage points above MA-VLA trained on the same demonstrations and cameras. In simulation, MAWAM reaches 90.3% mean success on eight tasks with teams of up to four arms. Ablations show that cross-agent attention and team-stage future-video supervision both improve coordinated control: without attention, a four-arm relay falls from 95.0% to 39.0%, and without this supervision, Place Food falls from 78.0% to 47.0%.

Better Retrieval, Not Better Binding: Pretraining Exposure in Egocentric Video Retrieval

📅 2026-11-19 13:46
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Video-language models (VLMs) achieve strong performance on video-text retrieval and are increasingly used for egocentric video understanding. Yet, despite gains in zero-shot retrieval, they have shown limitations in compositional understanding, and it remains unclear whether they understand compositional relationships between verbs and nouns. In this work, we introduce Ego-ExBind, a compositional diagnostic protocol for analyzing pretraining exposure and verb-noun binding in egocentric video-text retrieval. Ego-ExBind consists of an Exposure Ledger built from the EgoClip pretraining corpus used by EgoVLPv2, which defines Seen Composition, Unseen Composition, and Unseen Atom splits for EPIC-KITCHENS-100 multi-instance retrieval (MIR). We further probe noun and verb binding channels through controlled noun-swap and verb-swap negatives. We find that zero-shot retrieval is positively associated with pair exposure within seen compositions, even after controlling for the marginal frequencies of verbs and nouns. However, higher pair exposure is associated with larger noun-swap margins and slightly smaller verb-swap margins. This asymmetry motivates us to test whether exposure reweighting can improve retrieval with little change in the measured swap margins. To isolate the effect of exposure input, we keep the trained checkpoint fixed and replace only the exposure input at inference. We demonstrate that this exposure reweighting can improve video-text retrieval without stronger verb-noun compositional understanding, and that the gain depends on the correct sample-level exposure correspondence. By separating exposure-driven retrieval gains from verb-noun binding, Ego-ExBind provides a diagnostic framework for evaluating whether future improvements in egocentric MIR reflect compositional understanding rather than pretraining familiarity.

The Coverage Selection Gap: Ranking, Not Generation, Limits LLM Rubric Construction

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Task-specific rubrics are increasingly used to evaluate open-ended language-model outputs, yet constructing comprehensive evaluation criteria from complex source documents remains challenging. We study automatic rubric construction in professional mergers and acquisitions (M&A) due diligence, where evaluation criteria must be inferred from task instructions and supporting documents without access to an expert-authored reference rubric. We introduce a multi-axis generation framework that explores complementary structural and free-form views of the source evidence to construct a broad pool of candidate evaluation criteria. To distinguish failures of criterion discovery from failures of candidate selection, we introduce the coverage-selection gap, which measures the difference between reference-free ranking and a reference-informed greedy diagnostic under a fixed selection budget. Developed on 16 tasks and evaluated on four held-out tasks, our framework achieves 0.876 macro recall against senior-authored reference rubrics under an LLM-based criterion-equivalence protocol. Ablation studies further show that introducing new structural search axes improves recall by 6-10 percentage points, whereas increasing generation volume along existing search directions yields at most 1.5 points. These findings suggest that, once a high-coverage candidate pool has been constructed, effective reference-free selection becomes a substantial bottleneck in document-grounded rubric construction.

Persona Lenses: What Makes a Vision-Language Model See Through Its Persona's Eyes?

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language models have been used to simulate where a user looks on a web page. However, a persona in the prompt reaches the simulated gaze only partly. We measure this reach as the difference in one model's gaze between two MatrAIx personas that differ in a single attribute. We introduce persona lenses, which place the persona between the model and the page: a text lens (a two-step prompt in which the model first writes what the persona would look at), a pixel lens (marks on the region matching the persona's interests) and a weight lens (a dwell reward gated to keep the scanpath natural). Using Qwen3-VL (4B and 32B) and Qwen3.5-9B on generated and live pages, we compare the lenses with one another and with scale, the 32B model with no lens. It has been confirmed that the text lens separates the personas strongly but is not robust to profile changes that should not move gaze. The pixel lens separates the personas less but is unaffected by attributes unrelated to gaze. At 4B and 9B, the weight lens separates the personas on interests held out from training, with no extra step on a new page. The 32B model separates the personas with the base prompt alone. Therefore, the choice of a lens depends on cost and model size.

ResVision: Spectrum-Matched Resizing and Context-Preserving Inspection for Visual Reasoning

📅 2026-11-19 16:04
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agentic visual reasoning improves vision-language models by selectively enlarging evidence without adding new scene content, but the benefit depends on whether fine structure remains accessible and whether local observations stay grounded in the full scene. We identify three recurring bottlenecks: high-frequency detail can be attenuated during visual-token formation; spatially distributed evidence can remain encoded yet difficult for the language model to retrieve; and isolated crops can weaken whole-detail correspondence through scale and positional mismatch. ResVision addresses these limitations with two complementary components. Spectrum-Matched Resizing (SMR) estimates image complexity and selects an adaptive global sampling scale, avoiding unnecessary enlargement on simple inputs while allocating more visual tokens to detail-rich images. Context-Preserving Inspection (CPI) returns each inspected crop together with the complete working view, preserving local detail and global context across tool turns. We evaluate Direct and Agentic variants on four frontier VLMs and 29 benchmarks spanning six visual-reasoning families. Complete ResVision (Agentic Base plus SMR plus CPI) is best for every model, improving by 3.36 to 4.94 points over Direct Base and 1.87 to 2.94 points over Agentic Base. These results show that adaptive visual sampling and context-preserving inspection address distinct, complementary constraints in multimodal reasoning.

DL101 The Representation Capability of Neural Network Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Universal approximation theorems (Cybenko, Funahashi, Hornik & Leshno) provide the mathematical justification for why neural networks are powerful enough to model complex, nonlinear phenomena. Shallow networks can approximate any continuous function on compact sets. Leshno's theorem clarified that non-polynomial activations are necessary for universality, which explains why ReLU, logistic, hyperbolic tangent, and other common activations work (but pure polynomials do not). However, approximation theorems provide no efficiency, training, nor generalization guarantees. They do not indicate how many neurons are needed for a given accuracy, whether gradient descent will find the right parameters, or whether networks trained on finite data will generalize well. Barron provided quantitative error bounds for particular function classes (approximation rates for functions with bounded Fourier norm), whereas Montúfar was the first to show that expressivity grows exponentially with depth (in terms of the number of linear regions). Deep networks are strictly more expressive than shallow ones with the same number of parameters. Depth is fundamentally more powerful than width, providing a rigorous mathematical explanation for the use of deep learning models.

The FrontierOR Challenge: Agentic Algorithm Design for Operations Research

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Operations research (OR) relies on algorithms that experts tailor to each problem: a new routing, scheduling or network design problem can take weeks of specialist work before it is solved well at scale. Large language model (LLM) agents now write, test and improve code, but whether they can design algorithms that scale to realistic instances is open: in the FrontierOR benchmark's one-shot evaluation, the strongest model matches Gurobi, with an objective within 1% of Gurobi's and no slower, on only 39% of the large instances. The FrontierOR Challenge asks teams to build agentic workflows that design, test, and improve algorithms for many optimization problems at once. Stage 1 releases 60 of the benchmark's 180 audited problems, spanning six application categories and five formulation types, in weekly rounds of 15; Stage 2 introduces new problems outside the benchmark, with unseen instances. Two tracks are ranked separately: in the Main track we run the submitted algorithms in a network-isolated sandbox with open-source dependencies only; in the Self-hosted track teams solve the instances on their own machines, with any software, within a timed window. Every solution is checked for feasibility, and a feasible solution scores the ratio of its objective to a frozen known reference, averaged over hidden instances.

A Control-Theoretic Approximation to Predictive Coding Dynamics

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Backpropagation remains the dominant method for training deep neural networks, but its reliance on non-local computations has motivated the search for biologically plausible alternatives. Two prominent frameworks, predictive coding (PC) and deep feedback control (DFC), offer distinct approaches to local learning, yet their relationship remains unclear. Here, we show that DFC arises as a local approximation to predictive coding dynamics near fixed points, with exact equivalence in shallow linear networks and systematic deviations arising with depth, nonlinearity, and distance from equilibrium. We characterize how this approximation, which we call Approx PC, deviates from PC in its activity dynamics, and show that these deviations can be mitigated by relinearizing the feedback controller and approaching the output target through a sequence of intermediate nudges. In networks trained on MNIST and FashionMNIST, Approx PC reaches task performance comparable to PC trained with the same nudges, and weights learned with it transfer directly to PC inference. Our results unify energy-based and control-theoretic perspectives on learning and open the door to applying tools from control theory to the training of predictive coding networks.

Efficient Multimodal Agents for Auditing 3D Worlds

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal agents can now build interactive 3D worlds and games, yet these worlds, like those built by people, often contain glitches such as floating objects, walls that can be walked through, or objects that change when no one is looking. An automatic auditor could check such worlds at scale and provide rewards for agents that build them, but only if it is both accurate and cheap. Auditing requires three abilities, namely navigating to the objects that may contain a glitch, triggering glitches that appear only after a specific behavior, and recognizing what is wrong. Current auditors are either accurate but slow and expensive or cheap but unreliable. We study how much of the audit small models can take on. A 4B vision language model fine-tuned end to end on verified trajectories of a frontier teacher guided by Bayesian search learns to navigate and trigger, and six such explorers together match a frontier model on both, but recognition stays far behind. We therefore build an auditor in which a swarm of small explorers navigates and triggers, and a frontier model reviews what they record and report. On WorldAuditBench, our auditor succeeds on 55.9% of 213 tasks in Unreal Engine 5 and Three.js worlds, 13.6 points above the strongest auditor reported, at less than a quarter of its cost. It matches the frontier reviewer exploring on its own at 4.6 times lower cost and about half the latency. Without further training, it also answers embodied questions about real-world scans, matching the frontier reviewer exploring on its own and surpassing four other frontier models by 6.4 to 16.6 points.

DSFlow: A Neuro-Symbolic Data Workflow Agent with Statistical Diagnostic Guardrails

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Data science workflows often require tools that presume non-trivial technical knowledge. Large language models (LLMs) reduce this barrier, but a gap remains between flexible general-purpose agents and statistically controlled pipelines. We introduce DSFlow, a LangGraph-based neuro-symbolic data-agent platform that combines reusable, LLM-selected skills with deterministic diagnostic guardrails. Skills provide extensible task knowledge. Symbolic validation can accept a proposed method, request clarification or replanning, override an unsuitable method, or supply a safe fallback. Regression and clustering case studies illustrate how DSFlow uses diagnostic evidence to correct method choices and documents those decisions in auditable reports.

An LLM-Agentic Framework for Intelligent Work Order Management in Industrial Maintenance

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
