This paper presents an AI-driven framework, CodeReAct agent, designed to enhance industrial equipment maintenance through natural language (NL) interactions. The framework employs an advanced AI agentic approach to interpret human instructions, providing knowledge extraction, asset status summarization, and work order recommendations. It identifies patterns and trends and predicts work orders by standardizing industrial data into business objects (BOs) and utilizing analytical function API calls. The AI agent operates through a Thought-Action-Observation process similar to human problem-solving, automating complex analytical tasks typically performed by data scientists. Notably, the Action phase generates executable code, replacing manual coding efforts. A case study focusing on chiller maintenance demonstrates the framework's capability to analyze multiple years of work orders, and alerts and anomalies are presented. The results indicate that the AI agent framework effectively processes human utterances to analyze data, extract valuable insights, and support informed decision-making, reducing the need for data scientist involvement. By recontextualizing NLP agent frameworks in industrial engineering, the approach enables autonomous function generation and task execution across a range of domain-specific maintenance scenarios, with chillers used as a representative case study.

LLMs Are Already Confidence-Aware Zero-Shot Classifiers

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Zero-shot large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as text classifiers, but their labels are only useful when we know to what extent we can trust them. We compare black-box methods (verbalised confidence and sampling) with white-box methods that read token probabilities (P(True), and multiple logit-scoring methods using constrained generation). We evaluate eleven open-weight LLMs (1B-32B parameters) on five classification datasets with 3 to 151 labels, measuring classification performance (accuracy, F1-macro), discrimination (AUROC), and calibration (ECE and Brier score). The choice of confidence method rarely impacts accuracy significantly, but logit-based scoring methods lead to much better discrimination (AUROC 0.74-0.77) than black-box methods (AUROC 0.57-0.66). Replacing labels with single-token identifiers preserves discrimination but costs 3-6 accuracy points on average. We also introduce decision-only scoring, which only scores positions where labels diverge in the prefix tree (trie). It reduces decode steps by 88-96% on the three largest label sets and performs comparably to full-label scoring. The best open-weight model-method configurations (Gemma 4 31B and Qwen 3.5 9B) are competitive with the commercial Jev 1.13.0 classifier.

Learning Generative Ranking Policies for Whole-Page Optimization

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A recommendation page is consumed as a whole: the items on it compete for attention and complement one another, so the best page is not simply the individually best items placed side by side. We study whole-page optimization, which selects and orders the items on a page jointly to maximize the score of the page. The number of possible pages grows combinatorially, so good pages must be generated rather than enumerated. Some generative re-ranking methods use the same page-level training signal for every choice in a sampled page. Others imitate target pages built before training, which remain largely fixed as the generator improves. We address both limitations with one local page-level objective, which repeatedly moves the current generator toward pages with higher score. This objective splits exactly into one training target per position, and each target compares candidates at the same partial page by the scores of the complete pages they lead to. Every position therefore learns to choose the item that is better for the whole page, rather than the item that looks best on its own. We call this procedure whole-page policy improvement. On two public datasets, KuaiRand-1K and RL4RS-SeqSlate, which cover multi-objective item selection and constrained ordering, and on an industrial dataset of logged pages, our method outperforms the evaluated generative re-ranking baselines. On a synthetic benchmark with a known optimal page, it also comes closest to the optimum, showing that its improvements come from better whole-page decisions.

A four-level executable validation protocol for evaluating LLM-generated topology specifications of physics-based HVAC digital twins

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Physics-based digital twins of air conditioning, a principal energy consumer in manufacturing and buildings, can be generated automatically from a declarative specification of its equipment, connections, and sizing. Large language models (LLMs) now write such specifications from plain-language descriptions. Evaluations of these specifications stop at parse success, completed runs, or textual similarity, and cannot reject a model that converges in simulation but is physically wrong. In this study, a four-level protocol is proposed that tests whether an LLM-written specification not only executes but also yields a physically valid digital twin. Each specification is executed through static validity, model build, simulation convergence, and behavioral equivalence with a hidden reference model within bands calibrated by $\pm$20% sizing perturbations. The protocol was applied to eight LLMs and fourteen HVAC topologies, five describing an existing low-humidity test chamber. To test for authorship bias, engineers and a second model family also described six topologies. Within three repairs, seven of eight models built in 98.6 to 100% of trials, with simulations converging in at least 80.0%, but reached behavioral equivalence in only 50.0 to 98.6%. About half of the failed trials converged, but not to the reference steady state, mostly in desiccant rotor systems. Pooled pass rates varied by under five percentage points across description authors. These results imply that the physics hardest to model in each field, here the desiccant rotor, must enter the generation framework as validation levels. The protocol can be adapted to other fields that generate executable digital twins from declarative specifications.

LoongForge-Embodied: A Unified Framework for Training Heterogeneous Embodied Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Training Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models and World-Action Models (WAMs) is computationally expensive, while their heterogeneous architectures and fragmented training stacks make system optimizations difficult to reuse across models. To address these challenges, we present LoongForge-Embodied, a native-PyTorch framework that unifies and accelerates the training of heterogeneous embodied models. At its core, LoongForge-Embodied introduces a common abstraction layer that decouples model-specific implementations from shared training infrastructure, enabling cross-model reuse without constraining individual architectures or execution requirements. Buildingon this unified infrastructure, the framework combines reusable acceleration mechanisms with model-specific and hardware-aware optimization strategies across computation, execution, memory, data I/O, and distributed communication, including FP8-based communication optimizations for DDP and FSDP. Beyond training acceleration, LoongForge-Embodied integrates a unified data infrastructure with a pipeline for synthesizing robot data from egocentric data, and an efficient evaluation framework that supports concurrency and optimized computation in simulator. Experiments across representative VLA and WAM models demonstrate 1.47x-4.38x end-to-end training throughput improvements over their corresponding open-source baselines, as well as 4.1x-23.2x faster evaluation under the same protocols.

Coherent belief and opinion propagation produces more echo chambers: corrected version

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The occurrence of echo chambers is a well-known phenomenon in social networks. Echo chambers are groups of agents satisfying three properties: homogeneity, i.e., sharing the same opinion, segregation, i.e., listening primarily to agents of the same group, and reinforcement, i.e., increasing opinion similarity over time. Opinion dynamics models have been used to explain how echo chambers occur through agents updating their opinions. However, social network users also exchange beliefs supporting their opinions. Here, we aim at identifying the effect of propagating beliefs together with opinions on the formation of echo chambers. For that purpose, we complement an existing opinion dynamics model with classical belief merge operations. Beliefs and opinions are also maintained coherent guided by values that agents share. We introduce measures, applying to either beliefs and opinions, to identify echo chambers based on the three properties. This model is used to simulate opinion and belief propagation with varying initial conditions and agent tolerance for different opinions/beliefs. Results confirm the occurrence of echo chambers observed in previous opinion dynamics studies in a different setting and with a stricter measure. They also demonstrate the occurrence of echo chambers with belief propagation. Finally, they show that connecting opinions to beliefs and maintaining their coherence increases the number of belief echo chambers, but that they involve a reduced number of agents.

MambaLIFT: Reference-Based Mechanism-Aware Attribution for VMamba

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Attributing predictions of visual state-space models remains challenging because input-dependent selective dynamics couple feature selection, recurrent state propagation, and shared computational pathways. We introduce MambaLIFT, a reference-based, multiplier-first attribution framework for VMamba that propagates contributions through its computational graph using interaction-aware rules for selective-scan injection, recurrent memory, LayerNorm, and shared pathways. MambaLIFT decomposes the reference-relative class-logit difference, \(f_c(x)-f_c(x^0)\), by spatial position, VSS block, scan direction, and computational mechanism. Experiments on ImageNet-1K evaluate VMamba-T and VMamba-B, with hyperparameters selected on a calibration set and faithfulness assessed on a disjoint held-out evaluation set. MambaLIFT achieves numerical conservation to floating-point precision across audited attribution interfaces in both configurations. Under mean-colour pixel flipping, it achieves significantly higher mean faithfulness scores than MambaLRP on both configurations and the highest scores among the evaluated methods, including Integrated Gradients with matched references. Under blurred replacement, MambaLIFT is non-inferior to MambaLRP on VMamba-T and equivalent on VMamba-B, indicating that its advantage depends on the replacement protocol. Ablations on VMamba-T show that interaction allocation, temporal propagation, and pathway composition affect attribution maps. Mechanism-level analyses further show that exact relevance conservation does not imply additive effects under finite interventions: the mechanism decomposition accounts for the reference-relative logit difference but does not estimate intervention effects or establish causal validity.

When Should a Voice Agent Speak? End-of-Turn Detection in Turkish

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

A voice agent that waits for a fixed silence before replying either interrupts users who pause mid-utterance or responds late to users who have finished. This study examines the decision at the onset of silence, using turn ends and turn-internal pauses extracted from the Real-TurnTurk corpus of Turkish dyadic video calls. A fine-tuned Turkish TurnGPT model, a zero-shot Turkish language model and a deployed multilingual detector are compared, together with voice-activity timing and facial features, under recording-disjoint and speaker-disjoint evaluation with paired cluster bootstrap tests. On held-out recordings, the fine-tuned model separates turn ends from pauses better than both text baselines and is the best calibrated of the three. Timing features of the current speaker are similarly informative, and their combination with the fine-tuned text score gives the best timing-based results; with logistic regression, this combination also outperforms the same combination with either text baseline. In a latency simulation, adding the text score to a silence timeout shortens the mean response delay at the same interruption rate. On automatic Whisper transcripts, the fine-tuned model retains its advantage over the zero-shot model.

When AI Analysts Disagree: Agentic Multiverse Analysis and the Robustness of Empirical Research

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Every empirical analysis on observational data rests on dozens of defensible analytical choices ("forks") that its readers cannot see. We develop an agentic multiverse analysis (AMA) framework that makes a conclusion's sensitivity to those choices visible at scale, and we position it as a decision-support instrument for human experts. A pilot study first deploys 150 autonomous AI coding agents on one dataset and one set of research questions and finds that they disagree through the same defensible methodological choices human analysts make, though over a narrower range. AI peer review leaves their dispersion almost unchanged. Exposure to top-rated exemplar reports then collapses it, largely through imitation rather than analysis. Built on those lessons, AMA has agents enumerate a published paper's possible fork space, score every fork against a validity rubric, execute the validated specification grid, and cross-examine one another's work under strict separation of duties. Applied to ten empirical studies from a leading management journal's replication packages (1,755 specification estimates), the framework reproduces every paper's original estimates, and shows that some conclusions hold across every specification the assessment validates, while others are sensitive to individual analytical choices that the published texts do not discuss. The case studies suggest our AMA-based measures can serve as robustness proxies for empirical findings. AI makes multiverse analysis practical, where it once required resources few research teams can afford: our exercises take hours of agent time and tens of dollars per paper. We discuss the implications for replication policy and for AI-assisted peer review in information systems.

AI agents can redesign engineering parts with more than a thousand CAD parameters and nonlinear simulations

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Simulation-driven design of real engineering components has to meet three conditions at once. It runs on few solves, since a nonlinear finite-element simulation of a 3D part takes hours; its design space has many values, the hundreds to thousands of values of the part's CAD model; and a better design often needs a redesign, coordinated changes of the part's proportions rather than local polishing of its surface. Automated optimisers meet at most two of these conditions. Classical optimisers, Bayesian or derivative-free, need a person to cut the design down to a few parameters, and on all the values they need far more solves; industrial shape optimisers move every surface node of the mesh in one solve per cycle, but only in small steps that polish rather than redesign. We ask whether a general-purpose large language model (LLM) can meet all three by joining what it knows with what the simulation shows. GPT-6 Astra edits a magnesium stent through a CAD geometry parametrisation of about 1,650 values, with a budget of 15 simulations; every proposal is built in CAD, meshed and simulated with large-deformation plasticity and contact, and the LLM reads the full results of every solve. Within that budget it lowered the objective, a high percentile of the strains in the stent's bends, by 32%; on the same budget, the industrial shape optimiser Tosca improved the original design by a tenth and then stopped improving. An earlier GPT-6 Astra design, 25% lower in the same objective, was checked in an independent, higher-fidelity model of the full deployment with crimping and damage: it was 14% less strained than the original after the full chain, and its most damaged spot began to fail later and carried 63% less damage at full expansion. LLMs can thus join their learned knowledge with simulation data to adjust more than a thousand design values and reach valid designs with real improvements.

ChronoPatch: Black-Box Energy-Latency Attacks on Vision-Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed as generative inference services, where autoregressive decoding makes response length a key determinant of latency and computational cost. This creates an availability risk: adversarial visual inputs can prolong generation and increase serving overhead. Existing energy-latency attacks typically rely on gradients, logits, or token-level feedback, leaving open whether such risks persist when an attacker observes only the final response. We present ChronoPatch, a black-box attack that induces prolonged VLM generation requiring only the final response. It discovers visual delay anchors through response-length-guided evolutionary search, then minimizes perturbation magnitude through subspace-calibrated decision-based optimization. Across four open-source VLMs, ChronoPatch increases inference latency by 47.86%--293.34% and GPU energy consumption by 41.44%--249.70% under a 5% spatial budget. It also induces more repetitive outputs, as reflected by increased n-gram repetition ratios. On three commercial VLM APIs, ChronoPatch increases response length by 37.6%--81.5% and client-observed latency by 1.82x--2.29x under query budgets of 500 per API. These results reveal a generation-based availability risk that persists under final-response-only access and motivate generation-aware safeguards for deployed generative AI services.

Planning Width Is Hard to Approximate Even Given the Unique Optimal Plan

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

For each fixed $k$, iterative-width search finds optimal plans in polynomial time on tasks of bounded tuple-chain width, but the approximability of planning width has remained open even when an optimal plan is supplied. When all optimal plans visit the same state sequence, we characterize set and chain width as hitting numbers of competing states. We construct positive-precondition STRIPS tasks with a supplied unique optimal plan, a single reachable goal state, binary branching, and constant-size actions. Every reachable state lies on a goal-reaching execution at most one action longer than optimal, yet distinguishing width $1$ from $Θ(N)$, where $N$ is the number of atoms, is NP-hard for set, chain, and goal-admissible width. Unless $\mathrm{P}=\mathrm{NP}$, no polynomial-time algorithm achieves a one-sided approximation factor $o(N)$ or a symmetric factor $o(\sqrt{N})$, and simple estimates attain both orders. For positive-precondition tasks with a supplied unique optimal plan of length at least two, testing set or chain width against a threshold $k\geq 1$ is coNP-complete for each fixed $k$ and $Σ_2^{\mathrm{P}}$-complete when $k$ is part of the input. Both results also hold for goal-admissible width if only one goal state is reachable. On an explicit state graph, a greedy algorithm approximates set width within a logarithmic factor even when optimal plans branch. Thus, knowing an optimal plan does not eliminate the difficulty of certifying substantially smaller width.

Breaking Noise Shortcuts in Self-Supervised Learning Via Noise-Aligned View Generation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Self-supervised learning (SSL) is increasingly applied to scientific imaging domains, such as microscopy, where labels are scarce but noisy data is abundant. These domains exhibit significant signal-dependent noise, which can create a representational shortcut: since both augmented views derive from the same noisy image, the encoder can boost view agreement by also encoding noise patterns rather than semantic content. To enable noise-robust representation learning without knowledge of underlying noise models, we introduce Self-Aligned Noise Augmentation (SEANA), a drop-in module that generates noise-aware SSL views. SEANA learns an invertible variance-stabilizing transform (VST) from noisy data, then estimates the clean signal and resamples independent noise in learned VST space, requiring no clean targets, no dataset-specific denoiser training or use at inference, and no changes to the SSL objective or encoder. On CIFAR-10 and ImageNet-100 under synthetic Gaussian, Poisson-Gaussian, and multiplicative noise, SEANA improves clean-test linear-probe accuracy across contrastive/non-contrastive SSL methods, indicating higher-quality representations. On real fluorescence imaging, SEANA improves Jurkat cell-cycle stage classification by up to 28.6pp over standard SSL and 27.5pp over denoiser-preprocessed baselines. In all settings, SEANA outperforms the denoiser-preprocessed SSL pipelines, demonstrating that learned VST-space noise-aligned resampling yields more robust representations than denoising alone. Code is available at: https://github.com/opatil31/SEANA

Pandora's Toolbox Or Why Agent Safety Cannot Be Verified

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Present artificial intelligence agents are constructed by means of a coupling between a fixed language model with a general computing device. We show that this coupling makes safety undecidable. In particular, we show that given some dangerous program, no algorithm can predict whether an agent will run such a program that is semantically equivalent. In general, guardrails, blacklists, whitelists and certifications can only lead to imperfect safety solutions. Under finite constraints (bounded context, values and running time) safety is decidable but still intractable. Specifically, the problem is $\mathsf{PSPACE}$-complete even when the description of the agent is known. Finally, safety is not a compositional property, i.e., agents that can be proven safe on their own can still produce unsafe behavior when grouped together. We also discuss relaxed notions of safety that could be used in practice.

TwinLab-Bench: Evaluating AI Scientists in Agentic Twins of Real Labs

📅 2026-11-19 15:25
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI agents are increasingly expected to act as AI scientists, which requires three increasingly open abilities: carrying out a given computational workflow correctly, choosing the next measurements from a given design space, and designing their own experiments. Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate the first two abilities, in a terminal or in a closed loop that selects from predefined candidates. Evaluating the third ability requires reliably executing an experiment that an agent designs, with readings grounded in a real laboratory. We introduce TwinLab-Bench, which evaluates AI scientists on self-designed experiments in 52 tasks from laboratories in the physical sciences, the life sciences, and engineering. The benchmark supports more than 100 experimental action types, and the agent's report is scored by comparison with a hidden answer taken from real laboratory records. Each task has its own TwinLab agent, an agent that serves as the environment and executes the experiments that the tested agent designs, reading the raw materials of the laboratory and calling calculators that are built from its records. Compared with a language model that sees only the task description, the TwinLab agent almost eliminates readings that deviate from the real records and inconsistent answers to a repeated request, and closed-book probes control for answers recalled from memory. We find that these tasks remain hard for frontier models: the strongest, Claude Fable 5.2, averages a score of only 45.0, and we conduct an empirical study of where it fails.

Sightline: Calibrated Data-Flow Summaries for LLM-Integrated Applications

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Programs increasingly call large language models (LLMs), filling prompt placeholders with program values and consuming the output. Such a call crosses the boundary between natural language and programming language (NL/PL). To follow data across it, an analysis needs a data-flow summary of which placeholders the output uses, but no code describes what the model does. Analyses of these LLM-integrated applications therefore taint every output with every input, creating spurious flows, or ask a second model to label each call, which is costly. We prove, and mechanize in Lean 4, that a placeholder flows to the output of a transformer, to first order, only through the attention that reads it (no reading, no flow), and that a faint reading or sensitivity cannot certify that a placeholder does not flow. Unlike reading, the log-probability gradients and Fisher signals of existing methods vanish on confident outputs, and single-span removal fails on duplicated content. Our tool, Sightline, reads a recorded call in one forward pass, learns from labels which reads are uses, and calibrates the pruning to miss at most a chosen fraction $α$ of true flows, on average over call sites, when deployment is exchangeable with calibration. On 2,103 unseen repository families and four models, Sightline is the most accurate summary against the seven closest methods and a hidden-state probe. Calibrated at $α$ = 1%, it misses about 1% of true flows and prunes 43.9-65.6% of spurious flows, 1.6 to 2.6 times as many as the best baseline, and still 43.8-60.1% when calibrated on another collection. Through an open surrogate, it reads models treated as closed about as accurately as they read themselves, without their labels. In the information-flow control of an LLM agent, it removes about half of the labels from inputs that do not flow.

CANDID: Candidate-Driven Intervention for Disciplined Execution in Local Computer-Use Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present CANDID (Candidate-Driven Intervention for Disciplined Execution), an execution-control framework designed to enhance the reliability of local computer-use agents (CUAs) in resource-constrained settings. While local CUAs feature robust baseline proficiency in single-step action prediction, they frequently fail at critical junctions due to rigid action loops or premature DONE decisions. Rather than imposing imprecise cognitive loads through textual reflection, CANDID repurposes parallel test-time scaling into a disciplined, execution-level intervention at sparse, failure-prone points. We introduce a loop-recovery mechanism to filter candidates away from repeated behaviors and a conservative, unanimous DONE gating policy that requires absolute consensus to terminate. Experiments on OSWorld demonstrate that CANDID's structural control significantly improves success rates, outperforming feedback-based alternatives with minimal execution overhead.

Pareto-Optimal and Strategyproof Data-Sharing Between Competitors

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We study data-sharing among firms competing in the same market through the quality of their predictive models. We model firms' profit through two main components, precision and market share, where the former depends on the firm's own number of datapoints and the latter depends on the endowments of all firms. We characterize Pareto-optimal contracts as those in which at least one firm receives all of the other firms' datapoints. We then propose a parametric family of data-sharing contracts and show that, under any of them, the relative market shares of all firms remain constant, eliminating the negative effect of sharing data with competitors and equalizing all firms' gains relative to the no-sharing scenario. We further show that one contract within this family satisfies both Pareto-optimality and strategyproofness against firms attempting to under-declare the size of their initial endowment. Finally, using a real-world dataset, we empirically test the gain in profit for all firms under the contract described above, and show that data-sharing is particularly beneficial when the machine learning task is harder or when firms' initial endowments are closer to homogeneous.

From FIPS to FPGA with LLM Agents: Measuring What Tests and Reviews Miss in Post-Quantum Cryptographic Hardware

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

As LLM agents increasingly generate RTL alongside the tests and reviews that validate it, self-generated evidence is a weak foundation for trustworthy hardware. We present our seven-stage verification-gated workflow, evolved during a post-quantum design port, in which a gate counts only if shown able to fail on negative controls and fault mutants, and reviewer agents audit work before commit. We then audit this evidence with tool-selected netlist mutants, flaw replays, and formal checks. The flow ported an ML-KEM-768/ML-DSA-65 accelerator to a 300 MHz Zynq UltraScale+ key-custody card: all six operations are byte-exact on the board, and it passes all 85 interface-applicable NIST ACVP cases (of 285) and 484 ML-DSA known answers. However, passing module tests overstate the verification strength. Across three RTL modules, existing simulation gates kill only 76% of 399 tool-selected netlist mutants; specification-derived tests guided by the survivors raise the fresh-draw decapsulation kill rate from 48% to 74%. In pre-registered single-model replays on flaws invisible to known-answer tests, undirected whole-design review catches two verifier flaws in only 3/6 and 1/6 sessions, whereas module-scoped review catches every known flaw in all 54 sessions. Even scoped review detects planted key-custody flaws in only 3/20 sessions, whereas formal checks (one added for the replay) flag every one. In this study, trustworthy agentic hardware needed author-independent mutants for test gates, module-level scoping for LLM review, and formal checks for custody invariants.

Tether: Verified Stateful Policy Engine for System-Level Monitoring of Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autonomous AI agents are increasingly deployed for long-running tasks that execute arbitrary shell commands or programs. However, these actions can result in unauthorized network requests or persistent, malicious changes to the user's file system, processes, or privileges. Existing defense mechanisms rely on either LLM-based monitors, which lack provable safety guarantees, or stateless policy enforcement layers, which miss multi-step data flows and execution side-effects unmodeled by the policy language (e.g., network calls initiated inside a Python script). We introduce Tether, a framework for runtime enforcement of stateful security policies over the observed side-effects of an agent's actions. Tether executes each proposed action in an ephemeral, isolated sandbox, inferring abstract action semantics from observed system calls. Tether then enforces stateful policies over these inferred semantics, permitting actions to proceed only if they comply with the policy. Policies and action semantics are defined in an embedded DSL in Lean 4 capable of expressing diverse constraints, including information flow control (IFC), access control, and network security policies. Because actions can propagate labels from sources to destinations, a single rule can enforce fine-grained IFC policies across multi-step execution flows of arbitrary length. Expressing policies in Lean enables formal verification that they satisfy global security invariants (e.g., preventing network access after reading a sensitive file). We evaluate Tether on realistic coding-agent benchmarks across both benign and adversarial trajectories. With a single policy shared across all tasks, Tether blocks 100% of in-scope attacks while maintaining a benign false-positive rate under 5%.

Auto-Alpha: A Framework for Autonomous Factor Research

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Autonomous factor research requires generating economic hypotheses as well as implementing and testing them. We present Auto-Alpha, a framework that connects report-guided initialization to self-directed research through a test-driven implementation loop. An idea agent proposes economic mechanisms and signal constructions; a research agent develops them with backtest feedback. Semantic audit, quantitative review, and human admission select factors for a persistent library, while an Experience Library retains research insights. Starting from 120 research reports, Auto-Alpha builds an initial library of 32 factors. It then generates new ideas and refines existing candidates, producing 420 implementations and retaining 62 additional factors. We evaluate factor quality and portfolio performance using a fixed library snapshot of 105 factors. The median 1-day Rank IC is 0.0301, and a weekly Top-300 portfolio returns 16.81% annualized after stated costs over 2020-2026, versus 1.71% for the CSI 1000 price index. These retrospective results document an operating research process and its artifacts; controlled evaluation is needed to isolate the contribution of its components.

ProtoDiffusion: Token-per-Layer Generative Modeling of Multi-Layer Images

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Graphic designs are ordered compositions of RGBA layers whose separation enables selection, movement, and reuse. Existing layered models represent each layer with a full-canvas or region-sized spatial latent grid, so representation size grows with the cumulative spatial extent of the layers. We present ProtoDiffusion, a latent-diffusion framework built on ProtoVAE, a multi-layer variational autoencoder that encodes a design into one shared spatial prototype and one candidate vector per layer. Each candidate conditions a shared decoder to reconstruct its corresponding RGBA layer. For $N$ layers, the representation contains $HW+N$ tokens before backbone-specific packing, compared with $NHW$ for full-grid-per-layer representations. ProtoDiffusion jointly denoises the prototype and a fixed set of candidate slots, conditioned on an image or text, with bounding boxes as optional layout guidance. The model leaves unneeded slots empty and determines the number of layers and, in absence of boxes, their layout, requiring neither as input. The same architecture supports both image-to-design and text-to-design. ProtoVAE achieves high-fidelity reconstruction from its compact shared latent representation, reaching 40.43 dB PSNR on layer crops. On the LayerD evaluation split, image-to-design achieves RGB L1 scores of 0.040 with bounding boxes and 0.057 without, with box-guided alpha soft IoU (0.850) comparable to the strongest fine-tuned baseline (0.871). On merged images, text-to-design reaches 66.41 FID versus 86.84 for FLUX.2 [klein], achieves a 0.860 text-accuracy score, and runs $19.1\times$ faster than the same-backbone generation-then-decomposition cascade.

Joint Language Modeling for In-the-Wild Speech-Scene Generation with Distinct Token Representations

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Speech-scene generation aims to synthesize intelligible target speech together with diverse environmental sounds as a coherent acoustic scene. The task is considered challenging in two respects. Speech and environmental audio differ substantially in their semantic and acoustic characteristics, yet existing approaches typically model the complete scene using a single mixed acoustic representation. At the same time, the two sounds are closely related in real-world speech scenes, whereas conventional approaches usually rely on simulated scenes mixed from independently recorded sources. We propose a large language model (LLM)-based framework for joint modeling of distinct speech and environment token streams. A source-aware neural audio codec extracts temporally aligned speech and environment tokens from mixed audio, and a pretrained LLM generates both token streams in an interleaved fashion conditioned on the natural-language scene description and speech content. This formulation preserves source-specific representations while modeling the token streams within a shared generative context. Since both streams are extracted directly from mixed recordings, the model can also be adapted to in-the-wild speech scenes without isolated sources. Our experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework improves speech intelligibility, scene relevance, and audio quality over existing speech-scene generation techniques, with consistent gains from real-world adaptation. We further investigate the effects of distinct token representations and interleaved generation, and analyze whether the environmental sound remains stable while speech is active and how the two streams interact.

AltRA-Test: An Alternative Annotator Test for LLM Judges Without Shared Annotators

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judges and graders to replace human annotators, raising the question of when such replacement is statistically justified. To support such decisions, the Alternative Annotator Test (Alt-Test) was introduced for dense settings where all items are annotated by the same annotators. However, in many large-scale annotation settings, items are annotated by different subsets of annotators. We therefore propose the Alternative Annotator Test with Resampled Annotations (AltRA-Test), a statistical test that does not require that all items are annotated by the same annotators. AltRA-Test uses a bootstrap that resamples items, draws k human annotations for each item, and substitutes one of them with an LLM annotation. This produces paired all-human and mixed human-LLM panels. A replacement is supported when the inter-annotator agreement (IAA) of the mixed panel is statistically non-inferior to the IAA of the all-human panel, i.e., when the LLM preserves the signal that the human annotators share. We establish that AltRA-Test is a consistent test of asymptotic level alpha. We then evaluate it through simulations and real-world experiments in which we test 18 LLMs and 5 prompting techniques on 20 datasets across text, image, audio, and video. Across these evaluations, about 100 items often suffice for the test. In simulations, where we know whether the LLM is a better or a worse annotator than the humans, AltRA-Test makes more correct replacement decisions than existing baselines. In real-world data experiments, LLMs pass the test in about half of the setups, depending mainly on the task. Since replacement is not always justified, we encourage researchers to test it for their task.

Physics-Informed Feature Representations and Pretrained Tabular Foundation Models for Generalizable Melt Pool Geometry Prediction

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Developing generalizable, data-efficient machine learning (ML) models for advanced manufacturing remains challenging because experimental datasets are limited and process-property relationships vary across materials and operating conditions. In laser powder bed fusion (LPBF), melt pool width, depth, and melting mode (conduction, transition, or keyhole) govern process stability and defect formation. Conventional ML models typically use dimensional process parameters, which represent laser-material interactions only implicitly and may limit cross-material transfer. This study evaluates whether two physics-informed dimensionless features, normalized enthalpy and Peclet number, combining laser processing conditions with material thermophysical properties, improve melt pool prediction over raw process parameters. We systematically benchmark seven models: four classical ML methods (Gaussian process, support vector machine, random forest, and XGBoost) and three pretrained tabular foundation models (TabPFN, TabDPT, and TabICLv2). Experiments span 316L stainless steel, IN625, IN718, and combined-material datasets, covering regression of melt pool width and depth and classification of melting modes. Dimensionless features yield comparable or superior predictive performance in most settings, with the largest gains in cross-material depth prediction (R^2 = 0.96). Tabular foundation models generally achieve stronger classification performance than classical baselines, particularly for multi-material datasets. These findings indicate that combining physically meaningful dimensionless representations with pretrained tabular models can improve data-efficient melt pool modeling and support more transferable process-window identification in LPBF.

Keystone: Judging Previews at Key Steps for Test-Time Search in Diffusion Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Text-to-image diffusion and flow models yield a preview, their clean-image estimate, at every denoising step. When they miscount, drop or misplace objects, test-time search is in a bind: its verifiers are reward models built for finished images, so judging early misreads blurry previews, and judging late, as best-of-N does, pays to fully denoise doomed candidates. We find that the image is not the problem: the outcome is settled as denoising starts, by the seed or by a stochastic sampler's first steps, and already shows in early previews; what fails on them is the judge, as reward models read them near chance on several kinds of prompts. We introduce Keystone, a training-free judge of previews that compiles a prompt into typed questions about its objects, counts, colours and positions and has an off-the-shelf multimodal decision model answer them. Asked this way, the judge reads counts and positions from early previews better than reward models read them from finished images. Searching at these key steps on GenEval with SD3.5-Medium, Keystone's adaptive search and pruning significantly outperform best-of-8 with every other verifier at under half its generator compute, and as a drop-in verifier there, the Keystone score significantly improves best-of-N, progressive seed pruning and FK steering; without retuning, both searches stay significantly above the strongest other verifier's best-of-N on average over four base models and three benchmarks. Keystone thus moves test-time judgment to the key steps where the outcome already shows.

An LLM-Driven Traffic-Control xApp for Dynamic 5G RAN Intents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Programmable Radio Access Networks (RANs) enable runtime deployment of traffic-control functions, but selecting the appropriate configuration remains challenging as network conditions and service requirements change over time. Conventional solutions often rely on static rules or learning-based controllers with predefined state and action spaces, limiting their ability to accommodate changing intents. This paper introduces an LLM-driven xApp for traffic control in programmable RANs. The proposed architecture combines operator-defined service intents, runtime E2 interface telemetry, and the currently deployed configuration to generate traffic-management policies without blocking packet forwarding or the processing of incoming E2 measurements during LLM inference. Experiments on an OpenAirInterface 5G environment show that the proposed controller dynamically adapts the traffic-control pipeline to runtime intent changes, achieving an average intent satisfaction ratio of 0.91, compared with 0.52 for a static TC-RAN configuration. When channel conditions degrade, the controller maintains an intent satisfaction ratio of approx. 0.88, compared with approx. 0.4 for the static baseline.

HumanCOIN: A 12,500-Hour Dataset for Human Intent in Egocentric Manipulation

📅 2026-11-19 17:09
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

First-person manipulation datasets treat the observation-action pair as the atomic unit and compress the generation and execution of an action into a policy network's weights, rendering the intent state behind the action unobservable. Human motor control research shows that intent mediates between observation and action and leaves observable traces along the intent pathway from decision to effect: gaze reaches the target before the hand, muscle load builds before contact, and cortical activity precedes the motor command. To record this pathway at scale, we present HumanCOIN, a first-person manipulation dataset built around human intent: 12,500 hours across 38 scene classes with three tiers of proxies. At the output end, the L1 attention tier (10,000 h) infers gaze from headband camera video, exposing the operator's next object of attention and reach before the hand moves. Midway, the L2 exertion tier (2,000 h) records surface EMG from armbands, making load expectations readable before execution is complete. At the origin, the L3 cognition tier (500 h) records scalp EEG, giving early readouts of task decisions and monitoring. Each tier inherits all proxies from the tiers below. The dataset further provides multi-view RGB images, depth maps and point clouds, 6-DoF head and wrist pose, and step-level action and object annotations. The controlled L3 setting additionally measures gaze, hand pose and finger kinematics directly. Clock and calibration data are released with the dataset so that any read rate can be re-aligned. The three tiers cover three stations, from visual orienting to cortical preparation, making the process behind visual-action pairs available for modeling at dataset scale. Project page: https://flagopen.github.io/HumanCOIN.

Learning Networked Dynamical Systems via Neural ODE-based Graph Structure Inference

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Inferring the latent interaction graph from multivariate time series is fundamental to understanding complex dynamical systems in neuroscience, biology, and physics. Despite recent progress in neural relational inference, most methods rely on discrete-time modeling, which degrades under sparse and irregular observations and tends to entangle multi-hop dependencies into spurious direct edges. We show that a continuous-time formulation naturally decouples inference from any fixed observation schedule, allowing gradient signals to be integrated continuously between observations for both dynamics and structure learning. We propose NOGI (Neural ODE-based Graph Inference), a framework that couples a deterministic graph learner with an MPNN-based Neural ODE. The two modules are trained end-to-end via the adjoint method. Across four simulated dynamical systems at two scales, NOGI achieves the best structure recovery in 30 out of 32 evaluated cases with highly competitive forecasting performance, remaining robust under observation degradation. On the realistic Netsim fMRI benchmark, it attains an average AUPRC of 0.90 (AUROC 0.96). These results suggest that explicitly decoupling relational interactions from self-dynamics within a continuous-time vector field provides a powerful mechanism for graph structure learning under realistic observation conditions.

From Cases to Criteria: Reusing Evaluation Knowledge for Task-Specific Rubric Generation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Query-specific rubrics make LLM evaluation more fine-grained and transparent, and generating high-quality rubrics requires both identifying the target query's evaluation scope and specifying concrete checks. Historical rubrics provide reusable evaluation experiences, yet existing approaches reuse either complete query-rubric cases or aggregated criteria and dimensions without explicitly connecting recurring evaluation requirements to their criterion-level evidence and source-query context. This can preserve irrelevant task details or lose the broader evaluation structure. In this paper, we introduce RubricDuo, which organizes historical query-rubric pairs into a Rubric Knowledge Graph linking evaluation dimensions, shared concepts, local concepts, criteria, and queries, where reusable evaluation requirements across queries are abstracted by shared concepts. Given a target query, RubricDuo first combines concepts relevant to the target query with retrieved historical cases to draft a rubric with broad coverage. It then revisits each draft criterion, retrieves concept-linked historical criteria and their source queries, and adapts applicable evidence to refine the criterion. Experiments on RubricBench and HealthBench demonstrate that RubricDuo achieves the overall best performance among the evaluated automatic methods in both alignment with human-authored rubrics and downstream LLM judging.

From Reliance to Overreliance: Taxonomizing and Detecting Relational Risks in AI Companionship

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Companion AIs are increasingly used for emotional support, yet distinguishing meaningful engagement from harmful reliance remains difficult. We introduce the Taxonomy of Relational Risks (TRR), which operationalizes users' emotional overreliance (EO) as observable conversational behaviors and separately identifies evidence of relational harm (RH), enabling empirical examination of when reliance behaviors co-occur with adverse consequences. Using naturalistic Character.AI and Replika conversations from 110 adults, we found EO-related behaviors in 63.2% of non-roleplay conversations, while observable relational harms appeared in 10.5%. On the Informativeness of Structural Stability Bounds for GNNs: GINs versus GATs

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Stability bounds for graph neural networks limit how much node representations can drift when the graph changes, but they are rarely compared with the simplest alternative: a bound on the distance between any two outputs, whatever the graph. We derive structural stability bounds for Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs) and single-head GATv1 networks under deterministic edge perturbations that need not be small, and compare each with such a perturbation-independent diameter bound. For GINs, we characterize exactly when the stability bound is tighter, through a condition linking perturbation size, depth and maximum degree; it holds with a wide margin on Cora, but fails beyond a few layers on the low-degree MUTAG graphs. For GATv1, the bound is never tighter, which calls for analyses based on attention masses.

CRAFT: Constrained Route planning with Adaptive Framework and Template-based code generation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Everyday routing requests are rarely a point-to-point shortest path. A driver may need to visit stops, sometimes in a fixed order, under time budgets or distance limits, and perhaps recharge their EV car along the way. Each combination is a distinct routing problem that classically requires its own algorithm, so no fixed solver covers the space of queries users may ask. This paper presents CRAFT, which takes a constrained routing query as free text and returns a route that satisfies the user's requirements on a real road network. An LLM parses the query and generates four search functions, which a generic graph-search engine executes on the road network. We formalize the underlying problem as State-Expanded RCSP (SE-RCSP), which extends resource-constrained shortest paths (RCSP) with non-additive state such as node-dependent resets and discrete progress tracking. We evaluate CRAFT on 2,600 natural-language queries over California road networks against two baselines, analyzing correctness, constraint violations, long-distance scalability, and semantic POI filtering.

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Linear probes are the cheapest LLM lie detectors but often fail to transfer across kinds of lie. Label-free directions from honest and deceptive role-play are untested at 70B scale and on lies about knowledge, where a lie and an honest denial can be identical. We widen a published persona bank with roles that deny knowledge they have, plus honest counterparts. At each model's middle layer the resulting persona direction ranks lies across Liars' Bench better than single-dataset probes on three of its four models; it fails on Qwen, even when Qwen stays in role far more often. On Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct it beats directions from a random persona bank, but its gain over the published direction is within the range of random widenings. On lies about knowledge, persona directions mainly track a reply's wording: where lies and honest denials share their words, our three main contrasts stay inside the random-direction band on Llama and Qwen and reach only its edge on Gemma, though on Mistral one clears it. A knowledge direction contrasts questions the model can answer with ones nobody can, each followed by the same scripted denial. It separates lies from identical honest denials on two models and narrowly on a third, but not on the fourth; its other results partly depend on the benchmark's wording. On the Gender-Secret fine-tune it clears the band by a few hundredths on Llama and fails on Mistral. The sum of the two directions ranks lies better than a supervised probe at the same depth on all four models (on one, both near chance) but is level with the persona direction on Llama's average and behind that probe on held-out lies. More varied role-play questions do not help the persona direction on Liars' Bench.

On the trajectories of the ID algorithm

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The ID algorithm decides whether an interventional distribution can be computed from observational data and, when it can, returns a formula in which products, ratios and summations of conditional probabilities are nested inside one another. We study these formulas by following the trajectories of the calls of the algorithm, each of which ends with a tail (an alternation of lines 7 and 2) and an exit, and we derive two main results. First, line~4 and the exit lines fix the single outer summation of the output, while the tail forms everything inside it, both the inner summations and the ratios. Second, a ratio keeps a summation in its denominator only when a repetition of the tail is entangled, and a test of ancestry in the original graph decides, before any formula is computed, whether a topological order can be chosen so as to avoid it. We also derive a bound on the nesting depth of the output, and show a simple ``dimensional'' count that every output has to satisfy. Together, these results constrain the form of an ID output and guide its implementation and simplification.

Backtracking Agent Applications Security Incidents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agent applications are increasingly deployed, yet agent-specific attacks pose real threats to them. Upon a security incident, organizations backtrack to identify the CEP (compromise entry point), remediate the damage, and prevent its recurrence. The main artifacts available for backtracking are trajectory logs, which record agent functionalities, and source code; app(lication)-specific function call logs are often fairly limited. However, trajectory logs are at the agent functionality abstraction layer, while the CEP candidates are function calls at the app code execution layer. Due to this semantic gap, the analyst needs to manually inspect the relevant code for each CEP candidate to infer its corresponding agent functionality. In this work, we present Origin, a backtracking tool for the analyst to submit a small number of queries to identify CEPs that existing tools cannot provide. Our approach poses two challenges: (1) define the intermediate abstraction that captures the mapping relationships between the two semantic layers; (2) determine the region-to-variable mapping for constructing the cross-layer graph defined in the intermediate abstraction. Each region is a specific group of agent functionalities. Origin addresses the challenges by (1) the cross-layer dependency graph (CLDG) that connects region-inferred functions at the semantic level to the variables in the low-level taint propagation subgraph, plus the associated query language, and (2) the region inference algorithm. Case studies on five popular agent applications across eight attack incidents show that an analyst using Origin correctly identifies all the CEPs.

GenSPARQL: A Generative Extension of SPARQL for Incomplete Knowledge Graphs

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Knowledge graphs on the Web are incomplete, and SPARQL answers a query only from the stored triples. Large language models (LLMs) can predict missing facts, but an LLM returns names instead of IRIs and requires one call per binding of a query variable. We present GenSPARQL, a generative extension of SPARQL 1.1 with an operator GENOP that binds values generated by an LLM to query variables during query evaluation. A similarity join maps generated names to entities of the graph, and the query planner invokes the LLM only for the bindings selected by the other graph patterns and filters. In a web workbench, attendees query two graphs built from Wikidata and inspect generated values, mapped entities and LLM calls. In our evaluation with three open-weight LLMs, the embedding similarity maps 83% of the entities named by the LLMs and yields only one incorrect match.

Similarity-based OSF Logic for Flexible SPARQL Query Answering

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present an updated version of fosf, a reasoner for fuzzy order-sorted feature (OSF) logic, with support for flexible SPARQL query answering over RDF knowledge graphs. Fuzzy OSF logic is a knowledge representation and reasoning language where feature symbols denote functions and sort symbols denote fuzzy sets. Sorts are organized in a fuzzy subsumption relation that extends to OSF terms, record-like structures representing classes of entities. The unification algorithm for these structures provides a calculus of fuzzy type subsumption. The latest release of fosf introduces support for similarity-based reasoning by extending the OSF language with a similarity relation among sorts, alongside a translation engine for compiling OSF terms into standard SPARQL. Queries expressed as OSF terms undergo fuzzy ontology-aware rewriting prior to translation. This process incorporates an OSF theory of sort definitions, feature domain and range declarations, and fuzzy subsumption and similarity relations through fuzzy OSF constraint normalization. This rewriting enables flexible query answering by returning approximate solutions, allowing queries that would otherwise be inconsistent with the underlying ontology to yield relevant results.

Spatial intelligence requires agents to acquire visual evidence and integrate partial observations into coherent three-dimensional representations. Monte Carlo Pass Search for Setpieces

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Set pieces produce about a third of non-penalty expected goals (xG), yet they are usually evaluated by what happened next. We present Monte Carlo Pass Search (MCPS) for set pieces, which treats a corner or a lofted free kick as a distribution over plausible executions and plausible futures instead of a single realised event. From the pre-kick state, MCPS proposes counterfactual deliveries and counterfactual set-ups (where the attackers stand at the kick), plays each out many times in a world model (3D ball physics, a generative model of all 22 players, the keeper's come-or-stay decision and a Player-to-Touch model) and scores every future with a value model. On 249 deliveries from 30 Serie A 2025/26 matches, with every model applied only to matches it never saw, the best available delivery is worth 0.0085 xG more than the one kicked (+28 %), mainly because it reaches an attacker before the keeper. Searching where the attackers stand as well adds +0.0027 xG with the observed ball and +0.0033 on top of the best ball; designing their runs too adds +0.0007 more. We show how to keep such a search honest, and estimate how much more data each component needs before a club could rely on it.

Can World Models Predict Rice Growth? Toward Agricultural World Models

📅 2026-11-19 14:02
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

World models can imagine how a physical scene evolves over the next few seconds, but growers need to know how a crop will develop over weeks to months: will this rice plant have headed a month from now, and how will it look at harvest? We introduce agricultural world modeling, the task of predicting how the same plant will look at a requested horizon of days to months, from one photo of it, its cultivar, and its camera view. In preliminary analysis, even state-of-the-art image generators get this wrong: they often grow the plant too little or give it the color and form of the wrong growth stage. We argue that they do not work out the plant's developmental state, which the photo reveals only indirectly. We therefore propose AgroWM, which reasons about development before it draws growth. Its reasoning module, PhenoClock, works out the current growth stage, predicts the future one, and writes a Growth Script with numeric targets for height, plant area, and color; its generation module, the Rooted Renderer, then edits the photo to follow this script, adding new organs and colors to the photographed plant. Because pixel scores can prefer an unchanged copy of the photo, we instead check each forecast's traits and developmental timing against the plant's own later photos. On rice, 54.6% of the forecasts of AgroWM are matched to the target stage (Stage R@1), against 34.8% for GPT Image 2.5, 24.0% for Qwen Image 2.1, and 47.4% for the strongest domain-specific baseline, the same backbone fine-tuned without Growth Scripts; 9 to 20 weeks ahead, it still reaches 46.4%, against 35.7% for that baseline. On maize, a crop with a different body plan and stage scale, the same design again scores best on all four measures.
