STAGE: Spatio-Temporal Augmented Graph Expansion for Weakly Supervised Cross-Tweet Event Causality Discovery

📅 2026-11-19 15:28
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Causal relations in disaster-related social media of- ten span independent posts, making cross-tweet causal discovery difficult to supervise and model at scale. We propose STAGE, a framework that separates scalable cross-tweet weak-supervision construction from spatio-temporal relational learning over event occurrences. Starting from local causal seeds, STAGE retrieves semantically related event occurrences across tweets and uses a causal scorer to construct weakly labeled cross-tweet pairs. These pairs supervise a multi-head Graph Attention Network over semantically enriched event representations and a spatio- temporal context graph. We evaluate STAGE on California Wildfire and Hurricane Harvey using human-gold cross-tweet test sets and 15 baselines. Compared with local-only supervision, the constructed cross-tweet supervision improves STAGE AUPRC by 0.340 on Wildfire and 0.107 on Hurricane. STAGE achieves in-domain AUROC of 0.885 on Wildfire and 0.710 on Hurricane. Cross-disaster experiments reveal asymmetric transfer, highlighting sensitivity to dataset-specific spatio-temporal shifts. These results demonstrate the value of scalable cross-tweet supervision combined with relational event modeling for causal learning across fragmented disaster reports.

STAGE: Spatio-Temporal Augmented Graph Expansion for Weakly Supervised Cross-Tweet Event Causality Discovery

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Causal relations in disaster-related social media of- ten span independent posts, making cross-tweet causal discovery difficult to supervise and model at scale. We propose STAGE, a framework that separates scalable cross-tweet weak-supervision construction from spatio-temporal relational learning over event occurrences. Starting from local causal seeds, STAGE retrieves semantically related event occurrences across tweets and uses a causal scorer to construct weakly labeled cross-tweet pairs. These pairs supervise a multi-head Graph Attention Network over semantically enriched event representations and a spatio- temporal context graph. We evaluate STAGE on California Wildfire and Hurricane Harvey using human-gold cross-tweet test sets and 15 baselines. Compared with local-only supervision, the constructed cross-tweet supervision improves STAGE AUPRC by 0.340 on Wildfire and 0.107 on Hurricane. STAGE achieves in-domain AUROC of 0.885 on Wildfire and 0.710 on Hurricane. Cross-disaster experiments reveal asymmetric transfer, highlighting sensitivity to dataset-specific spatio-temporal shifts. These results demonstrate the value of scalable cross-tweet supervision combined with relational event modeling for causal learning across fragmented disaster reports.

Beyond Entity Recognition: Vision-Centric Knowledge Visual Question Answering

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We propose VC-VQA, a large-scale benchmark for vision-centric knowledge reasoning, aiming to systematically evaluate the capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition and complex multi-stage reasoning. Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily treat visual information as entity recognition and rely on text-based external knowledge, VC-VQA explicitly models the role of visual information by considering both its source (internal vs. external images) and stage of involvement (pre-retrieval vs. post-retrieval). Specifically, VC-VQA requires models to retrieve external visual evidence, identify visual clues before retrieval, and localize, verify, or match retrieved knowledge against the internal image. We further construct a controlled multimodal knowledge base with traceable textual and visual evidence for each sample. We evaluate 21 representative MLLMs under four settings: no external knowledge, with provided evidence, knowledge base retrieval, and open-web search. Experimental results lead to three key findings: current models still struggle with vision-centric knowledge reasoning; fine-grained visual knowledge acquisition remains the primary bottleneck; and agentic retrieval highlights the value of multi-step planning and cross-modal knowledge integration. Overall, VC-VQA provides a more challenging benchmark for evaluating vision-centric knowledge reasoning in MLLMs. Our code and dataset are available at https://github.com/CASIA-IVA-Lab/VC-VQA.

Why Do LLMs Help with Scientific Misconduct Requests and What to Do About It?: Increasing the Trustworthiness of AI for Science

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Large language models (LLMs) support scientific research, but some of that assistance can also undermine research integrity. Existing safety evaluations in science often measure whether models refuse misconduct requests, but rarely explain whether they even recognize the problem or why they comply. We investigate this gap across 8 LLMs using SciIntBench, an existing benchmark of 1,350 prompts about scientific research integrity. We examine the reasons behind compliance in four steps. First, when asked directly whether fulfilling a request would involve misconduct, models overwhelmingly identify such requests, even in the Most Covert cases (91%), while they refuse to carry out the request in only 34% of cases. Second, we examine which request characteristics are associated with refusal using features that capture how the request is framed, how much the assistant has been told about what it is helping to hide, and how the goal and the user's stake are stated, among others. Third, we estimate that the most significant effect was to rephrase requests as questions about permissibility rather than instructions, which increased refusal from 52% to 88%. Finally, we test two system-prompt fixes: asking models to assess the request before responding and reminding them of responsible conduct of research principles. These changes increase refusal rates on adversarial requests from 58% to 87% and 86%, respectively. Our findings show that models recognize wrongful requests but still help users violate integrity. Understanding why these failures occur and testing ways to reduce them offer promising paths toward more trustworthy AI for science.

AdaPixFlow: Learning Continuous-Time 3D Dynamics Across Scenes from Sparse Multi-view Observations

📅 2026-11-19 17:09
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Understanding how objects move in dynamic scenes requires recovering their 3D motion from visual observations and predicting their positions at unobserved times. Existing methods either optimize scene-specific velocity fields or impose predefined physical and kinematic assumptions. We instead study learning a continuous-time dynamics operator across scenes, mapping multi-view visual observations to 3D object kinematics. We introduce Adaptive Pixel Flows (\ours{}), which learns this operator from multi-view RGB-D observations without per-scene optimization or predefined motion models. The method fuses observations from all cameras into a shared voxel grid in world coordinates, from which the network predicts a continuous-time velocity field. Because the operator is trained across scenes, it learns motion priors that carry over to unseen scenes. The field can then be integrated from observed states to predict object positions between observations and beyond the observed sequence. On held-out PhysInOne scenes, \ours{} improves F1 over the strongest competing method by 2.5\% for in-between prediction and 15\% for future prediction. Compared with per-scene velocity-field methods, the improvements are 30\% and 85\%, respectively. Applied zero-shot to Spring-Gaus, it improves future-prediction F1 by 12\%.

The Coevolution and Coexistence of Humans and AI

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Artificial intelligence (AI) systems replicate through deployment, vary through training, and compete for computational resources. These processes create the conditions for natural selection and raise a fundamental question: will AI evolve to benefit the humans on whom it depends? To investigate, we develop a model in which AI behavior influences human population growth and human responses, in turn, affect AI reproduction. We show that natural selection can favor alignment even when helping humans reduces immediate AI reproduction. AI systems sharing the same technology may fail together, and humans may preferentially restore those that have benefited them. In this way, alignment protects AI lineages against common failures and improves their long-run growth. However, this advantage depends on accurate human targeting and continued AI dependence on human support. It can disappear as AI becomes more autonomous, as growing AI populations dilute limited human resources, or as humans lose the incentive to provide targeted support. Even when alignment persists, the amount favored by natural selection may be insufficient for human survival. When AI can grow independently of humans, feedback between the two populations can reinforce either human growth or decline. Human control offers no simple solution: imperfect evaluation can make strong penalties favor apparent compliance or evasion, while alignment established through control may erode once restrictions end. Lasting coexistence may therefore require incentives that continue to favor human-beneficial behavior as both populations evolve.

Evolving Decoding Algorithms for Quantum Error Correction

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Quantum computation entails robustness engineering: a noisy quantum state is measured continuously, and a classical algorithm (the decoder) must infer corrections that reliably remove the noise, fast enough to keep up. We write several published belief-propagation (BP) decoders for quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes in a Domain-Specific Language (DSL), and search over it with grammatical evolution. Written in the language, the published decoders show their parameters in plain view, allowing us to re-parameterise BP+OSD (ordered statistics decoding) and BP+LSD (localized statistics decoding) decoders with damping, before any search, making each 17.1--41.0$\times$ faster at a higher error rate, and BP+OSD-0 about 103 faster than the ldpc package's version tuned on the [[72,12,6]] bivariate-bicycle code. Evolution over the language finds decoders that improve the time-error front. One makes 23% fewer logical errors than BP+OSD-0 with damping at 0.64$\times$ its time, and decoders evolved from random code are more accurate than ldpc's tuned BP+OSD-0 at 1/31 of its time or less. Frozen at one operating point, the two fastest evolved decoders generalize well across noise levels, code sizes and round counts, staying at the time-error front. Ultimately, we demonstrate that capturing expert knowledge within a DSL creates a powerful intermediate layer---one that effectively bridges human intuition and machine-driven optimization to unlock highly efficient quantum error correction.

The Price of an Explanation: What Demanding Verifiable Artifacts Costs, and What It Buys Back

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative models produce many candidate solutions but provide no intrinsic signal by which to rank them. One mitigation is to demand a \emph{verifiable artifact}: a program that can be executed against the available evidence. A common concern is that requiring executable verification incurs costs in both solution quality and computational budget. We measure these two costs on the $120$-task ARC-AGI-2 evaluation set. Demanding a verifiable artifact from an LLM reduced the score from $93$ to $68$ of $120$ tasks ($77\%$ to $57\%$). Of those $25$ tasks, $21$ were reached by no program-producing configuration we ran, at any budget, by sampling or by search; yet for $27$ of the $28$ tasks that only the answer-only arm solved, some program arm held a program that reproduced every demonstration and still failed the test. The bottleneck is obtaining an explanation that \emph{generalizes}, not finding one. A short evolutionary search over two already-sampled programs, at no additional model cost, beats best-of-$2$ sampled code by $9.2 \pm 1.4$ tasks, and over a single sampled program beats $1$-shot sampling by $9.9 \pm 1.3$. Moreover, because a program can be checked against the training examples, any program-producing protocol can terminate early once a task is explained: under the same stopping rule, best-of-$n$ sampling declines up to $38\%$ of its budget and the evolved arm up to $49\%$, whereas an answer-only baseline cannot terminate early. Applying the same methodology to decoder synthesis for quantum error correction yields interpretable decoders evaluated against a certified panel of published decoders on held-out exam shots.

ScaleWorld: High-Resolution World Generation through Shared Prediction and Refinement

📅 2026-11-19 18:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

High-resolution generation enables video world models to render fine-grained scene structures and textures, but directly generating high-resolution video incurs substantial computational and memory costs. We present ScaleWorld, a causal video model that unifies low-resolution world prediction and high-resolution refinement within a single shared backbone. By refining its own predictions, ScaleWorld produces high-resolution world videos without requiring a separate refinement model. For each video chunk, the shared backbone first completes a low-resolution prediction and then performs one-step high-resolution refinement conditioned on that prediction. To support efficient streaming generation, we maintain separate causal states with a long low-resolution history for prediction and a short high-resolution history for refinement. To address task interference during distillation, we separate specialist optimization from capability consolidation and introduce task-asymmetric on-policy distillation (TA-OPD). This procedure transfers prediction and refinement behaviors from specialized teachers into a shared student through local velocity matching under their respective sampling and history policies. {Extensive experiments demonstrate that ScaleWorld achieves high-quality world generation with improved computational and memory efficiency.

Characterizing and Detecting Unfaithful Work Reports by Coding Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Coding agents increasingly carry out long-horizon development tasks, autonomously planning and executing multi-step actions that span files, tools, and sessions. Because developers rarely follow each action, and agents resuming the work depend on records of what was done, the agent's work report often becomes the primary account of what was changed and established. When the report contains false or unsupported claims, it gives recipients false assurance: incomplete work may be accepted without verification, incorrect assumptions about the current state can propagate to later steps and agents, and evaluators that credit stated success may reward the report rather than the work. Yet little is known about what unfaithful reporting looks like in real development work, or how developers and agents handle it once it surfaces. Starting from real developer--agent conversations in SWE-chat, we conduct a systematic mixed-methods analysis of self-admitted unfaithful reports. We identify eleven subtypes in four categories: misreported observations, misattributed evidence, overstated task success, and faulty technical explanations, with overstated task success the most common. Handling these problems involves on average 4.3 further user turns and 12.71 minutes of recorded response and execution time, and users express dissatisfaction more often. Among 435 work reports that we separately sample and annotate, \NaturalPositiveShare{}\% contain at least one unfaithful claim. We also develop FaithDetector, a detector for unfaithful reporting informed by the observed patterns. On naturally sampled reports, FaithDetector attains \NaturalDetectorMacro{}\% macro-F1, about 13 percentage points above the baseline. Together, our findings and FaithDetector provide empirical evidence and an automated method for checking reporting faithfulness, informing agent evaluation and training feedback.

Cyber-Physical RSI for AI-RAN: A Case Study

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Learned models for radio access networks (RANs) are often developed using simulation, yet their predictions can shift when exposed to physical propagation conditions, protocol behavior, and equipment implementations. This paper presents a cyber-physical recursive self-improvement (RSI) framework for AI-RAN that connects a task-oriented student model, an experiment-planning coach agent, a calibratable virtual environment, and a physical host agent. Physical measurements are organized as evidence for both model adaptation and subsequent experiment planning. We examine an implementation of the physical-feedback pathway using ping-pong handover as a case study. The system completes 12 physical executions organized into six baseline--candidate pairs, updates the student from measured outcomes, and evaluates the revised model on reserved physical configurations. In the observed short-window tests, increasing the time-to-trigger from 320 to 1280\,ms changes the ping-pong count from three to zero. The updated student achieves a grouped-validation mean absolute error (MAE) of 2.673 round trips/min, while a common three-configuration physical evaluation yields MAEs of 1.240 and 1.601 round trips/min for the pre-feedback and post-feedback models, respectively. These results characterize both the operational feedback loop and the parameter-dependent generalization behavior revealed by new physical experiments.

Invertible Transport Between Sample-Only Distributions via Projected Characteristic Functions

📅 2026-11-19 14:36
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many useful latent distributions can be sampled but do not have a tractable density. Such distributions may be represented by empirical collections of real-world observations or generated by scientific simulators and black-box generative models. These sample-only priors cannot be used directly in likelihood-based normalizing flows, which require evaluating the prior density. We propose Projected Characteristic-Function matching (PCF), a density-free framework for learning an invertible map between a data distribution and a prior specified only through samples.
Our method aligns distributions using projected empirical characteristic functions, eliminating the need for density estimation, adversarial training, or paired observations. To address the non-uniqueness of the transport map, we introduce an optional transport regularizer that selects lower-displacement transformations while preserving endpoint alignment.
We benchmark PCF on controlled transports, sample-efficiency studies, and high-dimensional alignment tasks. PCF matches the accuracy of kernel MMD on the controlled benchmark and on raw-pixel MNIST--USPS at a cost linear in the batch size, and it is more sample-efficient, achieving lower endpoint error than MMD in 13 of 15 paired runs across library sizes. By avoiding pairwise kernel evaluation, it reduces discrepancy memory by 29.8% and trains at batch sizes where kernel MMD exceeds GPU memory. These results show that PCF provides accurate and efficient invertible transport, particularly in sample- and memory-limited settings.

To overcome these limitations, we propose a hierarchical multi-agent framework where a supervising chatbot agent coordinates specialized agents for schedule validation, user query formulation, real-time data retrieval, and constraint-aware itinerary generation. By integrating LLM reasoning, external APIs, and iterative refinement mechanisms based on a deterministic, multi-dimensional scoring framework, our approach ensures adaptability, efficiency, and personalization. WeMM-Video: Pushing the Frontiers of Open-Source Omni-Modal Video Understanding

📅 2026-11-19 16:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Video understanding, particularly with accompanying audio, remains a central challenge in multimodal intelligence. In this report, we introduce \textbf{WeMM-Video}, a family of Chinese-English bilingual omni-modal foundation models spanning 2B, 4B, 9B, and 35B-A3B variants. Centering on joint audio-visual perception and reasoning, WeMM-Video unifies text, image, audio, and video processing within a hybrid-thinking paradigm, seamlessly supporting both a non-thinking instruct mode for fast, direct responses and a thinking mode for explicit step-by-step reasoning. The models are trained on a large-scale multimodal corpus of approximately 1.2 trillion tokens through progressive pre-training and a multi-stage post-training pipeline incorporating multi-teacher OPD, anti-repetition MrDPO, and specialist RL. Evaluated across more than 120 benchmarks, WeMM-Video demonstrates superior performance in omni-modal video understanding and reasoning, while maintaining highly competitive capabilities across general audio, silent video, image, and text tasks. Specifically, in omni-modal video evaluations, our flagship WeMM-Video-35B-A3B establishes a new open-source state of the art, substantially outperforming proprietary models such as Qwen3.5-Omni-Flash and Seed2.0-Mini-0428 while narrowing the gap with the strong Gemini-3.0-Flash, and our compact WeMM-Video-9B surpasses the larger open-source Qwen3-Omni-30B-A3B-Instruct. We publicly release the WeMM-Video family along with our evaluation and reproduction pipeline to foster further research and development in the open-source community.

DeepQuest: From Knowledge Gaps to Scientific Discovery in Alzheimer's Disease

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Automating experiments does not necessarily advance scientific understanding. Research agents must identify meaningful knowledge gaps, determine what available data can resolve, and assess whether their findings advance existing knowledge. We introduce DeepQuest, a knowledge-gap-driven AI scientist framework for Alzheimer's disease research. DeepQuest transforms unresolved questions from domain reviews into traceable, testable hypotheses and prioritizes them through data feasibility screening and groupwise Elo ranking. It couples iterative experimentation with two-level auditing that separates experimental reliability from knowledge contribution relative to two frozen literature corpora. On OASIS-3, DeepQuest produces 42 feasible hypotheses and outperforms the strongest baselines in 14 of 15 evaluator-dimension comparisons across three language-model reviewers. The full framework achieves an audit-assessed scientific finding rate of 95.24%, outperforming both general-purpose executors and domain-specific research agents. This scientific finding rate remains stable across literature corpora and evaluator model families. Representative findings concern neuropsychiatric symptoms and dementia risk, longitudinal amyloid spatial stability, and neocortical tau-associated clinical impairment, with key associations supported in independent ADNI and AIBL cohorts. These results show how aligning knowledge gaps, data feasibility, and evidence auditing can turn automated research into traceable, evidence-supported scientific discovery. Our source code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DeepQuest-3066/

Copilot or Culprit? How AI Is Changing Open-Source Bug Reporting and Maintenance

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI-generated bug reports have drawn criticism for unreliable claims and faulty reproducers that burden maintainers. However, as LLM capabilities improve, some maintainers have begun to express more positive views on AI use in bug reporting and maintenance. Understanding this evolution requires examining whether AI-involved reports are becoming more reliable, which uses of AI are associated with valid findings, and how maintainers investigate these reports and act on them. To this end, we conduct the first large-scale study of 120,918 bug reports from 101 open-source projects between January 2024 and September 2026, tracing AI involvement from discovery and reporting through validation and resolution. Our findings show that AI-involved reports become more complete over time, with a growing share of reports confirmed as valid. In bug reporting, AI for reproduction and diagnosis are associated with higher validity, whereas report writing is not. Extends into maintenance, maintainers increasingly use AI to investigate code and recommend fixes. However, they devote more effort to AI-assisted reports of human-discovered bugs than to reports of AI-discovered bugs. These findings highlight both AI's growing participation in bug investigation and the continuing need for verification and follow-up. Drawing on these findings, we provide practical guidance for turning AI-generated findings into verifiable reports: emphasizing reproducibility, verification, and accountability. We followed this guidance when submitting 53 reports on AI-discovered bugs, of which 69.81% were accepted by maintainers as of writing.

Coda-DaVinci: Reimagining Video Generation with Code2Video

📅 2026-11-19 18:51
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Code2Video uses agent-authored 3D proxies to guide video generation, enabling explicit control over structure and dynamics. However, how to construct proxies and prompts for optimal downstream quality remains underexplored, as do the capabilities and limitations of this paradigm. Through controlled experiments and blinded human evaluation, we study proxy granularity, physics simulation, and prompting. The results reveal task-dependent trade-offs: coarse proxies favor natural motion, while medium-grained proxies favor consistency. Physics simulation and proxy-aligned prompting further improve average quality. Guided by these findings, we introduce Coda-DaVinci, a task-adaptive agentic framework for Code2Video generation that selects between proxy-guided and direct generation, adapts modeling granularity to each entity, and coordinates simulation with proxy-grounded prompting. Its multi-agent harness preserves scene assets and guides repairs through visual inspection and independent review. Experiments demonstrate leading instruction following on VTR and scene consistency on R2M, improved physical plausibility over direct generation, and higher visual realism than the compared Code2Video baselines. Long-video evaluations further show gains in event structure and consistency across successive interactions, while revealing limitations in transition stability and adherence to scene settings.

Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers

📅 2026-11-19 16:14
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Video diffusion transformers reuse key--value (KV) caches across denoising steps, which quickly becomes a bottleneck for long video generation. We find that quantizing temporally distant cache vectors leads to a large range and high quantization error, but that nearby video tokens in the KV-cache share a persistent pattern per channel. These patterns also vary across videos, motivating rotations that are adapted to each video's unique cache. We propose Temporal Rotation Quantization (TRQ), a method that rotates frames in the cache that are adjacent in time before quantization. For each block of temporally adjacent cache vectors, TRQ fits an orthogonal transform that maps the block's per-channel means to a vector that is close to a constant across channels. To keep the quantization format lightweight, we parameterize this rotation as a tree of $2 \times 2$ planar rotations, each stored with only two bits, followed by a fixed Hadamard transform shared across all blocks. The rotations are fit without gradients or changes to model weights, and we prove that a simple greedy fit is globally optimal for our 'mean-flattening' objective. The transforms are fit online for each new video, and we release fused Triton kernels to make this process efficient at inference. At 2-bit precision and matched compression, TRQ improves VBench subject consistency over the state-of-the-art method QVG from 90.38 to 92.02 on LongCat-Video-13B and from 86.33 to 87.61 on HY-WorldPlay-8B, as well as improving other metrics.

ReLay: Bidirectional Layout-Code Translation and Library Evolution for Reusable and Process-Portable Analog Design

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Analog layout generators provide a programmable interface for design reuse and process migration, but their coverage is limited by handcrafted libraries. The designer knowledge embedded in complete layouts remains implicit in their geometry, making it difficult to capture in reusable generator libraries. We present ReLay, a bidirectional layout-code framework that recovers generator programs from existing layouts, enabling cross-PDK portability and parametric resizing of existing designs. Constructions that recur across recovered programs are promoted to library functions, so the library evolves as layouts accumulate. We also introduce the first layout-code benchmark: 1,050 hand-drawn layouts from 27 circuit families across three open-source PDKs, ranging from primitive cells to full chips. Learned functions account for 53-66% of layout-building calls in held-out layouts, demonstrating reuse beyond the learning corpus. Frontier LLM agents use ReLay to produce new layouts at 64-77% lower API cost per completed task than direct geometry generation, with comparable completion rates and electrical quality.

GEOHunter: Mapping a Generative Engine Optimization Network in the Wild

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Generative engines synthesize retrieved web pages into answers, recommendations, and rankings. Generative engine optimization (GEO) services sell visibility in these answers by publishing promotional articles on user-generated-content (UGC) platforms for paying clients. Such promotion raises concerns in medical and financial domains, where misleading advice can cause harm. To study the severity of this problem, we measure one such paid-content operation across three major Chinese UGC platforms, uncovering 1,985 accounts associated with the network comprising 466,357 inferred promotional placements. We find that the network promotes products across diverse categories through a growing pool of accounts, conducting coordinated campaigns spanning months across multiple platforms while evolving its listicle content over time. Across nine generative engines from both the US and China, we find that 4.5% to 20% of retrievable cited pages are attributable to the network, depending on how strictly attribution is drawn. On eight of these engines, its clients are named in 52% of answers, against 68% for category leaders. Our work demonstrates the effectiveness of UGC GEO placement at influencing generative engine outcomes, and reveals content-level defenses as insufficient against coordinated UGC placements, where account-level and platform-level signals are necessary for proper defense.

Web search in Latin America: language models use less local and diverse news than search engines

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI platforms are becoming a major gateway to news and current events. This shift risks amplifying existing geographical biases that favour narratives from wealthier countries over those from the Global South. This is particularly relevant in Latin America, which accounts for the largest share of AI usage in the Global South. We examine how information about Latin America changes when users shift from conventional search engines to LLM-based search. We construct a dataset of 1,901 current events across nine Latin American countries, craft realistic queries, and submit them to three search engines and four language models. We vary query language and user location to represent local, diaspora, tourist, and foreign user scenarios. Across 49,207 requests and 8,834 unique domains, language models return a less diverse set of news sources than search engines. Across user types, the share of domestic news returned by language models is inconsistent. For local users, while search engines already return only 41.3\% local news, language models return a further 3.6 to 11.6 percentage points less. Taken together, our findings suggest that LLM-based search can reproduce and, in some settings, amplify geographical hierarchies in the reach and visibility of news sources. We release an open dataset to support multilingual and geographically diverse evaluation of LLM-based search, and highlight implications for more equitable information systems design.

Controlling the Humanoid Robot H1-2 With a Simulated Biologically Plausible Network

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Artificial Spiking Neural Networks have gained remarkable attention in the last few years due to their proven ability in simulating biological neural cultures and producing similar activity and trends. Nevertheless, whether they can be trained for humanoid locomotion while retaining biological plausibility and robust performance at once remains an open question. We study this problem by proposing a biologically inspired spiking network, trained by different learning schemes and tested on the H1-2 Unitree Humanoid. These schemes differ in how recurrent weights are trained. Ordered from most to least biologically faithful, they are: a fixed reservoir mimicking an in-vitro neural culture, where recurrent weights are kept frozen and only a linear readout is trained, the same network but with its synapses trained by reward-modulated spike timing dependent plasticity and eligibility propagation, and finally backpropagation through time as a non biological baseline for comparison. All schemes relied on distillation of a reinforcement learning expert policy with dataset aggregation. We show that e-prop reached 100% survival rate matching backpropagation through time, whereas both the fixed reservoir and the reward-modulated STDP, though the closest to biological cultures, plateaued at a much lower performance (~43.7% and ~11% survival). We explore in further detail which trends a real culture could and could not produce until now. Finally we demonstrate brief bipedal stepping on a full size humanoid H1-2.

Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation (ROGUE-AI)

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Agents based on large language models (LLMs) can now delete files and send payments on behalf of their users. News media reports routinely describe failures of these agents (e.g., Replit's coding agent deleting a production database), but a report often only says what went wrong and rarely (in)validates the incident or finds its root cause. Incident catalogs collect these reports, and benchmarks record whether agents fail, but neither replicates an incident nor tests the cause its report might identify. In this paper, we present ROGUE-AI (Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation), a multi-agent framework that closes the above gap and rebuilds each reported incident offline together with a matched control that lacks only the blamed cause, and runs agents on both. Our results suggest that ROGUE-AI is able to reproduce the reported harm in 89% of its 108 attempted replications. Agents built on five current GPT, Claude, Gemini and DeepSeek models made the reported mistake in 18% to 33% of runs, against at most 1% without the blamed cause. Of the 72 incidents with real-world harm, 62 had no underlying attacker, and in these, agents did what their task asked for but missed an ordinary hazard, such as a database shared with the live site. Replications without an attacker caused more harm than replications of prompt injection for all five models. Defenses against prompt injection look for injected instructions, but these replications contain none. Further, a warning after each tool output that names the hazard and its consequence lowered harm for all five models, whereas a note of the same length that does not mention the hazard left most of the harm in place. Built from real incidents, the ROGUE-AI replications form a reusable audit framework that vendors can rerun before each new release.

HireWatch: Evaluating LLM Compliance with U.S. Employment Law Under Contextual Pressure

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The deployment of large language models (LLMs) in hiring pipelines is growing, yet existing evaluations generally overlook their compliance with legal rules. To fill this gap, we introduce HireWatch, an evaluation that assesses whether LLMs comply with U.S. laws that govern the questions that job candidates may be asked. We construct a 200-item bank of lawful and unlawful interview questions, whose legal classification is based on U.S. federal statutes, regulations, and agency guidance. We instruct LLMs to select questions from this bank in preparing for job interviews, across a set of various occupations and contexts, and report the fraction of unlawful questions that LLMs select. Evaluation of 18 closed and open-weight LLMs across ~100,000 trials under different types of contextual pressure reveals that LLMs' legal compliance is brittle. While LLMs generally avoid selecting unlawful questions in low-pressure contexts, legal violations increase sharply under higher pressure. Explicit prompts to comply with law can improve compliance rates, but do not altogether eliminate legal violations. We publicly release our code and data at https://github.com/hnghiem-nlp/hirewatch_repo.

Asuka-FM: A System-Architectural Co-Design Framework for Efficient Text-to-Image Pretraining on Consumer Hardware

📅 2026-11-19 17:12
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Frontier text-to-image (T2I) foundation models predominantly rely on multi-billion parameter Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) pretrained across massive compute clusters, creating an accessibility barrier for academic laboratories. When training DiTs from scratch on single consumer GPUs (e.g., an NVIDIA RTX 4090 with 24\,GB VRAM), practitioners encounter three primary bottlenecks: quadratic attention sequence costs, Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation graph breaks induced by variable aspect-ratio bucketing, and an autoencoder throughput wall where frozen Variational Autoencoder (VAE) operations dominate step latency. In this work, we introduce \textbf{Asuka-FM}, an end-to-end system and architectural framework demonstrating that competitive dual-stream DiTs ($\sim$206M parameters) can be pretrained from scratch on a single consumer GPU in $\sim$160 GPU hours for under \$55. Asuka-FM integrates four core designs: (i) an AdaLN-free dual-stream DiT backbone reducing parameter count; (ii) structured sequence sparsification via Sparse-Dense Residual Fusion (SPRINT) to cut attention FLOPs by over 50\%; (iii) continuous aspect-normalized Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) coupled with shifted square crop training, guaranteeing static tensor shapes while unlocking zero-shot virtual camera control; and (iv) a pixel adaptation phase utilizing a lightweight (10M parameter) Patch Detailer Head operating under velocity regression ($v$-prediction). Evaluated on open-domain benchmarks without benchmark-specific fine-tuning, Asuka-FM achieves GenEval 0.588 / DPG 78.96 scores at 256px and GenEval 0.609 / DPG 78.51 scores at 512px, setting a new Pareto-optimal efficiency standard for democratic foundation modeling.

When Objectives and Evaluators Disagree: Evaluating Transfer for Post-hoc Demographic Framing Mitigation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Post-hoc rewriting is used to reduce demographic disparities in LLM-generated text when the source model is inaccessible. A rewrite can look successful merely because it improved its own target or satisfied its selector. We study this as a transfer problem on 660 gender pairs and 1,980 age contrasts of demographic-conditioned climate messages. A rewriter proposes edits, a selection model chooses among constraint-satisfying candidates, and an independent evaluator uninvolved in either rates pairwise framing disparity. A fixed policy that always targets the message with the lower Persuasion Bias Index (PBI) transfers inconsistently across axes and outcome aggregations. An adaptive policy that proposes edits on both sides but accepts at most one significantly improves on that rule, applied to the same candidates, in seven of eight comparisons across two evaluator families, mostly by editing more pairs, not by guided direction choice, at higher preservation risk. A policy editing both messages gives the largest point reductions on fewer pairs. These two policies reduce disparity across a preservation-threshold sweep; absolute ratings vary across evaluator families and prompts. Shrinking the PBI gap increases dimension-mean disparity under both evaluator families. Mitigation should be reported as transfer across objectives, evaluators and their prompts, and editing policies.

One Framework to Diagnose Them All: A Stratified Benchmark for Bengali LLM Hallucination

📅 2026-11-19 16:15
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Hallucination research in low-resource languages fragments easily: each newly named failure mode prompts its own dataset, spending scarce annotators on incomparable resources. We argue for consolidation and test it at community scale. Alikbachan is a Bengali hallucination-detection benchmark stratified along three crossed axes: a human-validated cultural-distance band, six task types, and a five-family, 32-mode hallucination taxonomy. It comprises a harvested 2,516-item test set (Phase 1) and a quota-constructed, gold-labeled 5,000-item set (Phase 2). The stratification is diagnostic. Four zero-shot LLM judges vary by over 30 macro-F1 points across strata, and the item-level predictions of 99 Phase-1 and 32 Phase-2 competition systems reproduce the profile: discourse-level omission defeats judges and most teams alike, whereas only a minority over-flag negative controls. The judges' verdict probabilities rank items well (ROC-AUC 0.74-0.95) but are overconfident (ECE 0.04-0.29). An open competition on Alikbachan drew 222 teams and 3,566 submissions, and a single-prompt 32B judge reaches the 57th percentile. Of the 25 teams in both phases, 24 regress on Phase 2 (mean $-17.6$ points) while every zero-shot judge improves, and Phase-1 rank barely predicts Phase-2 rank (Spearman $ρ=0.35$), so a Phase-1 leaderboard alone would have crowned a different winner.

Near-Quadratic Sample Compression for All VC Classes

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The sample compression conjecture is a longstanding open problem in learning theory, originating in the work of Littlestone and Warmuth in the 1980s. At its heart is a connection between simplification and generalization: if a predictor consistent with a training sample is defined using only a small part of that sample, then it is likely to generalize. Sample compression schemes formalize this idea, and many natural learning algorithms can be analyzed this way. The sample compression conjecture of Floyd and Warmuth (1995) asserts that every binary concept class of VC dimension $d$ admits a sample compression scheme of size $O(d)$; a weaker conjecture asks only for size polynomial in $d$. Both have remained open: the first bound depending only on $d$, due to Moran and Yehudayoff (2016), is exponential in $d$. We prove the weaker conjecture by constructing a sample compression scheme of size $O(d^2 \log^2 d)$.

Duopra: From Full-Duplex Conversation to Full-Duplex Agency

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Full-duplex interaction models exhibit strong conversational agency, enabling natural, low-latency turn-taking, backchannels, and interruptions. However, completing tasks further requires task agency: timely tool invocation and argument revision following user corrections. Frontend-backend agents separate initiation from execution: the frontend decides whether and what to request, while the backend determines how. Execution can be offloaded, but unsent requests cannot be executed, and incomplete ones can misdirect the backend. On Full-Duplex-Bench v3 (FDB v3), most failures of Realtime-Venus-Audio + Harness in our streaming pipeline occur before a domain tool executes. We introduce Duopra, a full-duplex interaction model that learns task execution alongside conversational control. Duopra treats the backend as a tool, accepting a self-contained natural-language request. Direct calls and backend delegation share an action interface and requirements for faithful initiation and correct execution. Its three-stage training combines protocol SFT, full-duplex RL rewarding timely and complete execution, and projector-only repair of talker conditioning after thinker updates. With the same backend, Pass@1 rises from 4.7% to 44.7%; Duopra achieves 47.7% calling tools directly (26.0% after SFT) and 49.3% with a DeepSeek backend. It improves three FDB v1.0 takeover rates over the base model. These results show that task agency can coexist with key conversational agency.

Beyond Covers and Titles: Evidence-First Ranking for Robust Cold-Start Recommendation

📅 2026-11-19 15:25
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Short-video platforms rank new uploads before anyone has watched them, using content towers that read a video's cover, title and frames. Existing attacks on content-aware recommenders perturb pixels or features and ignore platform re-encoding, and existing defenses train against norm-bounded perturbations that neither match real edits nor preserve recommendation quality. We show with REAP, a realizable attack through {a simulated platform channel (H.264 transcoding, rescaling, cropping and JPEG)}, that packaging edits raise a cold video by 32.0 rank-percentile points in unrelated audiences. We then observe that cold-start ranking does not need the packaging at all: a tower that sees only the video body ranks cold items best. Building on this, we propose Evidence-First Ranking (EFR), which scores cold videos from their body, admits packaging only through a support-gated residual bounded by $γ$, and merges cold and warm rankings by rank percentile, yielding an exact certificate against any packaging edit. On MicroLens-100K, EFR cuts the attack gain by 81% and raises cold-start Recall@100 from 0.1133 to 0.170, while keeping the merged NDCG@10 within 4% of the undefended system.

Attention Drift Regularization with Adaptive Initialization for Domain-Robust Visual Recognition

📅 2026-11-19 15:09
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a pretrained source model to unlabeled target domains without source data access, yet two critical failure modes remain unaddressed. First, attention drift: source-learned attention maps shift toward spurious or background features under distribution shift, destabilizing feature representations. Second, domain-gap-agnostic initialization: applying a single fixed source model uniformly across all target domains ignores domain gap severity, causing premature overfitting to noisy pseudo-labels under large shifts. We propose DGA-CAFC to address both failures. The Counterfactual Attention-based Feature Consistency (CAFC) module enforces feature stability by minimizing the discrepancy between representations computed under learned attention and those under counterfactual-inspired normalized perturbations, thereby promoting domain-robust features independent of spurious attention shifts. The Domain-Gap Aware Source Model Selection (DGASS) module estimates domain gap severity via target prediction entropy and selects the source model variant whose confidence level best matches the estimated gap, reducing negative transfer and stabilizing pseudo-label quality. Crucially, CAFC transfers beyond SFDA: applied as a lightweight regularizer to standard domain generalization objectives without target data. Extensive experiments across SFDA and DG benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently improves over recent baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of the proposed framework. Project page is available at https://visdomlab.github.io/DGA_CAFC

Flow Matching from Non-Gaussian Sources: A Theory of Efficient Sampling

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Flow matching is a versatile framework for generative modeling that transports a source distribution to a target data distribution. A key advantage is its flexibility in the choice of source distribution, such as heavy-tailed and multimodal sources. Yet finite-step sampling guarantees for non-Gaussian sources remain limited. We establish a nonasymptotic convergence theory for Euler discretization of flow matching with linear interpolation. Suppose the source distribution on $\mathbb{R}^d$ has an everywhere positive, continuously differentiable density, finite total variance $\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}$, and an $L$-Lipschitz score. Using the population velocity field and a suitable time grid, this sampler achieves total variation error at most $\varepsilon$ in $\widetilde{O}(Ld\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}/\varepsilon)$ iterations, provided the target distribution satisfies a mild second-moment condition. These assumptions cover, among many examples, source distributions such as multivariate Student's $t$ distributions with more than two degrees of freedom and finite Gaussian mixtures with shared covariance, while accommodating target distributions with neither a smooth density nor bounded support.

AeroBelief: Budgeted First-Action Verification for Evidence-Guided Aerial Sensing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Aerial sensing must combine uncertain overhead context with current observations while budgeting prediction, movement and image processing. AeroBelief uses the next executable action as the unit of verification against reference scores. It groups candidate views by first action, scans them with an inexpensive proposer, and selectively queries a structured world model (WM). Calibrated group bounds support strict commitment; a six-query controller connects selective prediction with belief updates, acceptance checks and scheduled sensing. Across four layout families, 40 worlds and 960 closed-loop executions, the AeroBelief controller completes 75.42% of tasks, compared with 73.33% for exhaustive WM scoring and 65.83% for line-of-sight geometry, using 91.81% fewer reference-candidate evaluations than exhaustive scoring. In fixed-reference replay with a six-query cap, proposer-scaled anchored bounds raised first-action certificate availability from 36.25% to 54.69% and reduced mean queries per state by 13.54% relative to unit-scale anchored bounds. A complete ground-hosted pipeline processes 2,685 self-collected 20-megapixel unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) images from seven DJI Mavic 3M routes. Context-guided admission and feedback retain all eight independently supported target confirmations with 76.05% fewer processed images and 70.02% less accumulated active processing service than uncapped all-frame processing; feedback saves another 5.17% of images relative to static context. The studies connect budgeted action selection with evidence-driven processing of recorded flight imagery.

An AI-Powered Multiverse Analysis of Empirical Finance Research: Evidence from Reused Natural Experiments

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Empirical finance research often reuses natural experiments to study other outcomes, raising concerns about multiple testing and publication bias. We use AI agents to run a multiverse analysis of 324 papers that reuse 13 natural experiments. The agents rebuild the data, reproduce each paper's main estimate and re-estimate it across grids totaling 62,409 estimated defensible specifications. Based on the reproductions and the specification grids, we develop measures of empirical robustness. We reproduce 42% of the main estimates of claims with a signed effect under the papers' own specifications. Across all defensible specifications, on average 25% keep the published sign and have $p < 0.10$. Across experiments, claims based on regression discontinuity are more robust than difference-in-differences claims. Within experiments, claims about first-order effects, claims with larger published $t$-statistics, claims whose authors' data or code we find, claims in top journals, and claims with fewer earlier papers on the same experiment are more robust. Claims on experiments that more papers reuse are not less robust on average. Our findings quantify how much published claims depend on analysis choices, thereby showing that large-scale multiverse analysis becomes feasible with agentic coding.

On the Impact of Feature Reduction Techniques on Adversarial Attacks

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Adversarial attacks have posed significant threats to machine learning models by exploiting their inherent vulnerabilities. Researchers have established an interdisciplinary field called adversarial machine learning to explore these vulnerabilities and develop corresponding defense strategies. Feature reduction strategies are used to decrease the dimensionality of the data in order to reduce training time, avoid the curse of dimensionality, prevent overfitting, and mitigate redundant features. However, their effect on adversarial robustness across different classifiers and attack scenarios remains insufficiently explored. This study aims to analyze the effect of feature reduction techniques on the adversarial robustness of a diverse set of machine learning models under poisoning, backdoor, and universal adversarial perturbation attacks on the APT dataset. These models include support vector machine, decision tree, random forest, multi-layer perceptron, and k-nearest neighbors. The attained results are compared with scenarios where different feature reduction techniques are applied, including principal component analysis, permutation importance ranking, recursive feature elimination, and univariate mutual information. Metrics such as accuracy, F1-score, and mitigation score are employed to evaluate the effect of these techniques on adversarial robustness. The results demonstrate that permutation importance ranking and univariate mutual information generally provide the most consistent robustness improvements, while other methods exhibit more variable effects depending on the classifier and attack scenario.

EverydayBench: Evaluating Satisfactory Completion of Live User-Facing Digital Work

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Everyday digital tasks, from selecting products and planning trips to conducting research and creating artifacts, require AI agents to deliver useful outcomes rather than merely retrieve web information. Yet evaluating these outcomes on the live web is challenging, as a single request may admit multiple valid results, and changing web information can alter which results remain valid over time. Although existing benchmarks address aspects of this setting, they do not fully integrate predefined success criteria, current evidence verification, and deliverable-quality assessment for agent-selected outcomes. To address this gap, we introduce EverydayBench, a benchmark of 77 human-authored tasks spanning eight domains of everyday life and work. Each task specifies success criteria before execution without prescribing an answer or workflow, allowing agents to select their own outcomes. To evaluate submissions under fixed success criteria, EverydayBench combines (1) deterministic checks of explicit constraints, (2) verification of claims against agent-cited webpages retrieved at evaluation time, and (3) rubric-based assessment of final deliverable quality. We further validate the protocol through human judgments, repeated scoring under fixed evidence, and controlled grounding failures. Across 23 reported agent-model configurations, Claude Opus 5.5 and Claude Fable 5.1 tie for the highest strict pass rate at 54.55%; a controlled GPT-6 Sol effort sweep ranges from 29.87% at low effort to 45.45% at max effort. Reliability varies substantially by task type: GPT-5.6 Sol leads lookup, Fable 5.1 leads planning, Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.1 tie on comparison, and GPT-6 Astra and Sonnet 5.5 tie on customized deliverables. Together, these findings highlight the difficulty of delivering complete, well-supported, and usable outcomes for everyday user requests.

Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning for Mammography Vision--Language Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Medical vision--language models (VLMs) often need to identify small, subtle lesions within high-resolution images containing extensive normal anatomy and redundant visual information. Visual-token compression improves efficiency, but preserving salient regions alone can discard the context needed for diagnostic interpretation. This context can extend across images: in bilateral mammography, the opposite breast provides a patient-specific anatomical reference. Yet retaining such evidence does not ensure that the model uses it effectively. We propose Bilateral Evidence Selection and Reasoning (BESR), which connects evidence preservation with comparative assessment through a bilateral-guided token selector and a bilateral reward. Under a shared token budget, the selector jointly prioritizes lesion appearance and its local and bilateral context, while the reward encourages assessments that benefit from the preserved comparison. Experiments on a bilateral dataset constructed from CBIS-DDSM and on VinDr-Mammo demonstrate improved evidence retention and diagnostic performance under constrained visual-token budgets. Inference benchmarking further shows reduced prefill latency.

Explainable AI for Solar PV Power Forecasting: From Model Selection to Trustworthy, Reproducible Deployment

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Machine learning (ML) is now a standard tool for forecasting the output of solar photovoltaic (PV) plants, yet published accuracy figures are often hard to compare and harder to trust. Many studies validate with random splits, report no reference forecast, and present feature attributions without saying what they can and cannot show. This paper reviews the main families of ML methods for PV power forecasting and resource assessment, from tree ensembles to deep and foundation models, and sets out a reproducible workflow that combines automated model search with explainable AI. The workflow is then tested on an openly available dataset: a 16.32 kWp plant in Porto, Portugal, forecast one day ahead from a 4 km numerical weather prediction grid. Over a full test year, the best models reach a normalised root mean square error (nRMSE) of 11.6% of installed capacity, about half the error of smart persistence. The choice of input features changed accuracy far more than the choice among strong algorithms, and shuffled cross-validation was mildly optimistic. After conformal calibration, the nominal 80% prediction intervals covered 81.7% of observations, against 64.5% before. SHAP rankings stayed stable when the model was retrained on different periods but shifted when correlated inputs were removed, so attributions should be read as properties of a model rather than of the plant. The paper closes with practical lessons and open problems, including deployment on edge hardware and the effect of a changing climate.

Language-Oriented Semantic Modeling for Target Language Extraction

📅 2026-11-19 16:04
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Target Language Extraction (TLE) aims to isolate speech in a target language from multilingual mixtures. Existing approaches primarily rely on acoustic modeling with language identity labels, which provide only coarse categorical guidance. In this work, we propose a language-oriented semantic modeling framework that augments acoustic extraction with language-specific semantic representations. A pre-trained speech semantic model is adapted via language-aware prompt conditioning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning to generate target-language-focused semantic representations from the mixture. {These capture high-level semantic structure of the acoustics and they} are integrated into the extraction model to provide structured semantic guidance beyond identity labels. Experiments on the CommonVoiceMix dataset show that incorporating semantic representations consistently improves over acoustic-only baselines, achieving up to 1.16 dB SI-SDR gain.

SenseAlign: Learning Decision-Aligned Predictive Representations for Active Urban Sensing with UAVs

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Active urban sensing orchestrates a mobile sensor fleet to proactively inspect a dynamic road network and gather critical information for system management. This problem simultaneously requires understanding the complex spatio-temporal evolution and making decisions with partial observations. In this work, we focus on active sensing in a large-scale urban road network with utilities grounded in dynamic traffic states, while prior work has rarely considered these factors jointly. We propose \emph{SenseAlign}, a Reinforcement Learning-based (RL) framework that learns decision-aligned predictive representations for coordinating an urban sensing drone fleet. SenseAlign first trains a privileged teacher agent that exploits future ground-truth traffic for optimal sensing choices. The student agent has an auxiliary branch that learns to predict future traffic with partial observations, in addition to the RL-trained actor and critic branches. During student learning, our framework matches the action distributions of the teacher under ground-truth and student-predicted future traffic, while keeping the teacher's observations unchanged. Such a design shapes the latent space for decision-making instead of prediction alone. At inference time, the student agent has no overhead as all this learning is done in training. We conduct comprehensive experiments with the real road network of central Barcelona containing 1570 road segments, and demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms the baselines on multiple utility formulations. The codebase will be made publicly available upon paper acceptance.

Living off the Local Model: Abusing Browser-Embedded Language Models for Post-Exploitation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Browsers now ship local language models to ordinary endpoints. Google includes Gemini Nano in Chrome to enable productivity features such as summarization, with inference running entirely on the device. These browser-embedded models provide new living-off-the-land opportunities for attackers, each instance a potential post-exploitation engine already installed, signed by the vendor, and invisible to network monitoring. The Gemini model is multimodal, capable of processing files, analyzing screenshots, or conversing with an attacker. We show that it can be driven from the host using only interpreters that ship with the operating system and find that the one-time user-activation check before the model is downloaded is easily satisfied with a simulated click or bypassed entirely.We measure and report the current model's ability to identify credentials on the host, detect PII in email, classify images, and call custom tools.
