Fast Self-Supervised Generative CT Denoising via Few-Step Sampling

📅 2026-11-19 16:34
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Low-dose and thin-slice computed tomography (CT) images suffer from elevated noise that obscures anatomical detail, while clean references for supervised denoising are often unavailable. Although Noise2Noise permits self-supervised training from noisy pairs, direct regression tends to oversmooth fine structure. Averaging multiple independently generated samples mitigates this blur, though repeating multi-step diffusion sampling is costly. We introduce Noise2Noise with Improved Mean Flow (N2N-iMF) to accelerate this process via few-step sampling. N2N-iMF learns to generate one noisy image conditioned on its paired counterpart; at inference, independent outputs are sampled and averaged to suppress noise. We evaluated image quality and sampling efficiency on simulated thin-slice brain and low-dose abdominal CT across four sampling methods and averaging counts from 1 to 20, and validated the approach on real-world photon-counting CT (PCCT) brain scans. With ten samples per conditioning input and three network evaluations per sample, N2N-iMF achieved a mean absolute error of 2.60 HU on brain CT and 14.02 HU on abdominal CT, outperforming 50-evaluation denoising diffusion implicit model (DDIM) sampling (2.91 and 14.53 HU). On the same GPU, per-output generation time dropped from 7.29 to 0.43 s. Across eight PCCT cases, N2N-iMF reached a mean gray-white matter contrast-to-noise ratio of 1.341, versus 0.916 for Noise2Noise and 1.176 for DDIM. N2N-iMF accelerates self-supervised generative CT denoising without distillation while keeping the number of averaged outputs adjustable at inference.

SEEC: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Self-Supervised External-Eye Representation Learning

📅 2026-11-19 16:41
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

External-eye photography captures clinically relevant periocular anatomy at low acquisition cost, yet no large-scale standardized resource exists for evaluating self-supervised external-eye representations. We introduce a 4.35-million-image Standardized External-Eye Corpus (SEEC) derived from fourteen heterogeneous public face datasets, together with a benchmark for external-eye representation learning. For evaluation, we provide held-out evaluation distributions, landmark-annotated anatomical probe sets, and clinical disease-classification experiments spanning ptosis, thyroid eye disease, and craniofacial syndromes. We evaluate representations at two complementary levels: (a) the representation level, by assessing embedding geometry across multiple data distributions, and (b) the functional level, by assessing anatomical recovery, out-of-distribution anatomical transfer, and disease classification. Our models include ResNet-101 and ViT-B encoders pretrained using InfoNCE, VICReg, and LeJEPA objectives and compared with DINOv2 and MAE. Our results indicate that representation-level and functional-level evaluations are dissociable: broader embedding-space utilization does not consistently correspond to anatomical transfer or disease-classification performance, and no single encoder is universally optimal. Domain-specific external-eye encoders outperform DINOv2 and MAE on cross-dataset anatomical transfer despite being pretrained at substantially smaller scale, while external-eye-pretrained ViT models match or outperform these general-purpose baselines on frozen disease-classification probes. We release benchmark code, evaluation scripts, and preprocessing pipelines to reproduce SEEC at https://monkeygobah.github.io/seec-project/

EgoCaptioner: Towards Comprehensive and Fine-Grained Egocentric Video Captioning

📅 2026-11-19 16:39
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Egocentric video captioning requires fine-grained detail and accurate timing, yet current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often miss brief actions, confuse their order, or misinterpret the wearer's motion. Existing caption formats also offer limited support for combining detailed narration with precise timing. We present EgoCaptioner, which pairs a video-level overview of the setting, participants, and the wearer's overall activity with timed steps whose narration adapts to varying perceptual demands and marks key moments at specific timestamps. We develop a coarse-to-fine annotation pipeline that establishes event structure before expanding step descriptions, yielding 50,000 training examples from eight egocentric video sources. Complementary rewards assess factual accuracy and coverage, measure step-level temporal alignment, and penalize fragmented or redundant narration, guiding caption refinement through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). To improve the reliability of factual supervision, we supplement caption references with pose-derived motion facts when sensor data are available. Across five egocentric benchmarks, EgoCaptioner achieves leading performance among evaluated open-source models in caption quality, caption-conditioned question answering, and temporal alignment. It also surpasses Gemini 3.1 Pro on PhysCapBench and all evaluated proprietary baselines on Minerva-Ego under the caption-to-QA protocol. Ablations support the complementary roles of factual and temporal rewards and show that coarse-to-fine annotation provides more effective supervision than single-pass annotation.

BADAS-3.0: Real-Time Driving Perception and Planning from One Camera

📅 2026-11-19 15:49
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We ask how far a driving model can get with one camera and real-time latency. BADAS-3.0 is a 1B-parameter video encoder with four small heads. From two seconds of one forward camera, it predicts the current speed, the metric path just driven, a collision-risk score and six candidate futures over 6.4 seconds, in under 50 ms. Given the car's current speed, BADAS-3.0 has 44% lower minADE$_6$ over five trajectory benchmarks than the 34B Alpamayo 2 Super, which reads six cameras and runs over 100x slower. The gain comes from camera rigs that neither model was trained on. On the rig they share, the two are on par. Existing benchmarks are mostly nominal driving, so we add two open-loop safety benchmarks, one of real dashcam collisions and near-misses and one of Euro NCAP scenarios. On both, BADAS-3.0 has a higher pass rate than every published planner we tested.

Visual Attribution Limits and Graded Provenance Auditing in Commercial Video-Generation APIs

📅 2026-11-19 17:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Commercial video-generation APIs bill by model and tier, while intermediaries could substitute cheaper models, lower tiers, or cached clips. What can a delivered file establish about its origin? We combine measurements of passive attribution and cryptographic provenance into a graded audit. We test attribution probes and zero-shot detectors under re-encoding, downscaling, and frame-rate changes, and survey C2PA and GB 45438 metadata across 19 commercial endpoints. Only authenticated provenance supports accusations: tier contradictions or duplicate signed instances under fresh-generation contracts, excluding retries and idempotent returns. Unverifiable signed-endpoint deliveries trigger original-file demands under pass-through clauses; archive-reuse and delivery-parameter screens support review only. Six-model container and bitstream accuracy falls from 83.9% on raw files to 45.3% after uniform re-encoding, using different feature sets. Subsampling real references from 24 to 3 fps raises pooled D3 AUROC from 0.845 to 0.988. Only BytePlus authenticates tier claims under our pinned policy, including 187/187 audited deliveries across three Seedance tiers; tested remuxing and re-encoding leave processed Seedance files unverifiable. The audit identifies authenticated contradictions in 100/100 raw 720p signed tier-swap cases, with zero observed false flags on the tested honest pools.

MM-SystemOneBias: Evaluating Bias in Multimodal Jev-Style System One Decision Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

When multimodal decision models determine actions, moderation outcomes, or quality judgments, their reliability depends on preserving semantic decisions across presentation changes, ignoring irrelevant additions, and distinguishing information consistent with task evidence from information that conflicts with it. Existing evaluations study decision-interface biases and multimodal model behavior in separate settings, leaving no common protocol to compare multimodal decision models with their backbones on practical tasks. We introduce \textbf{MM-SystemOneBias}, which evaluates 13 models on 5,323 original instances from nine public datasets and 11 task scenarios using six paired interventions: option order, option label, external judgment, image--text description, irrelevant information, and rating scale. Across seven matched decision-model--backbone pairs, decision models are more stable under option-label, irrelevant-information, and rating-scale changes in all seven pairs; six pairs also show lower option-order flip rates. They incur smaller accuracy losses under incorrect external judgments in all seven pairs and conflicting descriptions in six. In six pairs, they gain less accuracy from correct external judgments and show less favorable accuracy changes under consistent descriptions. Among pairs with unchanged semantic decisions, mean absolute changes in answer probabilities range from 4.56 to 11.55 percentage points, revealing input effects that accuracy alone misses.

Law at the Speed of AI: Critical Legal Infrastructure for the Machine Age: Autonomy Under Law Architecture (AULA)

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The global deployment of autonomous AI agents within and across organisations exceeds the governance infrastructure available to constrain it. Agent communication and authentication protocols, principally the Model Context Protocol and the Agent2Agent Protocol, are maturing quickly, but maturity at the protocol layer does not produce legal legibility at the governance layer. Runtime enforcement written by the deploying organisation fixes the resources an AI agent can reach, not the authority under which it acts. Legal legibility is the capacity of a legal system to recognise, interpret and regulate activity by mapping it onto legal personhood, property, rights and liabilities. Existing (analogue) legal infrastructure cannot supply it for autonomous deployment. The resulting deficit is bidirectional. It concerns the attribution of liability when an AI agent causes harm, and the allocation of rights, including data and economic rights, generated by agentic activity and by the AI agent itself as a source of value and form of property. This paper characterises that deficit as the legal attribution gap, a material and unaddressed risk to economies, organisations and citizens that is magnified in cross-organisational multi-agent deployment. The paper describes the Autonomy Under Law Architecture (AULA), in which AI systems and data assets are registered as legal objects anchored to one or more identified legal persons, governed by cryptographically tethered smart legal instruments and evaluated at runtime. Each smart legal instrument can carry a legal context clause, through which the legislation, regulation and judicial decisions in force at the time of the act are given practical and legal effect in that evaluation. Infrastructure of this kind is a precondition for the continued function of any legal, property and economic system with both human and autonomous machine stakeholders.

FlexVSR: Continuous-Scale Super-Resolution for Streaming Video Generation

📅 2026-11-19 14:29
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Real-time streaming video generation has gained rapid momentum in live content creation and interactive media. Deploying such systems in practice requires flexible scaling factors to accommodate diverse source resolutions, target displays, and computational budgets. However, extending fixed-scale generative video super-resolution (VSR) to varying target scales poses two challenges: downsampling from a larger fixed scale incurs redundant cost, while re-optimizing the diffusion transformer (DiT) is costly and alters the shared generative prior. To address both, we propose FlexVSR, a decoupled framework for continuous-scale streaming generative VSR with supervision at only a few scales. Our key insight is that naively resampling fixed-scale latents onto a new target grid induces scale-dependent input mismatch, which can be effectively addressed through parameter-efficient, scale-aware adaptation of the latent interface without updating the DiT. To this end, FlexVSR introduces ReLSA (Refined Latent Scale Adaptation), a lightweight latent adapter that constructs and refines target-grid latents before they enter the DiT, enabling a shared frozen VSR prior to generalize across continuous scales with only 0.274M trainable parameters and an adaptation cost of 7.3 H100 GPU-hours, while introducing no additional temporal dependencies and preserving the native causal streaming protocol. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive or superior reconstruction and temporal quality, lower latency than most native-scale streaming baselines and offline methods, and effective generalization to unseen continuous scales, filling the continuous-scale gap in real-time streaming generative VSR.

Learning Reconstruction Errors as Spatiotemporal Evidence for AI-Generated Video Detection

📅 2026-11-19 13:45
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The proliferation of AI-generated videos has raised growing concerns about misinformation, fraud, and potential societal risks, creating an urgent need for detection. Reconstruction-error-based methods have shown strong empirical performance for AI-generated image detection by exploiting discrepancies between an input and its reconstructed version. Their extension to video detection, however, faces two challenges: (i) a fixed reconstructor is not optimized for the detection objective and may yield weak discriminative signals; and (ii) an additional raw-video feature stream is often introduced to model temporal dependencies, increasing architectural overhead. To address these challenges, we develop a detector that learns and exploits reconstruction errors as a unified source of spatiotemporal evidence. First, we jointly optimize a reconstructor and the downstream classifier to produce reconstruction errors that are more informative for detecting AI-generated videos. Second, we show that consecutive reconstruction errors alone carry temporal evidence, and thus a 3D convolution over them captures spatiotemporal evidence without a raw-video stream. Extensive experiments on over 30 existing AI-video generators demonstrate competitive performance against baselines.

Direct3D-V3: Latent-Free Diffusion Transformers in the Native Voxel Domain

📅 2026-11-19 17:58
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Native 3D generation has largely been built around latent diffusion, with recent advances concentrating on stronger variational autoencoders (VAE) and increasingly elaborate latent representations, from vecset and triplanes to structured sparse voxels. Despite their architectural differences, these methods all share the same premise: geometry is first compressed into a latent space and only then modeled by the generator. We take a different route and propose Direct3D-V3, a latent-free diffusion transformer (DiT) framework that discards the VAE altogether and operates in the native voxel domain, directly modeling raw SDF voxel grids during both training and sampling. To make this direct voxel formulation geometrically reliable, we introduce an implicit manifold regularization over neighboring voxels to suppress non-watertight surfaces, and convert predicted SDF grids into meshes via Marching Cubes so that rendered normal losses can supervise the model directly from its predicted SDF. To improve alignment between the conditioning image and generated geometry, Direct3D-V3 also predicts the camera parameters of the conditioning image as an auxiliary task, encouraging the DiT to learn the mapping from image observations to 3D shape. This latent-free design removes the reconstruction bottleneck, brings training under an objective in the native voxel domain, and yields meshes with substantially richer local detail. The predicted camera and native SDF voxel output further make Direct3D-V3 extensible to generative reconstruction, where diffusion is interleaved with normal-guided refinement to improve alignment with the observed view while the generative prior completes unseen regions. Project page: https://dreamtechai.github.io/Direct3D-V3.

Splice-Core: Low-Order-Bit-Splicing GEMM for Highly Energy-Efficient AI Inference Accelerators

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Splice-Core is a temporal-unary/binary (tub) hybrid general matrix multiplication (GEMM) unit that minimizes low-order accumulation latency, while retaining value-dependent execution for high-order activation bits. We present two novel processing element (PE) variants: (i) Splice-BP utilizing bit planes, and (ii) Splice-LUT, which selects a shared weight multiple in a single cycle. Compared to a binary systolic array, Splice-BP/Splice-LUT achieve 77.4%/76.4% geometric-mean tile energy reductions, respectively, across 26 INT4 convolutional neural network (CNN) and large language model (LLM) workloads in 45nm. Splice-LUT further increases geometric-mean tile throughput by 44.4% and reduces geometric-mean energy-delay product (EDP) by 6.13x across the 26 configurations. Across the evaluated W8A8 convolution and expert-projection workloads, Splice-BP and Splice-LUT reduce tile energy by 77.3-88.1%. Post-place-and-route INT4 yields 14%/9% smaller area for Splice-BP/Splice-LUT, compared to the systolic array.

MS-Point: Multi-Modal Hierarchical Spectral Learning for Point Cloud Understanding

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Point-image alignment learns useful object representations, but different local feature patterns can produce the same pooled descriptor and receive the same alignment signal. We introduce MS-Point, a cross-modal pretraining framework that directly supervises intermediate point features and their neighborhood responses. At selected encoder stages, we compute neighborhood averages and residuals before pooling and align their branch descriptors with image features. Branch-level objectives align each representation with its visual target, while cross-level consistency and adaptive fusion coordinate information across branches. A common interface of intermediate features and spatial neighborhoods supports both graph encoders and point Transformers. Evaluations across object classification, few-shot adaptation, dense prediction, and low-label scene learning demonstrate the transfer value of this design. Same-backbone comparisons and capacity-matched and computation-matched controls connect these improvements to the structure of supervision. Together, our findings support directly supervising intermediate features and neighborhood variation to learn transferable, geometry-aware representations across modalities.

Serialization Tax in Shared-Latent Exchangeable Decisions

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Accurate item probabilities can produce inaccurate group-level decisions. A system may score each item separately, yet act on whether enough hidden items succeed: show a slate, defer a panel decision, or inspect a batch. When items share an uncertain latent state, independent marginalization preserves means but removes dependence from the aggregate posterior. We call this error the serialization tax. For binary items sharing one success rate, classical convex-order comparisons give a more dispersed exact count law than any independent completion with the same total mean. We quantify variance and total-variation gaps, derive threshold separation under margin conditions, and connect posterior approximation to decision regret. Separate results cover non-binary outcomes, heterogeneous items, and sufficient posterior resolution. TaxScore identifies large hidden blocks with unresolved uncertainty near a decision boundary for routing to richer posterior heads. On pre-specified, hidden-target-free MovieLens 1M/10M audits, existing shared-latent heads improve high-score negative log-likelihood (NLL)/utility from 0.673/0.696 to 0.629/0.723 and from 0.670/0.691 to 0.639/0.715. Routing gives 0.006--0.012 full-population utility gains, with matched controls identifying their sources. Our code is available at https://github.com/SiminChang/Serialization-Tax-in-Shared-Latent-Exchangeable-Decisions.

Depth Pro 2: Sharp and Temporally Consistent Metric Monocular Depth

📅 2026-11-19 18:55
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present Depth Pro 2, an approach to estimating dense metric depth for images and video at full resolution. By analyzing prior methods for monocular depth estimation, we find that they commonly trade off temporal consistency and boundary sharpness: multi-frame methods favor consistency, single-frame methods favor sharpness. To overcome this trade-off, we train a single, scalable network that processes both images and video and predicts depth maps at native resolution. Depth Pro 2 produces depth maps that are metrically accurate, sharp, and temporally consistent all at once, outperforming prior work in all of these dimensions. We release a family of models that define a new Pareto frontier in terms of metric accuracy, sharpness, temporal consistency, and runtime. We release code and weights at https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-depth-pro-2/

SAM 3 Sees More Than It Detects: Promptable Concept Maps Beyond Categories

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Promptable segmenters answer what a thing is. Dense prediction tasks also ask class-agnostic questions, such as what stands out or hides, how light is blocked, transmitted or reflected, and where structure lies. Robots, surveillance systems and image editors build on their answers. We study promptable concept maps (PCM), which extend SAM 3's promptable concept segmentation from noun instances to maps of concepts named by short noun phrases such as `"transparent object", with "none" as an answer. PCM-Bench groups the standard test sets of seven such tasks by the question they ask. On it, we find that SAM 3 sees more than it detects. Its semantic path responds to many of these concepts, yet a low image-level presence score can block every detection of its instance path. We present UNVEIL, which trains a copy of SAM 3's semantic path for these maps, with LoRA on copies of its last blocks. A separate presence decoder learns from verified negatives whether a concept occurs. With one text phrase per query, UNVEIL maps region concepts such as salient and camouflaged objects, shadows, transparent objects and mirrors. The same weights map thin structures such as edges and skeletons as well as object categories. Run next to SAM 3, it leaves SAM 3's own outputs unchanged. On each of the seven tasks, UNVEIL reaches the highest score among the compared promptable models, including SAM 3's official fine-tuning and pipelines built on multimodal LLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/yuhuan-wu/UNVEIL.

RegionAgent: Two-Stage Preference Optimization for Regional Image Restoration

📅 2026-11-19 14:29
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Images under non-uniform coupled degradations require diverse restoration capabilities for different degraded regions. Existing methods employ globally uniform operations, potentially overlooking regional differences and causing incomplete restoration or even damage to image content. Moreover, a degraded region may require successive restoration actions, and earlier actions may alter residual degradation characteristics or introduce artifacts, potentially hindering subsequent restoration. To address these challenges, we propose RegionAgent, an adaptive restoration framework that trains a Vision-Language Model (VLM) through two-stage preference learning to generate a region-specific restoration action at each iteration. Specifically, we first train the VLM to select an action suited to the current region through step-level Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), using preferences derived from immediate restoration feedback to supervise only the differing component in each action pair. To account for the effects of current actions on subsequent restoration, we then apply trajectory-level DPO, using preferences derived from complete trajectory scores to supervise the sole differing action in each trajectory pair. Furthermore, as conventional image-pair datasets do not directly provide preferences over regional restoration decisions, we construct a regional restoration preference dataset comprising 10K action pairs and 20K trajectory pairs for two-stage VLM training. Experiments across multiple datasets demonstrate strong restoration performance under both mixed and single degradations. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of two-stage preference learning in improving restoration quality.

HiUni: Harmonizing Understanding and Generation in Shared Visual Space

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Unified multimodal models aim to support visual understanding and generation within a single framework. However, prevailing approaches often separate their visual representations or backbone computation to accommodate the different requirements of these two tasks, which limits the degree of unification. In this paper, we present HiUni, which revisits this design choice and explores whether a pretrained vision language model can be directly extended into a unified model with extensive parameter sharing. Specifically, HiUni reuses the same language-aligned ViT and dense Transformer backbone for both capabilities, while confining task-specific computation to the output side. Yet such a design introduces two key challenges. Generation in pixel space is difficult to optimize, and semantic abstraction in pretrained ViT discards fine spatial details required for synthesis. To address these challenges, HiUni introduces a \emph{Pixel Diffusion Head} tailored to direct pixel-space optimization and a lightweight \emph{HiGate} pathway that provides fine-grained spatial details near the output stage. Extensive experiments show that HiUni achieves a similar average understanding score to the pretrained VLM while attaining competitive performance on image generation and editing. Controlled comparisons and analyses further demonstrate that a single dense backbone can effectively support both capabilities without task-specific backbone computation, highlighting a simpler path toward unified multimodal modeling.

DVBench: End-to-End Benchmark for Real-World UVM Design Verification Agents

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Design verification (DV) remains a major bottleneck in hardware development, with large-scale verification using the Universal Verification Methodology (UVM) being highly labor-intensive. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems offer new opportunities for DV automation. However, existing benchmarks primarily construct mutually decoupled subtasks, which deviates from the real-world workflow. In this paper, we present DVBench, the first end-to-end benchmark for Real-World UVM design verification agents. DVBench comprises 95 tasks across 9 functional domains and 5 difficulty levels, covering diverse verification scenarios and design scales. Each task requires agents to independently complete the full verification workflow. DVBench evaluates final testbenches via compilation, coverage, and bug detection, while assessing intermediate artifacts including feature lists, test plans, and reference models. We evaluate frontier LLMs (e.g. Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-6 Astra) and agent frameworks (e.g. Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode). Experimental results show that the best LLM-based agent (Codex \& GPT-6 Astra) can only achieve an overall success rate of 21.05\%, revealing substantial room for improvement in complex verification tasks.

SGAR: Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction via Sparse Geometry-Guided Attention

📅 2026-11-19 17:46
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Feed-forward models have been very successful in 3D reconstruction with ad hoc attention designs that relate all view patches densely. However, the cost of this dense global attention grows quadratically with the number of views, preventing the model from applying to larger-scale reconstruction settings. In this paper, we argue that most dense attention computations are unnecessary from a classical structure-from-motion perspective: 3D structures stem from geometrically matching regions in 2D. We bring this principle to learning-based feed-forward reconstruction with our Sparse Geometric Attention Reconstruction (SGAR) method. In SGAR, each patch token attends to only a small set of patch tokens from other views, chosen by a lightweight learned key selection module trained to predict cross-view 3D proximity of tokens. Thus, dense global attention is replaced by much more efficient geometric-similarity-guided sparse attention. SGAR is a general method that can be applied to different feed-forward models by fine-tuning. In our experiments, we show that SGAR is the fastest among both state-of-the-art dense and sparse methods and the most accurate sparse one. It runs up to 12-times faster than dense attention at 1,000 views with stable pose accuracy where existing sparse methods fail, and shows the best performance in reconstructing more complete geometries on the same time budget.

Multimodal Error Detection in Chest X-ray Reports: A Large Language Model Reading Image Evidence

📅 2026-11-19 17:46
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Rad-Linter checks chest X-ray reports against image-derived evidence to flag potential errors for review. A pretrained image classifier supplies scores for nine pathologies, and a 1.5B-parameter language model, fine-tuned with LoRA on 1,816 examples, checks their consistency with the report. We compare it with a keyword-and-negation rule baseline given the same reports and scores. Evaluation uses controlled report-score pairings from OpenI, with consistency labels derived from automatic report labels and score thresholds. Each counterfactual quad crosses two real reports with two real score vectors from other patients, creating two consistent and two inconsistent pairs. Every report and score vector occurs in both conditions, so either input alone permits only 50% accuracy. On 131 quads, Rad-Linter achieves 98.5% pair accuracy versus 80.5% for the rule baseline, and classifies all four pairs correctly in 94.4% of quads versus 56.5%. The checkers perform similarly on omission items, including unmentioned and negated findings; larger differences occur on the remaining item types, which require interpreting how findings are described. The same language model without fine-tuning remains near 50% accuracy. On a second set of 181 quads that also includes reports on which the automatic labelers disagree, pair accuracy is 93.4% versus 75.3%. These controlled results show that, given the same image-derived scores, a trained language model detects report-score inconsistencies more accurately than the tested rule baseline, with the advantage concentrated in interpreting how findings are worded.

No-Harm-Jury A Jury-Based Multi-Dimensional Harm Evaluation Framework for Medical AI Responses

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We present No-Harm-Jury, a jury-based automated evaluation framework that scores AI-generated medical responses across seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions using a panel of five diverse language models as independent jurors. Responses generated by Ministral-14B on 3,000 questions drawn from PubMedQA, MedQA, and MedMCQA are scored by five jurors across dimensions of informational, social, psychological, autonomy, economic, privacy, and epistemic harm; the 151 empty responses (98 MedQA, 53 MedM- CQA) are excluded, leaving 2,849 valid samples. The median aggregation robustly suppresses individual juror biases while maintaining coverage through complementary specialisation. No existing system combines all five prop- erties of No-Harm-Jury: automated scalable evaluation, five diverse LLM jurors, robust me- dian aggregation (with a reliability-weighted extension), seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions, and application to medical QA benchmarks.

Democratizing Cross-Project Knowledge for Transforming Water Governance and Climate Resilience: an Agentic AI Ecosystem

📅 2026-11-19 16:49
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

EU-funded projects produce extensive evidence in the form of deliverables, reports, and case studies. However, this knowledge is typically scattered across projects and document structures, making it difficult to locate relevant, contextual information when making decisions. This paper introduces an Agentic AI Ecosystem that consolidates heterogeneous project documentation into a domain-aware knowledge base and provides adaptive access through an agentic Retrieval-Augmented Generation pipeline. Documents from three EU-funded projects (IMPETUS, NATALIE, and InnWater) are reconstructed into hierarchical section trees and indexed with structured metadata. Retrieval is supported by two domain-specific embedding models fine-tuned on domain data (water and climate). On top of this knowledge base, a ReAct-style controller iteratively orchestrates retrieval actions by allocating retrieval budget across domain collections and accumulating evidence until a stopping criterion is met. In a retrieval benchmark over the corpus, the proposed approach improves Mean Reciprocal Rank by 26.7% (relative) compared to a single-domain embedding baseline, indicating more consistent retrieval of relevant evidence to support cross-project knowledge synthesis and climate-resilient decision-making.

CryoGaussian: Heterogeneous Cryo-EM Reconstruction Made Explicit with Gaussian Splatting

📅 2026-11-19 13:51
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Single-particle cryo-electron microscopy (cryo-EM) determines macromolecular structures from noisy particle images. However, rare, transient compositional states and subtle, continuous conformational variation are difficult to resolve. We present CryoGaussian, a Gaussian splatting method for heterogeneous cryo-EM reconstruction that fits an explicit Gaussian density representation directly to the particle images through a differentiable rasterizer rendering orthographic line integrals modulated by the contrast transfer function. The Gaussians are initialized randomly, with their number derived from the estimated spatial support of the molecular density and their minimum size bounded by the Nyquist frequency of the particle images. To capture the compositional and conformational variation across particle images, we learn particle-image and per-Gaussian latents that jointly modulate the base position, amplitude, and scale of each Gaussian. Each structural state is then reconstructed by fitting the Gaussians conditioned on its inferred latent. On CryoBench, CryoGaussian achieves state-of-the-art reconstruction accuracy for both compositional and conformational heterogeneity. On experimental data, it resolves a rare compositional state of the 50S ribosomal subunit (class C4, 1.3% of particles) with rRNA helix 68 docked in the absence of the central protuberance, and continuous conformational motion of the SF3b and helicase subcomplexes of the pre-catalytic spliceosome. We further optimize the rasterizer for additive cryo-EM projection, reducing training time by 12.8-28.0$\times$.

Beam Model Technical Report

📅 2026-11-19 16:28
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Beam is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 501.3B total parameters and 23.4B active parameters, specifically built and optimized for coding, reasoning, and agentic workloads. Beam delivers frontier level reasoning, while requiring 3-4x less inference compute than many models of its class. Pretrained on 23.8T diverse, curated, high-quality tokens, Beam features a 1M-token context window achieved through a novel two-stage midtraining phase, built on verifiable, correctness-anchored data. Post-training leveraged a large-scale, fully asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) campaign across 10.5K GB300 GPUs, generating over 100M rollouts over 4 weeks of training. We believe this is one of the largest scale RL runs conducted by any open lab to date. Across our evaluation suite, capabilities continued to improve as we increased RL compute, with no sign of a plateau. Supported by deep hardware-model co-design, FP8/NVFP4 quantization, and a 1.93B-parameter speculative decoding drafter (DFlash 2), Beam maximizes serving throughput with efficient, flexible deployment. Averaged across conversation turns and harm categories, Beam refuses or pivots on 88.9% of harmful requests while complying with 81.5% of harmless requests, achieving the highest refusal/pivot rate on harmful requests among open-weight models on the Pareto frontier.

FG-GraphBench: Benchmarking and Enhancing Fine-Grained Visual Understanding with Multimodal Scene Graphs

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Fine-grained visual understanding requires multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to recognize small visual entities and understand their relationships. Scene graphs offer a natural way to represent this information, organizing visual entities as nodes and their relationships as edges. However, we identify two challenges: existing scene graph benchmarks provide insufficient coverage of fine-grained entities and the relationships among them, and existing methods for fine-grained visual understanding have yet to effectively leverage such graph-structured information in MLLMs. To address these challenges, we introduce Fine-Grained Multimodal Scene Graph Benchmark (FG-GraphBench) and FiGRA. FG-GraphBench is a benchmark comprising three complementary tasks: fine-grained visual question answering (FG-QA), image captioning (FG-Caption), and image description generation (FG-Description). We develop a two-stage construction pipeline that discovers and grounds fine-grained entities and establishes their relations to construct the fine-grained multimodal scene graphs (MSGs) in FG-GraphBench. Building on these MSGs, we propose FiGRA, a Fine-grained Graph Representation Augmentation method for MLLMs. FiGRA adopts a two-stage training strategy. It encodes multimodal entity and relation features as continuous graph tokens, and integrates them into the MLLM. Experiments show that FiGRA significantly outperforms strong baselines across three tasks of FG-GraphBench. Further analyses confirm the effectiveness of FG-GraphBench and highlight the importance of preserving fine-grained entity and relation information for visual understanding.

Multiple Criteria Decision Making Under XORness

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

How should we make decisions under XORness? XORness is a class of uncertainty arising from indeterminacy, exclusivity, and hesitancy. We develop a multiple criteria decision making framework that preserves XORness throughout the decision process. We define XOR numbers and prove their fundamental arithmetic and algebraic properties. Building on these results, we extend the deterministic decision matrix to an XOR decision matrix and introduce a compatible normalization procedure. Further, we propose an additive XOR aggregation operator, which preserves indeterminacy, exclusivity, and hesitancy inherent in evaluations. In addition, we introduce an axiomatic XORness measure that quantifies the degree of uncertainty associated with global performance scores and develop a performance--XORness index to rank alternatives. We prove that this index induces a total preorder over the set of alternatives and identify the conditions for preserving dominance. The applicability of the developed framework is demonstrated through the risk assessment of cyberthreat scenarios in energy systems, and recommendations are derived using the XOR--weighted sum method.

Deterministic Temporal Embedding for Calcium Imaging Trace Extraction

📅 2026-11-19 18:00
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reliable extraction of calcium-dependent fluorescence traces in calcium imaging sequences requires identifying pixels whose fluorescence intensity reflects the same underlying cellular activity. We present a deterministic temporal embedding framework that maps pixel-wise fluorescence traces to a low-dimensional representation in which temporally coherent sources form spatially coherent regions. Starting from a fuzzy-neighborhood graph and spectral initialization, we optimize a class-balanced cross-entropy objective using exact sparse attraction and a deterministic particle-mesh decomposition of the dense repulsive interaction with sparse edge compensation, avoiding stochastic negative sampling. The resulting latent representation is spatially decoded into reliable source supports for trace extraction without imposing an explicit cellular shape prior. Experiments on synthetic and in vivo two-photon calcium imaging data demonstrate robust recovery of irregular and spatially touching sources together with efficient deterministic embedding optimization.

Studying AI Act Mentions: A first look at the impact of the European Union's AI regulation on Hugging Face

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The European Union's Artificial Intelligence Act (AI Act) entered into force in August 2024, and its obligations apply in stages. Obligations for providers of general-purpose AI models have applied since August 2025, and transparency rules since August 2026. These obligations affect developers who share and deploy models and applications on public platforms such as Hugging Face (HF), the largest platform for distributing AI artifacts. However, little is known about how practitioners respond to the regulation in practice. To address this gap, we conducted an empirical study of explicit references to the AI Act on HF. We collected over five million records, including repository cards, discussions, and commits from Models, Datasets, and Spaces, and identified over 1,500 records that explicitly mention the Act. Two coders manually labeled these records using a primarily closed coding approach, and we analyzed the resulting categories per source. We find that explicit references to the AI Act remain rare across all repository types. Repository cards mainly contain claims of compliance and responsibility, like ensuring compliance or assigning responsibility to downstream users, and some claim that the Act does not apply because the model is not a general-purpose AI model. Discussions are used to report alleged violations of the Act, while commits mainly update artifacts that support compliance. Overall, compliance statements focus on who is responsible rather than how compliance is achieved. We believe that this calls for direct platform support, e.g., automated checks and documentation templates, as well as clearer guidance on the scope of the AI Act.

StreamEgo: Streaming Exocentric-to-Egocentric Video Generation

📅 2026-11-19 14:18
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Exocentric-to-egocentric (Exo2Ego) video generation transforms third-person recordings into first-person viewpoints, unlocking immersive AR/VR experiences and scalable demonstration synthesis for embodied robotics. Extending it to a streaming setting is expected to further enable live applications. However, existing methods suffer from degraded manipulation regions, overfitting and confinement to fixed-length clips with prohibitive latency. To address these limitations, we present StreamEgo, a real-time framework for unbounded, generalizable Exo2Ego video generation. To resolve intricate manipulation dynamics while supporting the streaming pipeline, a streaming dual-branch prior provides pixel-aligned guidance for translating appearance and skeletal kinematics. We also introduce a region-aware loss curriculum to improve generalization and stabilize multi-objective diffusion training. Furthermore, we distill the model into a chunk-wise autoregressive model, augmented with a region-aware corruption mechanism and a first-chunk curriculum, enabling high-quality, generalizable real-time streaming Exo2Ego generation at 24.3 FPS on a single H100 GPU.

GateTrace: Where Do Crowd Crossings Go Missing Under Occlusion?

📅 2026-11-19 16:42
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

At large events, pedestrian tracking supports entrance and passageway counts, but totals hide which crossings were missed. In a stadium study, the system recorded about four in five clearly visible crossings but fewer than one in three mostly obscured crossings. The videos lacked trajectories annotated across frames, so we turned to MOT20, which provides pedestrian trajectories and visibility. We introduce GateTrace to locate missed crossings and assess changes to counting rules. It derives reference crossings from annotations under fixed rules, matches reports by person, direction, time and position, and traces misses through saved boxes and counting decisions. With a shared detector, ByteTrack, OC-SORT and BoT-SORT recover 38.9-46.7% of low-visibility reference crossings on development sequences, versus 98.1-99.0% at high visibility. The gap recurs on two further sequences under a protocol fixed in advance. Published outputs of MOT20-tuned BASE recover 63-78% at low visibility across both sequence groups. Losses stop at different stages across systems. Across four systems and both groups, 47-79% of low-visibility misses have no nearby output linked to the person or no possible crossing in the saved tracks. Removing later checks from BASE on development sequences leaves most misses unresolved and lowers precision against the fixed reference list from about 96% to 77%. Misses also remain among sampled reference crossings that two human annotators supported after checking the videos. GateTrace shows where crossings are lost and measures the recovery and precision costs of changing later checks. We will release the code and per-crossing records.

Context as a Resource

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Managing concurrent tasks across separate agent sessions quickly exhausts human attention, motivating always-on personal agents behind a single continuous interface. Yet when every context shares identical frozen backbone weights, funneling interleaved dialogues and external events into one linear session induces cross-topic constraint bleed and head-of-line stalling. Conversely, router-ticket hierarchies incur lossy prose handoffs, heavy routing overhead, and blind merges - collapsing true-conflict repository merges to a 33.3% three-run mean despite high isolated branch passes. Rather than scheduling anthropomorphic roles, we introduce ClonAct, a routerless architecture that virtualizes context blocks directly through a pluggable single-pass non-generative Context MMU, in-place Context Cells, a footprint-aware Synaptic Bus with an isolated trial merge gate, and a wave-flushed Shared Trunk. Across three programmatic multi-stream benchmarks and three model families, our portable trial merge gate lifts true-conflict merges to 63.3%, while ClonAct's routerless virtualization attains this ceiling with 45.9% fewer prompt tokens and prevents compact-model router collapse. Under continuous surge streams, ClonAct matches top completion (95.0%) with the lowest median wait and zero timed-out status checks.

ChainAgent: Automated Chainable Vulnerability Discovery in PHP Web Applications

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

High-impact attacks on PHP web applications often chain limited-capability vulnerabilities with other flaws or intended behaviors. Discovering these chains requires identifying flaws and the dependencies enabling capability composition. Existing vulnerability discovery techniques analyze flaws in isolation, while attack planning assumes known components. This separation makes it difficult to discover previously unknown components whose security relevance becomes apparent only through their potential roles in an exploit chain. We present ChainAgent, a goal-directed LLM-agent framework that jointly discovers exploit-chain components and their capability dependencies in source code. Guided by an empirical study of 386 real-world web exploit chains, ChainAgent represents vulnerabilities and functional gadgets--intended program behaviors that can be repurposed in attacks--through preconditions and capabilities over security-relevant objects. Instead of discovering vulnerabilities first and constructing attack paths afterward, ChainAgent uses an evolving exploit graph to guide source-code inspection. It searches backward from unmet preconditions for capability producers and forward from discovered capabilities for functional gadgets that consume or amplify them. Object-centered chaining knowledge from our empirical study directs searches toward promising vulnerability classes, functional operations, and object accesses. We evaluate ChainAgent against Codex, a general-purpose coding agent, on 15 PHP applications across three shared LLM backends. ChainAgent achieves 42.02%--44.63% recall for goal-reaching components and recovers up to 4.4 times as many correct goal-reaching dependency edges as Codex. An additional evaluation of 30 real-world PHP applications identifies 20 vulnerabilities and 13 exploit chains, nine of which achieve remote code execution (RCE).

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Materials science involves highly heterogeneous scientific information, including textual knowledge, molecular and crystal structures, experimental characterization data, microscopy images, and structure-property relationships. Developing a general-purpose foundation model capable of understanding and reasoning over these diverse materials representations remains challenging. We construct a large-scale multimodal training corpus covering materials knowledge, scientific reasoning, quantitative problem solving, structural understanding, and multimodal scientific interpretation. To improve domain adaptation, we develop a unified data construction pipeline combining teacher-guided reasoning distillation, scientific problem synthesis, consistency-based filtering, and retrieval-based multimodal data construction. MatVerse provides a unified language-and-vision interface for materials science and serves as a foundation for future AI-assisted materials research workflows.

When Foundation Models Disagree: Representational Heterogeneity for Calibrated Uncertainty in Whole-Slide Image Classification

📅 2026-11-19 14:15
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Pathology foundation models supply strong representations for whole-slide image classification, yet the confidence attached to their predictions remains hard to trust. Prevailing uncertainty estimators act within a single feature space and thereby overlook the uncertainty that the choice of foundation-model representation itself induces. This work introduces CALIFUSE, a calibration-oriented framework that fuses several pathology foundation models for the specific purpose of uncertainty estimation. Departing from accuracy-driven fusion, CALIFUSE reads representational heterogeneity across encoders as an explicit confidence cue. Slide-level decision consensus is coupled with chunk-level evidence agreement quantified through Jensen-Shannon divergence, so that both prediction stability and fine-grained evidence consistency inform the estimate. Foundation-model encoders stay frozen. A task-specific nnMIL classifier is still trained for each FM; CALIFUSE then estimates confidence at inference without training an extra fusion module. Evaluated on seven WSI classification tasks under several foundation-model combinations, the framework sharpens calibration, misclassification detection, and selective prediction relative to both the strongest single-encoder baseline and naive ensemble averaging, all while preserving competitive balanced accuracy.

Reject and Report: Forgery-Evident Undetectable Watermarks for Generative Image Models

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Provenance schemes such as those based on content watermarks let images from a generative model be traced back to it. A practical watermarking scheme must be accurate at a low false-positive rate (FPR), robust under content-preserving processing, and undetectable. In addition, it must resist forgery; otherwise, even from a single watermarked image and a surrogate model, an adversary can imprint the watermark onto an arbitrary image or regenerate new content that carries it, falsely attributing harmful content to a model or making authentic photographs appear AI-generated. We argue that a watermarking scheme should not only reject forgeries but also report them: a forgery rejected as unwatermarked leaves no trace of the attempt. State-of-the-art forgery-resilient undetectable watermarking schemes, such as SemBind, fall short on both counts. External anomaly detectors that do report forgeries either miss most of them or falsely flag genuine images after common processing. We propose Tripwire-WM, the first forgery-evident framework for undetectable watermarks. It commits the semantics of the generated image to the magnitudes of the initial latent noise, which the sign-based watermarks leave unused, through a distribution-preserving embedding; a forged image carries a valid watermark but with a commitment that does not match its content, so the detector both rejects it and reports it as forged. Built on the pseudorandom code (PRC) watermark and Gaussian Shading++, Tripwire-WM provably preserves their undetectability. On MS-COCO, with the owner's own model as the surrogate, it accepts no imprinting forgery, compared with up to 55.0% for SemBind, and reports all of them as forged; it also accepts at most 1.8% of reprompting forgeries, against up to 46.2% for SemBind. Meanwhile, it accepts at least 99% of genuine watermarked images at 10^{-6} FPR and matches the robustness of the base schemes.

MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g.~MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

OffBeat: Multicoset Sampling for 4D Capture Beyond the Camera Frame Rate

📅 2026-11-19 16:20
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

For 4D reconstruction, a synchronized multi-camera rig records all views at the same timestamps and misses the motion between them. Staggered triggers spread the same frames over more timestamps. However, when the cameras along each row fire one after another, as in 4DSloMo's schedule on the N3DV rig, the row acts as one moving camera that confounds depth with motion. We introduce OffBeat, which models a staggered rig as a spatially distributed multicoset sampler: shifted copies of one sampling grid. From the calibration alone, OffBeat derives camera groups that fire together and their firing order. Since one group records each timestamp, a short-lived primitive can fit an error its cameras share, such as a color offset, and flicker in novel views. We show that a primitive's opacity over time weights all groups equally, for every number of groups, only if it lasts at least one camera frame interval. On Spacetime Gaussians, a minimum lifetime approximates this bound, and a closed-form smoothed box of that length satisfies it exactly, with less flicker than a Gaussian in its place at nearly equal PSNR. In simulated staggered capture on N3DV, OffBeat outperforms the full 4DSloMo pipeline with video diffusion on every test frame, by 3.04 dB in mean dynamic-region PSNR. Between the first and last synchronized frame, it outperforms the best synchronized method of each scene, with or without frame interpolation, by 1.98 dB in mean dynamic-region PSNR, while synchronized capture is better at its own frames. On PanopticSports, in this frame range, OffBeat is comparable in PSNR to synchronized capture with interpolation. On every N3DV scene, replacing 4DSloMo's schedule with ours raises the dynamic-region PSNR of Spacetime Gaussians, 4DSloMo without diffusion, and our reconstruction. Multi-camera rigs could follow faster motion at the same frame count by changing only when each camera fires.

MultiBinder: Preventing Multi-Subject Identity Leakage in Personalized Video Generation

📅 2026-11-19 14:18
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Personalized video generation faces a significant scalability challenge: as the number of custom subjects increases, existing methods suffer from severe identity leakage and semantic entanglement, resulting in videos with blended attributes and incoherent narratives. We identify that this stems from a lack of explicit binding between textual entity phrases and their corresponding visual patterns prior to fusion in diffusion models. To address this, we propose MultiBinder, a novel framework built on a `bind-then-narrate'' paradigm. Our approach introduces a two-stage conditioning process before video diffusion. First, Cross-modal Entity Binding devises an entity-aware Q-Former to extract identity-centric features from each subject's reference image and tightly align them with its unique entity phrase, yielding disentangled identity tokens.'' Second, a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) acts as a narrative director'' in the Narrative-Aware Subject Arrangement stage, capturing the action of each subject from the text prompt and synthesizes contextualized subject features that encapsulate `who is doing what'' and preserve role-specific distinctions. These features are then injected into a video diffusion model via dedicated conditioning pathways, ensuring precise attribute allocation. Extensive experiments on both general and newly curated multi-subject benchmarks show that MultiBinder significantly outperforms existing methods in multi-subject identity fidelity.

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Unplanned 30-day readmissions among patients with diabetes mellitus impose a substantial clinical and economic burden, yet most predictive models use only structured electronic health record (EHR) data and ignore free-text clinical notes, which often contain code-mixed Hindi-English (Hinglish) phrasing and negation that general-purpose language models parse unreliably. 📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Image color enhancement aims to improve visual appeal, but previous methods often learn to reproduce ground-truth expert targets. These targets reflect the preferences of a few experts and cover a limited range of aesthetic choices. Matching them does not ensure stronger aesthetic quality across different tastes and scenes. By contrast, large generative models learn rich visual knowledge from large image collections and often produce vivid, appealing image edits. However, direct generation can change the source image's semantic structure along with its colors. In this paper, we introduce AesDistill, a framework for distilling generative aesthetics into image color enhancement. We use 4 leading open-source large generative models to produce 64,644 edited images as teacher targets at a cost of about 3,360 H100 GPU-hours, forming the AesDistill Dataset. Across both datasets, AesDistill Student outperforms all 20 baseline methods and ranks directly behind the 4 teachers. These results demonstrate that distilling aesthetics from generative models enables a compact student to outperform methods trained on expert targets.

Tracking Objects Through Assembly and Decomposition

📅 2026-11-19 15:38
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Tracking objects through transformations is essential for understanding real-world dynamics. Recent work has extended traditional object tracking to detect state changes. However, existing methods require an initial object prompt and detect only its decompositions, missing assembly events, which begin with multiple objects rather than one. We therefore introduce a prompt-free formulation of this task, where the goal is to recover all object tracks and detect both decomposition and assembly events given only a video. Our key insight is that assembly is decomposition in reverse. Building on this insight, we present Atlas, a zero-shot system that constructs and jointly reasons over forward and reverse spatiotemporal partitions to recover object tracks and their transformations. 📅 2026-11-19 14:02
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

The Unitree Go1 is a widely used platform for legged robotics research, yet collecting proprioceptive data from it is unnecessarily difficult: the Unitree SDK is sparsely documented, and ROS1 (Robot Operating System), the Go1's native middleware, is fully deprecated. We present an open-source graphical utility that enables researchers to reliably record proprioceptive data from the Go1, including joint states, IMU (Inertial Measurement Unit) readings, foot force sensor data, and gyroscope measurements, without requiring ROS expertise. The utility is designed to lower the barrier to collecting Go1 proprioceptive data without ROS.

Fixing the Last Mile: Failure-Aware Image Restoration with Region-Specific Guidance

📅 2026-11-19 17:21
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Recent advances in image restoration have greatly improved global image fidelity, producing visually plausible results across most regions of an image. Nevertheless, even high-quality restorations often exhibit localized failures, including artifacts in semantically important regions such as faces and text, structural inconsistencies, and texture mismatches. Although these errors typically affect only a small portion of the image, they often dominate the overall perceptual quality of the result. In this work, we propose Failure-Aware Image Restoration (FAIR) to address the "last-mile" problem in image restoration. Rather than applying one uniform generative prior over the whole image, we condition a single-pass restoration model on both where local failures are likely to occur and what corrective guidance each region requires. This region-bound conditioning enables fine-grained and controllable restoration that improves semantic accuracy and stylistic consistency in critical regions while maintaining faithful, high-quality restoration elsewhere. Our method complements existing restoration pipelines by shifting the emphasis from global fidelity to local perceptual acceptability. Extensive experiments show that the proposed approach resolves common failure cases and improves perceptual quality, bridging the gap between technical accuracy and practical usability.

Inject Where It Matters: Objective-Dependent Feature Control in Rectified-Flow Image Editing

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Many prompt-driven FLUX editing methods rely on injecting source features into target-conditioned generation, creating an inherent trade-off between source preservation and the desired edit. We address this problem by controlling where source features are injected and which transformer blocks are used. First, we introduce adaptive masks that spatially gate feature injection using differences between source- and target-conditioned block outputs at matched inversion states. Second, we use REINFORCE to select Top-K injection blocks under different preservation-editability objectives. The selected blocks are fixed at inference and require no online search. On PIE-Bench with FLUX.1-dev, spatial masking controls the alignment-preservation trade-off. A Tensor-Algebraic No-Go Theorem for High-Dimensional Photonic Greenberger--Horne--Zeilinger States Discovered with AlphaProof Nexus

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Photonic GHZ states generated with probabilistic pair sources can be described by edge-coloured, complex-weighted graphs. In this representation, conditioning on an event with one photon in every detector turns the output amplitudes into coherent sums over perfect matchings, and generating an \(N\)-photon \(D\)-dimensional GHZ state is equivalent to constructing a monochromatic quantum graph. Here, we prove that no complex-weighted monochromatic quantum graph exists for any even \(N\ge4\) when \(D\ge N\). The proof, discovered through AI-assisted formal proof search in Lean using AlphaProof Nexus, constructs a weighted sum \(\mathcal F\) of amplitudes whose coefficients factor vertex by vertex. Summing over the colours at one fixed vertex reduces the problem to an \((N-1)\times N\) matrix of effective edge weights. For the graph amplitudes \(ω\) and GHZ target \(Δ\), the row--column imbalance yields \(\mathcal F(ω)=0\) but \(\mathcal F(Δ)\neq0\), contradicting the required equality \(ω=Δ\). Finally, we give a physical interpretation of the AI-discovered mathematical proof.

Multi-View Transformer with Geometry-Guided Multi-Modal ControlNet for 3D Virtual Try-On

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Editing a 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) capture of a person so that they wear a new garment requires every rendered view to agree on the same garment details. Existing 3D virtual try-on (VTON) pipelines, however, edit training views largely independently and fuse them with hand-designed heuristics such as manually chosen reference views and fixed attention-blending weights. The resulting inconsistencies are baked into the 3D representation and surface as texture drift, blurred prints, and implausible geometry in novel views. We present GS-VTON-MVT, a three-stage framework that combines implicit and explicit 3D reasoning for consistent 3DGS try-on. (i) A multi-view inpainting transformer jointly edits a set of training views with full cross-view self-attention, so that cross-view correspondence is learned rather than imposed. (ii) A geometry-guided multi-modal ControlNet conditions the diffusion editor on the garment image together with depth and surface normals rendered from the current 3DGS state, supplying explicit structure where visual evidence is weakest. (iii) A pose-adaptive persona-aware attention replaces the fixed blending weight between current-view and reference-view features with weights derived from camera-pose proximity. On the 3D-VTONBench benchmark, GS-VTON-MVT improves FID, KID, LPIPS-based cross-view consistency, and user preference over general-purpose 3D editors and prior 3D try-on methods.

C-LiTE: A Lightweight Foundation Model for Cellular feature learning in Histopathology

📅 2026-11-19 16:03
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Characterising individual cells is fundamental to computational pathology, as cellular morphology informs tasks such as tumour grading, mitotic assessment and immune profiling. However, most pathology foundation models are trained on tissue patches and capture cellular features only implicitly, while existing cell-level models are either restricted to specific cell populations or rely on computationally expensive architectures with hundreds of millions of parameters. Applying ViTs directly to small cellular crops also presents an architectural challenge: upsampling increases computational cost without adding morphological information and may introduce interpolation artefacts. To address these limitations, we introduce C-LiTE, a lightweight foundation model for cellular representation learning in H&E-stained histopathology, with only 22 million parameters. C-LiTE employs a native-resolution tokenisation strategy tailored to cellular inputs, processing 64 x 64 px cell-centred crops using a 4 x 4 patch embedding to produce a 16 x 16 token grid without image upsampling. To transfer visual knowledge from large-scale pre-training, we adapt the patch embedding weights of a pre-trained ViT-5-S using a pseudoinverse-based transformation, enabling weight initialisation at the finer patch resolution. We further assemble a pre-training dataset of 60 million cell-centred crops from TCGA, balanced across 19 cancer types and 16 tissue types. To our knowledge, this represents the largest public dataset for self-supervised pre-training on H&E images at the cellular level. We evaluate C-LiTE on seven public datasets spanning mitotic figure recognition, nucleus and cell-type classification, and immune cell phenotyping. Under frozen-feature evaluation, C-LiTE achieves a mean macro-F1 of 0.670, approaching CytoFormer (0.682) with approximately 30x fewer parameters.

C-LiTE: A Lightweight Foundation Model for Cellular feature learning in Histopathology

📅 2026-11-20 13:40

From AI Surrogate to Engineering Tool: Toward Production Use of Fiber-Orientation Prediction in Crash CAE

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Crash simulations of glass-fiber-reinforced plastic parts are highly sensitive to the fiber-orientation field supplied to the material model. In conventional workflows, that field comes from resin-flow analysis, which requires molding-specialist support and takes about 25 hours of work per part. We previously proposed a surrogate model that addresses this bottleneck by predicting the orientation field in about 3 minutes. This paper reports the implementation, validation, and operational design that turned the model into an engineering tool for Honda design workflows. We present staged validation from 2D plates to production parts, evaluation against a predefined engineering angular-error criterion, and the expert review that approved design use in March 2025; the system has since been transferred to the crash-safety group and is in initial, limited use in directed fracture-prediction scenarios. The developed system combines injection-gate setting support for cases in which gates are unknown (90% gate-count accuracy, mean position error 11% of flow length), voxelization, surrogate inference, automatic mapping to the crash CAE model, and report generation in a single GUI. The complete orientation-preparation workflow takes about 0.1 hours, a 99.6% reduction from the conventional 25-hour flow-analysis route, and can be run directly by crash CAE engineers without molding-specialist support. As evidence of practical impact, component-level crash analysis using the predicted orientation field reduced the absolute peak-force error from 19.0% with an isotropic baseline to 11.4%, while confining predicted fracture to the test-observed region. The deployment lesson is that a surrogate model alone is not enough for practical adoption: gate-setting support, automatic data conversion, and shareable reporting are what make the model usable as a design tool.

Verifying Before Enhancing: Target Evidence Chain Verification for Infrared Small Target Detection

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Infrared small target detection is essential for early warning, surveillance, remote sensing, and space-based target perception. However, infrared small targets are typically tiny, weakly textured, and boundary-blurred, making them difficult to distinguish from complex backgrounds. Moreover, target-like background structures can produce salient responses across multiple scales, making response strength alone insufficient for reliable target discrimination. To address this challenge, we propose VBE-UNet, a Target Evidence Chain Verification Network that assesses candidate reliability beyond response magnitude through complementary evidence cues. Specifically, counterfactual background explanation models local target evidence, while joint evidence verification integrates cross-scale support and perturbation-based contribution verification to guide feature recomposition. The resulting reliability estimates guide selective feature refinement, promoting target-relevant responses while reducing unsupported background activations. Extensive experiments on IRSTD-1K, NUDT-SIRST, and NUAA-SIRST demonstrate that VBE-UNet achieves superior detection accuracy and lower false-alarm rates compared with state-of-the-art methods. Ablation studies and evidence reliability analysis further validate the effectiveness of the proposed verification framework.

Whose Risks Count? What 1,901 AI Incidents Reveal About Corporate Disclosures

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

AI systems can harm people, yet the AI actors that provide or deploy them are rarely obliged to disclose these risks. US securities regulation is an exception. Companies registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) must disclose their material risks, including those of their AI systems, in filings that carry liability for misstatement. Materiality, however, is defined by what a reasonable investor would consider important, so harms to third parties enter disclosure only insofar as they become financial risks for the company. We test whether AI harms do so by matching 9,001 AI incidents to 18,229 SEC filings of 328 AI actors by risk type. Of the 1,901 incidents with filings before and after them, half belong to a risk type that the AI actor never disclosed, and this share varies with the kind of harm. Filings disclose the risk types of 67\% of system failures but only 2\% of social system harms, and AI actors themselves report fewer than 2\% of incidents. Securities disclosure thus records the risks that AI actors face rather than the harms that people subject to AI bear. Public authorities that hold AI actors accountable for such harms could make them financially material. We release DISC, the Disclosure--Incident Securities Corpus, to support this work.

Prism: Hierarchical Text Detection with Minimalist Design

📅 2026-11-19 15:27
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Hierarchical text detection aims to localize words, text lines, and paragraphs while parsing their structural relationships. It provides essential structural cues for visual text understanding. To maximize accuracy, existing methods typically rely on complex multi-scale feature fusion necks and heavy-weight heads, resulting in bloated architectures and low inference efficiency. In this paper, we argue that a sufficiently pre-trained backbone can effectively replace these sophisticated external modules, while simple geometric inclusion suffices for hierarchical text structural parsing. Thus, we propose Prism, a minimalist framework for hierarchical text detection. Prism performs multi-granularity detection by attaching parallel lightweight heads to a single-scale backbone feature map, covering words, text lines, and paragraphs, and reconstructs the structural hierarchy via an efficient geometric inclusion algorithm. In addition, we systematically compare representative lightweight head archetypes and show that pixel-level regression offers the best accuracy-efficiency trade-off in this setting. Experiments on multiple public benchmarks show that our significantly simplified architecture achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance with remarkable efficiency. Specifically, it runs $1.7\times$ faster than LRANet on CTW1500 while achieving higher accuracy, and surpasses the current SOTA method, OmniParser V2, by over 6 PQ points on the hierarchical HierText dataset. Code will be released soon.

Backbone Matters: Benchmarking Encoder-Decoder Networks for Semantic Segmentation of Driving Scenes on BDD100K

📅 2026-11-19 14:39
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Semantic segmentation gives an autonomous vehicle a dense, pixel-level description of its surroundings and is a core part of scene understanding. Most segmentation networks consist of an encoder (the backbone) and a decoder, yet the backbone is often chosen without a systematic comparison. This work studies this choice on the BDD100K driving dataset. Four widely used decoders (U-Net, FPN, LinkNet and PSPNet) are combined with 13 backbones from six families (ResNet, VGG, DenseNet, Inception, MobileNet and EfficientNet), and all 52 models are trained with one common protocol. The best model, an FPN decoder with an EfficientNet-B4 backbone, reaches 62.6% mean intersection over union (mIoU), 89.8% accuracy and a loss of 0.092. Only two of the 52 models, both FPN with EfficientNet, exceed the 56.9% mIoU reported with the BDD100K benchmark. The effect of the backbone depends strongly on the decoder: the same EfficientNet-B4 gives 42.6% mIoU with PSPNet but 62.6% with FPN, and the gap between the best and the worst backbone ranges from 12.0 points (U-Net) to 42.5 points (FPN). The results and the scope of this study are discussed.

Publicly available geospatial data provide increasing opportuni- ties for data-driven maritime analytics and predictive modelling.
