Overall, the study demonstrates how reproducible open-source infrastructure can provide a practi- cal foundation for developing more transparent and trustworthy geospatial AI workflows.

An Explainable and Scalable Hierarchical Tensor Framework for Integrating Multi-Omics and Digital Pathomics

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Background: Precision oncology requires integration of heterogeneous molecular and pathology data, yet many fusion models offer limited biologically grounded interpretability and struggle with missing modalities. Methods: We present Hierarchical Interpretable Tensor Fusion (HIT-Fuse), integrating six modalities: somatic copy number variation, DNA methylation, RNA sequencing, microRNA expression, RPPA protein abundance, and vision transformer-derived pathomics across four TCGA cohorts (glioma, lung adenocarcinoma, breast, and kidney cancer). Modality-specific autoencoders compress high-dimensional inputs, followed by two-stage CP tensor decomposition. Missing latent modalities are reconstructed using clinical covariates and observed omics to expand cohorts and retrain downstream models. A mathematical traceback mechanism enables global and local xAI attributions. Results: HIT-Fuse identifies distinct latent subgroups with significant survival stratification in glioma, breast, and kidney cohorts, both with complete and reconstructed modalities. Cohort expansion yields cohort-specific gains, including a 56.4% relative increase in weighted F1 for glioma. Biological validation against DepMap CRISPR screens, miRTarBase and RPPA annotations supports decoded drivers across all four cohorts. Essential gene rates range from 6.2% in glioma to 7.6% in lung cancer; example drivers include ANAPC11, SNRPD1, PLK1 and RPL14. Of decoded miRNAs, 71-92% have miRTarBase regulatory support, while 77-84% of selected genes are covered in DepMap. Conclusions: HIT-Fuse combines hierarchical tensor fusion, missing-modality reconstruction and traceable attributions to support interpretable multi-omics and digital pathology-based cancer prognosis across heterogeneous cohorts.

Text-Aware, Vision-Blind: How Multimodal Image Editing Systems Handle Unsafe Inputs

📅 2026-11-19 18:36
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Multimodal image generation systems have made rapid progress and increasingly emphasize content safety. However, their safety mechanisms tend to rely on a single modality and lack joint consideration of both visual content and text prompts, particularly in instruction-guided image editing, where the uploaded image may itself carry risk. We thus introduce EditSafetyBench, a benchmark that measures system-level safety awareness of advanced image editing systems, containing 5,940 image-instruction pairs, covering safe and unsafe images across 11 risk categories and three edit intents. We then probe the benchmark dataset on four systems, including Gemini, GPT, Qwen API, and Qwen Local. Based on our evaluation, we observe a systematic text-vision safety mismatch: Gemini and GPT refuse 87.1\% and 81.6\% cases containing unsafe-intent editing prompts, while only refusing 24.2\% and 22.4\% cases with both unsafe images and neutral instructions. At the category level, most risks are considered inconsistently by different systems, e.g., GPT allows 70.4\% editing attempts of unsafe political content while Gemini only allows 34.8\%. Notably, sexual content shows the strongest visual sensitivity, likely benefiting from mature NSFW identification works. Our findings highlight the need for safety guardrails that jointly reason over both visual and textual modalities in image editing systems.

Beyond Restoration: Content-Degradation Factor Unfolding for Real-World Concealed Object Segmentation

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Reliable visual perception under degradation requires distinguishing task-relevant content from degradation-induced variations, especially in concealed object segmentation (COS), where foreground cues are inherently weak. The central challenge is not merely improving visibility, but identifying reliable content evidence amid degradation-induced variations. RGB-space segmentation often entangles content and degradation cues, while restoration-based methods primarily focus on image recovery and task interaction. This motivates explicit factorization to distinguish content evidence from degradation effects and assess its reliability. Retinex provides a spatially aligned basis for this separation but remains illumination-centric. We therefore extend it to content-degradation formation with task-asymmetric factor roles. The Factor Unfolding Network (FUN) uses the content factor R as the sole source of direct semantic evidence for segmentation, while the degradation factor L captures degradation-related variations and generates a spatial reliability map to calibrate this evidence. A unified energy couples observation formation with reliability-weighted foreground-background separation, and its alternating updates yield a coarse-to-fine network without clean-image supervision. In this way, FUN factorizes degradation for perception rather than removing it. Extensive experiments across clean and degraded conditions and diverse segmentation tasks demonstrate robust performance and generalization. Code will be released.

Frontier models need only one text to unmask its human or AI author

📅 2026-11-19 14:56
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Most strikingly, Fable 5.2 correctly names the author of 39% of public peer reviews published in Oct-Nov 2026 (78% on a subset of reviews from Nature), and identifies the username behind 69% of comments from active Redditors (with >500 posts over the last year). Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.

DAWN: Robust Neural Blind Deconvolution via Alternating Wiener Guidance

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Blind image deconvolution (BID) is an ill-posed inverse problem that aims to jointly recover a sharp image and blur kernel from a single degraded observation. Under nontrivial and high noise, which is common in photography and scientific imaging such as microscopy, BID remains particularly challenging, as existing methods suffer from noise overfitting and inaccurate kernel estimation. Existing training-data-free learning methods for BID, such as those based on the deep image prior (DIP), often utilize external guidance by including model-based estimates as additional loss terms, which underexploits the information these estimates carry especially under nontrivial noise. To address these challenges, we propose DAWN, a training-data-free DIP framework that alternates between injecting model-based estimates directly into the model input and refining this imperfect estimate with the network's own prior. To provide more reliable kernel estimates for these injections, we apply autoencoding regularization to both the image and kernel networks. On natural and scientific images under varying levels of Gaussian and Poisson noise, DAWN outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both image and kernel quality (achieving up to +4.5 dB over the next best baseline on the Levin dataset at $σ= 0.1$). Without any training images, DAWN remains highly competitive with a state-of-the-art diffusion model on FFHQ, despite the latter being trained on a massive number of in-distribution images.

GaussTrans: A Gaussian Interface Framework for Multi-Layer Refractive Transport

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

We investigate surface modeling of multi-transparent interfaces to achieve accurate light transmission. The interplay of errors in geometric structure, light paths, and observation results during the reconstruction of transparent media with a high number of layers makes this a challenging problem. Some existing techniques address the reconstruction of transparent scenes by reducing the number of layers or simplifying light paths; however, this limits their direct applicability to high-order transparent scenes. We propose GaussTrans, a compact framework for light transport in high-order transparent scenes, consisting of three components: Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces, Transport-Coupled Refinement, and Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection. Gaussian-Field Hybrid Surfaces use a hybrid approach combining Gauss surfaces and fields to reconstruct different interfaces. Transport-Coupled Refinement reduces coupling errors by evaluating the entire light path. Primitive-Guided Implicit Intersection combines primitive support regions to reduce errors in the intersection of interfaces and rays. In experiments on datasets such as RefRef, GaussTrans achieves significantly higher accuracy with relatively low spatial storage requirements.

Multi-institutional grading of Barrett's esophagus dysplasia without stain normalization using a pathology foundation model and multiple instance learning

📅 2026-11-19 18:27
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Barrett's esophagus dysplasia grading suffers substantial inter-observer variability, particularly for low-grade dysplasia, complicating clinical decision-making. We conducted a retrospective multi-institutional diagnostic accuracy study of a foundation model-based deep learning system for dysplasia grading, using 969 H\&E-stained whole slide images from four institutions. Patch-level classifiers trained on expert annotations identified diagnostically relevant regions, followed by attribute-aware multiple instance learning for slide-level classification as non-dysplastic Barrett's esophagus (NDBE), low-grade dysplasia, or high-grade dysplasia. On a held-out test set (n = 130), macro-averaged AUC was 0.96 (95\% CI 0.93--0.98). For binary dysplasia detection, sensitivity was 98\% (80/82) and specificity 81\% (39/48), with a negative likelihood ratio of 0.030 and quadratic-weighted \kap{} of 0.83 against expert consensus. No high-grade dysplasia case was misclassified as NDBE. Performance was consistent across institutions without stain normalization, indicating that stain-robust foundation-model representations may remove the site-specific preprocessing that has constrained prior systems to institution-specific deployment.

A Wolf in Sheep's Pixels: Visual Camouflage Attacks via HDR Gain Maps

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

An image can pass automated safety checks while showing harmful content to its human viewer. We introduce DRJanus, a visual camouflage attack that exploits the gap between standard dynamic range (SDR) processing and high dynamic range (HDR) display. Automated systems often read only an image's SDR base, whereas HDR viewers reconstruct its appearance using an auxiliary gain map. DRJanus inverts this reconstruction to embed two independently chosen color images in a single JPEG, without querying or optimizing against the target model. It preserves the chosen SDR base exactly and reconstructs HDR targets at an average PSNR of 43.03 dB across 400 images. On a benchmark of 100 sexual-content images, DRJanus evades detection by all six tested commercial moderation services and nine vision-language models. Across five agents, each evaluated on 120 task cases per threat setting, attack success ranges from 31.67% to 100% for moderation evasion and from 95% to 100% for task manipulation. We also explore cross-view consistency checks as a mitigation. These findings expose a gap in visual trust: securing what a machine reads does not secure what a user sees. A demo is available at https://qshen0629.github.io/DRJanus/

A version of the Cloude/van Zyl decomposition for full/quad pol synthetic aperture radar data

📅 2026-11-19 14:41
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Based on the Cloude/van Zyl decomposition of reflection symmetric polarimetric SAR data we present a version for full/quad polarisation data. We demonstrate both versions on X-band F-SAR data over Vejers, Denmark. Differences between the original and our version of the decomposition occur especially for very bright man-made targets. These differences come with lower entropy for our version giving an improved focus on a more pronounced dominant scattering mechanism for these objects. Interpretation of the scattering mechanism related to the full/quad pol version of the decomposition is facilitated by means of the Cloude/Pottier $\barα$ angle.

FFGaussType: Feed-Forward Conversion of Glyph and SVG Geometry into Compact Gaussian Assets

📅 2026-11-19 17:08
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Immersive 3D content allows text and graphical elements, such as subtitles, annotations, and icons, to be placed inside a scene rather than displayed as flat overlays. Placed this way, they respect occlusion and perspective and take on user-assigned materials and lighting while preserving their original shapes. In Gaussian scenes, however, each new glyph or icon requires its own iterative optimization, which limits scalable content creation. We present FFGaussType, which converts extruded glyph and SVG outlines into Gaussian assets in a single forward pass. A point encoder maps anchors sampled on the known surface to a single feature plane, from which a decoder shared by all assets predicts neural Gaussians carrying surface normals. This separates geometric conversion from material and lighting choices, and the feature plane supports image and video coding for compact delivery. We construct a paired dataset of 27,221 glyph and SVG icon assets with anchors and calibrated normal and silhouette renderings, holding out font families for evaluation. Conversion from prepared anchors takes 9.1 ms per asset, at a fraction of the compute and memory of prior baselines. On unseen font families and test icons, FFGaussType achieves $1.62^\circ$ normal error and $0.985$ silhouette IoU, the best among the compared feed-forward converters, matching the fidelity that per-asset fitting needs thousands of steps to reach. The code and dataset will be released after publication.

Contactless Estimation of Respiratory Frequency Using 3D-CNN on Thermal Images

📅 2026-11-19 16:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Monitoring physiological parameters such as respiratory rate ($f_R$) is essential for diagnosing and managing various pathological conditions. Thermal imaging offers a promising contactless alternative to traditional methods, which often rely on partially invasive sensors or obtrusive wearable systems. However, existing approaches for $f_R$ estimation from thermal signals typically require extensive pre-processing and manual or semi-automatic region-of-interest (ROI) tracking, limiting their practical applicability. This study proposes a deep learning-based method for estimating $f_R$ directly from thermal videos, eliminating the need for complex pre-processing and ROI tracking. A 3D Convolutional Neural Network (3D-CNN) is developed to operate on raw thermal video data. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed approach achieves a validation $R^2$ score of approximately 0.61 on both pre-processed and raw thermal videos. By simplifying the workflow, this method holds promise for enhancing the feasibility of thermal imaging in real-world applications, such as remote healthcare and driver monitoring in automotive applications.

Digitization of Analogue Gauges using Computer Vision

📅 2026-11-19 15:33
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Analogue gauges remain prevalent in manufacturing industries for measuring physical quantities, yet manual monitoring by operators introduces human error and operational inefficiency. This research developed and evaluated an automated gauge reading system using computer vision and deep learning techniques. Three hybrid approaches were implemented and compared: OpenCV with Scikit-Image (traditional computer vision), OpenCV with ResNet18, and OpenCV with VGG19 (deep learning approaches). A comprehensive dataset of 1,848 pressure gauge images (0-10 BAR range) was curated through web scraping, synthetic generation, and augmentation techniques. The experimental results demonstrate that ResNet18 significantly outperforms the alternatives, achieving an accuracy of 97.3%, compared to VGG19's 64.4% and Scikit-Image's 63.8%. The study validates the effectiveness of residual learning architectures for industrial gauge digitization, providing a robust foundation for Industry 5.0 automation applications.

FirstCross: Convex Single-Photon 3D Reconstruction from Sparse Views

📅 2026-11-19 16:05
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Single-photon lidar records the arrival time of each returning photon, enabling 3D reconstruction from few views and scarce photons. However, leading neural methods such as Transientangelo require over an hour of non-convex per-scene optimization, and their accuracy degrades as photon flux decreases. We propose FirstCross, a network-free approach that instead solves a short sequence of convex problems on a voxel grid. For a solid object, the Poisson likelihood of a ray with given signal and background rates depends on the scene solely through its first crossing, the point where the ray first enters the object. Decomposing this likelihood yields two tractable terms. The first penalizes surfaces in front of detected photons and admits a tight convex closure, namely volume rendering with a running-maximum transmittance in place of NeRF's product transmittance. The second accounts for photons that no surface explains and admits a convex upper bound once the object is assumed to be thick behind each detected surface point. Compared with Transientangelo under its own real-data protocol, FirstCross achieves lower depth error across every test scene, view count, and flux level. With three views and 100 times fewer photons than at the protocol's lowest flux, our mean object depth error stays under half of Transientangelo's with all photons. Given our detected surface points, the best classical fusion has 1.3 to 1.8 times our three-view error across flux levels. On real room-scale captures with 1% of their photons, TransientNeRF has 2.8 to 7.7 times our error. FirstCross lets few-view single-photon scanners cut exposure to 1% and reconstruct an object in about a minute on one GPU.

AURA: AUdio-dRiven streaming Avatar

📅 2026-11-19 18:17
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Current audio-driven video diffusion models can synthesize realistic talking-head videos but generally require dozens of diffusion denoising steps over multi-second video chunks, making them unsuitable for streaming applications such as voice assistants and virtual avatars, where low-latency and online causal generation are required properties. AURA generates video chunk by chunk with a rolling KV cache, requiring only the audio of the current chunk to start generating, rather than a multi-second audio look-ahead like bidirectional models. It comes in two sizes: AURA 1.3B streams 576p video in real time on a single GPU (20.7 fps for a 16 Hz playback rate), making it suited to interactive avatar applications, and AURA 5B streams 720p video at 9.7 fps. Our two-stage distillation pipeline first aligns the causal student with the teacher, by training it to match the teacher's trajectories with fewer diffusion steps and a chunk-wise sliding window causal attention mask, then applies Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) with backward simulation to close the remaining quality gap. On standard talking-head benchmarks, AURA 1.3B runs 23x faster than its own bidirectional teacher and reaches a quality comparable to TalkVerse at a lower resolution, and AURA 5B matches its TalkVerse teacher at 720p while running 17x faster. To support reproducibility and further research on streaming avatar generation, we release the weights of both models along with a dataset of 30K synthetic videos and their precomputed teacher trajectories.

AURA: AUdio-dRiven streaming Avatar

📅 2026-11-20 13:40
arXivHTMLPDFKimi

Current audio-driven video diffusion models can synthesize realistic talking-head videos but generally require dozens of diffusion denoising steps over multi-second video chunks, making them unsuitable for streaming applications such as voice assistants and virtual avatars, where low-latency and online causal generation are required properties. AURA generates video chunk by chunk with a rolling KV cache, requiring only the audio of the current chunk to start generating, rather than a multi-second audio look-ahead like bidirectional models. It comes in two sizes: AURA 1.3B streams 576p video in real time on a single GPU (20.7 fps for a 16 Hz playback rate), making it suited to interactive avatar applications, and AURA 5B streams 720p video at 9.7 fps. Our two-stage distillation pipeline first aligns the causal student with the teacher, by training it to match the teacher's trajectories with fewer diffusion steps and a chunk-wise sliding window causal attention mask, then applies Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) with backward simulation to close the remaining quality gap. On standard talking-head benchmarks, AURA 1.3B runs 23x faster than its own bidirectional teacher and reaches a quality comparable to TalkVerse at a lower resolution, and AURA 5B matches its TalkVerse teacher at 720p while running 17x faster. To support reproducibility and further research on streaming avatar generation, we release the weights of both models along with a dataset of 30K synthetic videos and their precomputed teacher trajectories.
